### [1] [Many-body quantum chaos in excitonic spectra from first principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03294)
*Daniel Hernangómez-Pérez,Rafael A. Molina*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: First-principles GW-BSE calculations show that excitonic many-body Hamiltonians in van der Waals heterostructures can exhibit quantum chaos, transitioning from regular to fully chaotic dynamics via disorder-induced valley mixing, with universal Porter-Thomas statistics linking chaos to optical observables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate that realistic excitonic many-body Hamiltonians from first-principles can exhibit quantum chaos governed by random-matrix universality, and to understand the role of disorder in driving ergodic dynamics.

Method: First-principles GW-Bethe-Salpeter equation calculations on a WS₂-graphene heterostructure, with/without lattice disorder; analysis of energy-resolved spectral correlations, symmetry analysis, and many-body oscillator strength fluctuations.

Result: Pristine samples show incomplete chaos due to approximate valley symmetry; disorder-induced flat bands catalyze valley mixing, driving full chaotic (ergodic) dynamics with reduced symmetry; oscillator strengths follow universal Porter-Thomas statistics; Thouless time estimated via long-range spectral correlations.

Conclusion: Excitons serve as a highly tunable platform for probing many-body ergodicity and its spectroscopic signatures in realistic interacting 2D materials.

Abstract: We demonstrate that realistic excitonic many-body Hamiltonians obtained from first-principles GW-Bethe-Salpeter equation calculations can exhibit quantum chaos governed by random-matrix universality. Considering a prototypical van der Waals heterostructure (WS$_2$-graphene), with and without lattice disorder, we analyze their energy-resolved spectral correlations and identify a disorder-driven crossover from regular to complete chaotic dynamics. We show that while pristine samples exhibit incomplete chaos (non-ergodicity) due to an approximate valley symmetry that restricts excitonic mixing, the presence of disorder-induced electronic flat bands act as a catalyst for valley mixing to drive the system into a fully developed chaotic (ergodic) regime with reduced symmetry. Crucially, fluctuations in many-body oscillator strengths are shown to follow universal Porter-Thomas statistics, directly linking the underlying quantum chaos and experimentally accessible optical observables. Finally, by examining long-range spectral correlations, we estimate the Thouless time associated to excitonic mixing across the entire many-body bandwidth. Our results establish excitons as a highly tunable platform for probing many-body ergodicity and its spectroscopic signatures in realistic interacting 2D materials.

</details>


### [2] [Anisotropic magnetoresistance of 2D Rashba films with in-plane Zeeman field and short-range disorder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02747)
*Igor Gornyi,Alexander Khaetskii*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper shows that in a two-dimensional Rashba film with an in-plane Zeeman field and delta-correlated scalar disorder, the in-plane field does not produce anisotropic magnetoresistance (AMR) in the leading quasiclassical conductivity due to a geometric mechanism: the total area enclosed by the two Rashba-Zeeman Fermi sheets is independent of the in-plane field, leading to a scalar, field-independent Born self-energy and isotropic quasiparticle lifetime, so that point-like nonmagnetic impurities do not yield AMR in this model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether an in-plane Zeeman field combined with Rashba spin-orbit coupling can produce anisotropic magnetoresistance (AMR) in the presence of short-range scalar disorder, settling the quasiclassical problem for point-like nonmagnetic impurities.

Method: The authors study dc conductivity analytically using the Kubo formalism with a density Ward identity to analyze Born self-energy and vertex corrections, focusing on the geometric property of Fermi contour area invariance under in-plane Zeeman field.

Result: The in-plane Zeeman field does not produce AMR in leading quasiclassical conductivity; the Born self-energy remains scalar and field-independent, the quasiparticle lifetime is isotropic, and the diagonal intraband Kubo conductivity is field-independent due to the invariant two-sheet occupied area.

Conclusion: Point-like nonmagnetic impurities do not generate AMR in this Rashba-Zeeman model at the quasiclassical level; nonzero AMR requires mechanisms beyond short-range disorder, such as long-range disorder or other scattering physics.

Abstract: We study the dc conductivity of a continuum two-dimensional Rashba film with an in-plane Zeeman field and delta-correlated scalar disorder. Although the field deforms the two helicity Fermi contours and rotates the spin texture, it does not produce anisotropic magnetoresistance in the leading quasiclassical conductivity. The mechanism is geometric. A density Ward identity fixes the spin-vector part of the Born self-energy to the derivative of the total particle density with respect to the field. This derivative vanishes, because the total area enclosed by the two Rashba-Zeeman sheets is independent of the in-plane field. The Born self-energy is therefore scalar and field independent, and the quasiparticle lifetime stays isotropic. The same area invariance controls transport: once the leading impurity ladder reduces the current vertex to the parabolic velocity, the diagonal intraband Kubo conductivity collapses onto the two-sheet occupied area and is field independent as well. The result settles the short-range-disorder quasiclassical problem: point-like nonmagnetic impurities do not produce AMR in this model. A nonzero AMR requires physics beyond this quasiclassical short-range-disorder mechanism.

</details>


### [3] [Electrically tunable interfacial thermal conduction via electronic structure engineering in ${Au}$/$Bi_{1-x}$$Sb_{x}$ topological insulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02899)
*Min Young Kim,Joon Sang Kang,Jangwoo Ha,Hyungyu Jin,Sandy A. Ekahana,Pratik Saud,Chris Jozwiak,Eli Rotenberg,Aaron Bostwick,Jyoti Katoch,Joseph P. Heremans*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper experimentally demonstrates that topological interface states in metal/topological insulator junctions enable electrically reversible modulation of interfacial thermal conduction, attributed to carrier redistribution between topological and bulk band states via thermal and electrical driving mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide direct experimental evidence for the role of topological interface states in thermal conduction and to demonstrate electrically reversible modulation of heat flow at solid-state interfaces, addressing the need for active thermal management in increasingly dense, high-power devices.

Method: Measured interfacial thermal conductance of Au/Bi₈₉Sb₁₁ and Au/Bi₈₇Sb₁₃ junctions under varying temperature and bias conditions; performed control experiments using trivial semimetals and insulating interlayers; interpreted results via carrier redistribution between topological interface and bulk band states driven by Fermi-Dirac broadening (thermal) and quasi-Fermi-level shifts with WKB tunneling (electrical).

Result: Distinct temperature- and bias-dependent behavior of interfacial thermal conductance was observed, with topological specificity confirmed by control experiments. Electrically reversible modulation of thermal conduction was demonstrated.

Conclusion: Electrically tunable interfacial heat conduction via topological interface states offers a mechanically robust alternative to conventional structure-driven thermal control, positioning interface electronic structure engineering as a promising route for active thermal management in solid-state devices.

Abstract: This work provides direct experimental evidence for the role of topological interface states in thermal conduction across a metal/topological insulator junction. It also shows that this conduction can be reversibly modulated by electrical current injection, offering a new approach toward active control of heat flow at solid-state interfaces. Specifically, the interfacial thermal conductance of ${Au}$/$Bi_{89}$$Sb_{11}$ and ${Au}$/$Bi_{87}$$Sb_{13}$ junctions demonstrates distinct temperature- and bias-dependent behavior. Both responses are attributed to carrier redistribution between topological interface and bulk band states, driven thermally by Fermi-Dirac broadening and electrically by quasi-Fermi-level shifts and WKB tunneling into nearby bulk bands. Control experiments using trivial semimetals and insulating interlayers further confirm the topological specificity of the effect. Such electrically tunable interfacial heat conduction positions interface electronic structure engineering as a promising route for active thermal management. In doing so, it lays the groundwork for a mechanically robust alternative to conventional structure-driven thermal control compatible with increasingly dense, high-power solid-state devices.

</details>


### [4] [Hall Shift Current and Nonlinear Anomalous Hall Effect in Gapped Dirac Fermion Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02953)
*Toshihito Osada*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper identifies a new mechanism for the nonlinear anomalous Hall effect in tilted 2D gapped Dirac fermion systems, arising from side-jump displacement via Landau-Zener tunneling across the mass gap, which complements the conventional Berry curvature dipole mechanism and may explain observations in α-(ET)₂I₃.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explain the nonlinear Hall effect accompanied by nonlinear longitudinal transport observed in the organic Dirac fermion system α-(ET)₂I₃, which has an extremely small gap, and to identify a new interband mechanism beyond the conventional Berry curvature dipole picture.

Method: The authors theoretically derive a nonlinear anomalous Hall effect mechanism originating from side-jump displacement associated with Landau-Zener tunneling across the mass gap in tilted two-dimensional gapped Dirac fermion systems.

Result: In lightly doped tilted Dirac systems with a small gap, the interband mechanism (side-jump from Landau-Zener tunneling) contributes to the nonlinear anomalous Hall effect alongside the conventional intraband Berry curvature dipole mechanism.

Conclusion: The newly identified interband mechanism provides a plausible explanation for the nonlinear Hall effect with nonlinear longitudinal transport observed in α-(ET)₂I₃, an organic Dirac fermion system with an extremely small gap.

Abstract: We discuss a new mechanism for the nonlinear anomalous Hall effect in tilted two-dimensional gapped Dirac fermion systems. This mechanism originates from a side-jump displacement associated with Landau-Zener tunneling across the mass gap. In lightly doped tilted Dirac systems with a small gap, this interband mechanism can contribute to the nonlinear anomalous Hall effect in addition to the conventional intraband mechanism arising from the Berry curvature dipole. It provides a possible explanation for the nonlinear Hall effect accompanied by nonlinear longitudinal transport observed in the organic Dirac fermion system α-(ET)2I3 with an extremely small gap.

</details>


### [5] [Effects of Impurity Scattering on Orbital Hall Conductivity and Orbital Transport in Ru-based Alloys](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03076)
*Yumin Yang,Yanxu Chen,Wenqi Xu,Dahai Wei*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper investigates impurity scattering effects on orbital current generation and transport in Ru-based nonmagnet/ferromagnet bilayers with Cu or Ti alloying. The orbital Hall effect is dominated by an intrinsic mechanism robust against weak disorder but suppressed by stronger alloy disorder, while the orbital diffusion length remains constant (~14 nm) across impurity ranges, indicating orbital transport is not governed simply by impurity scattering. Static impurities and dynamic lattice disorder affect orbital transport through distinct microscopic channels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish the role of impurity scattering in orbital current generation and transport, which remains less understood compared to conventional spin Hall systems.

Method: SOT measurements and thickness-dependent drift-diffusion analysis on Ru-based nonmagnet/ferromagnet bilayers with Cu or Ti alloying to tune impurity scattering.

Result: The orbital Hall effect is dominated by an intrinsic mechanism that is robust against weak disorder but suppressed by stronger alloy disorder; the orbital diffusion length remains nearly unchanged at ~14 nm over the investigated impurity range.

Conclusion: Orbital transport is not governed simply by impurity scattering; static impurities and dynamic lattice disorder affect orbital transport through distinct microscopic channels, offering guidance for developing high-efficient orbitronic materials.

Abstract: The role of impurity scattering in the generation and transport of orbital current remains less established than in conventional spin Hall systems. Here we investigate Ru-based nonmagnet/ferromagnet bilayers in which the impurity scattering is tuned by Cu or Ti alloying. According to SOT measurement and thickness-dependent drift-diffusion analysis, we extract the effective orbital Hall conductivity and the orbital diffusion length. We find that the orbital Hall effect in polycrystalline Ru is dominated by intrinsic mechanism that is moderately robust against weak disorder but suppressed by stronger alloy disorder. However, the orbital diffusion length remains nearly unchanged at approximately 14 nm over the investigated impurity range. This behavior indicates that orbital transport is not governed simply by an impurity scattering. Together with previous temperature-dependent measurements, our results show that static impurities and dynamic lattice disorder affect orbital transport through distinct microscopic channels. These results provide new insight into how disorder governs orbital generation and transport, and offer experimental guidance for developing high-efficient orbitronic materials.

</details>


### [6] [Substrate-Mediated Persistent Photodoping in WSe2/hBN Field-Effect Transistors Enabled by Defect States in SiO2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03121)
*Sung-Ha Kim,Seong-Yeon Lee,Tae-Jeong Kim,Beomkyu Shin,Young-Jun Yu,Kenji Watanabe,Takashi Taniguchi,Soungmin Bae,Ki-Ju Yee*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper investigates UV-induced photodoping in WSe2 FETs and identifies SiO2 substrate defects, not hBN defects, as the dominant origin of the trap states responsible for persistent photodoping and photogating.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Photodoping determines the optoelectronic response of 2D semiconductor devices, but the origin of the responsible trap states has remained unclear.

Method: Wavelength-dependent photodoping measurements on multilayer WSe2/hBN heterostructure FETs on SiO2/p-Si substrates, with control experiments on SiO2-removed devices, combined with defect-state distribution analysis and first-principles calculations for hBN defects.

Result: Strong n-type photodoping under 405 nm illumination (orders weaker under 640 nm and for p-type); photodoping is strongly suppressed when SiO2 is removed; SiO2 defect states serve as charge reservoirs; hBN defects cannot explain the observations.

Conclusion: Substrate-related trap states (defects in SiO2) are the dominant origin of UV-induced photodoping and photogating in WSe2 FETs.

Abstract: Photodoping plays an important role in determining the optoelectronic response of two-dimensional semiconductor devices; however, the origin of the responsible trap states remains unclear. In this work, we investigate UV-induced photodoping in multilayer WSe2 field-effect transistors (FETs) based on WSe2/hBN heterostructures on SiO2/p-Si substrates. Wavelength-dependent measurements reveal pronounced n-type photodoping under 405 nm illumination, whereas the effect is orders of magnitude weaker under 640 nm excitation and for p-type photodoping. Furthermore, when the SiO2 layer is removed, both n-type and p-type photodoping are strongly suppressed, demonstrating that the oxide layer is essential for persistent photodoping. Analysis of defect-state distributions in amorphous SiO2, together with first-principles calculations for hBN defects, shows that the experimental observations cannot be explained by defects in hBN. Instead, the results indicate that defect states in the SiO2 substrate act as charge reservoirs that facilitate charge transfer and long-term carrier trapping. These findings highlight the dominant role of substrate-related trap states in UV-induced photodoping and photogating behavior in WSe2 FET devices.

</details>


### [7] [Comparative Evaluation of Encapsulation Methods for Endohedral Doping of Single-Wall Carbon Nanotubes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03151)
*Cristian M. Borja-Peña,Martin Magg,Hussein Slim,Yamaldi M. Bakary,Cedric Desroches,Denys Kurylo,Kazuhiro Yanagi,Benjamin S. Flavel,Salomé Forel,Wim Wenseleers,Sofie Cambré*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper investigates the encapsulation of tetrathiafulvalene (electron donor) and tetracyanoquinodimethane (electron acceptor) inside single-wall carbon nanotubes (SWCNTs) for stable n-type doping. Various filling methods (melt filling, solution reflux, vacuum sublimation) are compared, and density gradient ultracentrifugation is used to separate filled from empty nanotubes. A vacuum-phase cleaning method is introduced to remove external adsorbates without solvent washing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable and stable doping of SWCNTs, especially n-type, remains challenging due to environmental sensitivity and competing extrinsic effects. Encapsulation of charge transfer molecules inside SWCNTs offers a promising route to achieve stable doping while preserving the outer surface for further processing.

Method: Arc discharge SWCNTs are filled with TTF (donor) and TCNQ (acceptor) via melt filling, solution reflux, and vacuum phase sublimation. Filled vs. empty nanotubes and metallic vs. semiconducting types are separated using density gradient ultracentrifugation. Encapsulation efficiency and electronic modification are characterized by absorption spectroscopy, resonant Raman scattering, thermogravimetric analysis, and electron paramagnetic resonance. A complementary vacuum-phase method removes externally adsorbed molecules without solvent washing.

Result: Systematic comparison of filling methods; successful separation of filled from empty SWCNTs and metallic from semiconducting ones via density gradient ultracentrifugation; the vacuum-phase method effectively removes external adsorbates, yielding cleaner encapsulated systems.

Conclusion: Encapsulation of charge transfer molecules inside SWCNTs is a viable strategy for stable doping. The vacuum-phase cleaning method enables cleaner encapsulated systems, which is beneficial for applications in nanoelectronic and optoelectronic devices.

Abstract: Single wall carbon nanotubes (SWCNTs) are promising building blocks for nanoelectronic and optoelectronic devices, yet reliable and stable doping, particularly n type, remains challenging due to strong environmental sensitivity and competing extrinsic effects. Encapsulation of charge transfer molecules within the SWCNT cavity offers a promising route to stable doping while preserving the nanotubes outer surface for subsequent processing. Here, we systematically investigate the filling of arc discharge SWCNTs with the electron donor tetrathiafulvalene and electron acceptor tetracyanoquinodimethane, comparing different methods for filling, including melt filling, solution reflux, and vacuum phase sublimation. We follow the entire processing workflow from raw, unfilled powders to aqueous dispersions and employ density gradient ultracentrifugation to separate filled from empty nanotubes as well as metallic from semiconducting ones. Encapsulation efficiency and electronic modification are assessed using absorption spectroscopy, resonant Raman scattering, thermogravimetric analysis, and electron paramagnetic resonance. Finally, we introduce a complementary vacuum-phase method that removes externally adsorbed molecules without extensive solvent washing, enabling cleaner encapsulated systems.

</details>


### [8] [One-dimensional carbon nanostructures with periodic graphitic nitrogen substitution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03267)
*Nicolò Bassi,Shantanu Mishra,Zheng Zhang,Xiao-Ye Wang,Feifei Xiang,Nils Krane,Carlo A. Pignedoli,Klaus Müllen,Pascal Ruffieux,Akimitsu Narita,Roman Fasel*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper reports the on-surface synthesis of two 1D carbon nanostructures (a polymer and a graphene nanoribbon) with periodically incorporated graphitic nitrogen atoms, confirmed by bond-resolved AFM and STM. The nitrogen substitution induces spin-polarized electronic states in the gas phase (open-shell), but upon Au(111) adsorption, charge transfer leads to a closed-shell ground state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve periodic and precise incorporation of graphitic nitrogen atoms into extended carbon lattices for tuning electronic properties, which is a key requirement for applications in spintronics, catalysis, and energy storage.

Method: On-surface synthesis monitored by scanning tunneling microscopy (STM), bond-resolved atomic force microscopy (AFM) for atomic-scale structural characterization, scanning tunneling spectroscopy (STS), and density functional theory (DFT) calculations for electronic property analysis.

Result: Successfully synthesized two 1D nanostructures (polymer and graphene nanoribbon) with periodic graphitic nitrogen. AFM revealed atomic-scale visualization of nitrogen sites. Nitrogen-centered localized electronic states were found to undergo spin polarization in the gas phase (open-shell), but electron transfer to Au(111) substrate resulted in a closed-shell ground state.

Conclusion: A straightforward and generally applicable route to synthesize graphitic nitrogen-substituted carbon nanomaterials was demonstrated, with potential applications in spintronics, catalysis, and energy storage.

Abstract: Heteroatom substitution is a powerful route to tune the chemical and electronic properties of carbon nanomaterials. In particular, replacement of an sp2 hybridized carbon atom in the graphene lattice with a nitrogen atom (denoted as graphitic nitrogen) induces substantial changes in the electronic properties. These include changes in the band structure that can influence electronic transport, and magnetism. A key requirement for applications is both the periodic and precise incorporation of the heteroatoms in extended carbon lattices. Here, we report the on-surface synthesis and characterization of two one dimensional carbon nanostructures, a polymer and a graphene nanoribbon, consisting of periodically incorporated graphitic nitrogen atoms. The on-surface reactions toward formation of the nanostructures were monitored by scanning tunneling microscopy. The bond-resolved chemical structures of the reaction intermediates and products were investigated by atomic force microscopy, which enabled atomic-scale visualization of the graphitic nitrogen sites. The electronic properties of the nanostructures were studied by scanning tunneling spectroscopy and density functional theory calculations. Our analyses revealed the presence of localized nitrogen-centered electronic states. In the gas phase where the nanostructures are in a neutral charge state, these states undergo spin polarization leading to an open-shell ground state. Upon adsorption on Au(111), the nanostructures exhibit electron transfer to the surface, which resulted in a closed-shell ground state. Our results demonstrate a straightforward and generally applicable route to synthesize graphitic nitrogen-substituted carbon nanomaterials with potential applications in spintronics, catalysis and energy storage.

</details>


### [9] [Graph-Based Kirchhoff Modeling of Non-Ohmic Electron Transport in Self-Assembled Nanonecklace Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03698)
*Obed Issakah,Srivathsan Badrinarayanan,Ravi F. Saraf,Janghoon Ock*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A graph-based Kirchhoff framework simulating gold nanonecklace networks reproduces nonlinear I-V characteristics (I ∝ (V-V_T)^ζ), showing that collective Coulomb-blockade transport emerges from staggered activation of threshold junctions and voltage-driven percolation. Parameter sweeps reveal activation voltage shifts V_T, network density tunes ζ (1.9–3.1), and topology independently affects response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Experimental measurements of gold nanonecklace networks are limited to global I-V response and topology, leaving the mechanisms governing the macroscopic nonlinear transport poorly understood.

Method: A graph-based Kirchhoff framework was developed, representing the nanonecklace network as a graph (nodes = junctions, edges = conducting segments). The solver computes active nodes, conducting subgraph, nodal potentials, and edge currents at each bias, while independently varying activation-voltage statistics, network density, and structural topology.

Result: The model reproduces the non-Ohmic response I ∝ (V-V_T)^ζ. Mean activation voltage shifts V_T without changing ζ; increasing density raises ζ from ~1.9 to 3.1 and enhances current; topology controls response even at fixed density and node characteristics.

Conclusion: The model establishes a design tool for engineering collective transport in self-assembled nanonecklace devices, with qualitative agreement to experimental observations.

Abstract: Gold nanonecklace networks are promising platforms for single-electron switching, chemical sensing, and biogating devices because of their nonlinear current--voltage ($I$--$V$) characteristics arising from collective Coulomb-blockade transport. However, the mechanisms governing this macroscopic behavior remain poorly understood because experimental measurements are generally limited to the network topology and global $I$--$V$ response. To address this, we developed a graph-based Kirchhoff framework that represents a self-assembled nanonecklace network as a graph, with nodes corresponding to junctions between necklace segments and edges to the conducting segments themselves. The solver returns the active nodes, conducting subgraph, nodal potentials, and edge currents at each applied bias, while allowing the activation-voltage statistics, network density, and structural topology to be varied independently. The model reproduces the experimentally observed non-Ohmic response, $I \propto (V-V_T)^ζ$, and shows that this behavior emerges from the collective, staggered activation of threshold junctions and voltage-driven percolation of the conducting subgraph. Independent parameter sweeps reveal that the mean activation voltage shifts the threshold $V_T$ while leaving $ζ$ nearly unchanged, increasing network density raises $ζ$ from approximately 1.9 to 3.1 and enhances current, and topology controls the response even at fixed density and node characteristics. These trends agree qualitatively with experimental observations and establish the model as a design tool for engineering collective transport in self-assembled nanonecklace devices.

</details>


### [10] [Emergent $\mathbb{Z}$-type topology in a quasi-one-dimensional extended QWZ model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03401)
*Zebedeus F. Osseweijer,Lumen Eek,Cristiane Morais Smith*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper studies topological end states in nanoribbons based on the QWZ model, finding that real-space ribbon geometry can induce an emergent chiral symmetry that changes the topological classification from class D to BDI. The existence of this symmetry depends on ribbon termination and width (even-odd effect), and extended models support multiple topological end states with higher winding numbers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the real-space geometry of nanoribbons (long and end termination, width) affects the emergence of zero-dimensional topological end states and the symmetry classification of confined Chern insulators beyond the commonly assumed dimensional reduction.

Method: Analytical derivation of topological phase boundaries for finite-width QWZ nanoribbons; characterization using Z2 and winding-number invariants; study of extended QWZ models with longer-range couplings.

Result: An emergent chiral symmetry (combining orbital and spatial transformations) promotes the 1D system from class D to BDI; symmetry existence depends on ribbon termination and exhibits an even-odd effect in width; extended models yield phases with multiple topological end states and higher winding numbers via hybridization of edge modes from higher-Chern-number bulk phases.

Conclusion: Both long and end termination fundamentally alter the topological classification of confined Chern insulators, highlighting the interplay between crystalline geometry, emergent symmetries, and dimensional reduction.

Abstract: We investigate the emergence of zero-dimensional topological end states in nanoribbons described by the Qi-Wu-Zhang (QWZ) model and its extensions with longer-range couplings. While dimensional reduction from two to one dimension is often assumed to preserve the symmetry classification of the parent system, here an additional symmetry can emerge originating from the real-space geometry of the ribbon. This symmetry acts as a chiral symmetry, combining orbital and spatial transformations, and promotes the effective one-dimensional system from symmetry class D to class BDI. We demonstrate that the existence of such a symmetry depends both on the long and end termination of the ribbon and exhibits an even-odd effect with respect to ribbon width, revealing that the commonly studied rectangular ribbons constitute a special high-symmetry case. For the conventional QWZ model, we derive analytic expressions for the topological phase boundaries of finite-width nanoribbons and characterize the resulting hybridization-gap phases through ($\mathbb{Z}_2 $) and winding-number invariants. We further show that extended QWZ models with longer-range couplings support phases with multiple topological end states and higher winding numbers. These phases arise through distinct mechanisms, including the hybridization of multiple edge modes inherited from higher-Chern-number bulk phases. Our results demonstrate that both long and end termination can fundamentally alter the topological classification of confined Chern insulators, highlighting the interplay between crystalline geometry, emergent symmetries, and dimensional reduction.

</details>


### [11] [Evidence of length scale effect in contact electrification in conducting thin film heterostructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03428)
*Paul C. Lou,Ravindra G. Bhardwaj,Anand Katailiha,W. P. Beyermann,Sandeep Kumar*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper experimentally demonstrates a length scale effect in contact electrification between permalloy and degenerately doped p-Si, where charge penetration depth scales with material thickness (full 400 nm vs. 51 nm in 2 μm Si), mediated by flexoelectricity, and induces metal-insulator transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contact electrification between conductors is expected to show length scale effects due to diminished screening in finite dimensions, but this has not been experimentally demonstrated.

Method: Experiments on a permalloy/degenerately doped p-Si heterostructure system, comparing charge transfer behavior in 400 nm thick vs. 2 μm thick p-Si samples, with flexoelectricity as the mediating mechanism.

Result: In 400 nm p-Si, contact electrification induces interlayer charge transfer through the entire thickness; in 2 μm Si, charge carriers diffuse only to ~51 nm from the interface. This length scale effect also drives a metal-insulator transition in p-Si layers for both thicknesses.

Conclusion: The findings open new opportunities to tailor physical properties in conducting materials via contact electrification by exploiting the length scale effect.

Abstract: Contact electrification between two conducting materials is expected to exhibit length scale effect because the screening effect will diminish in conductors as a function of material dimensions. As a consequence, the interfacial charge accumulation will diffuse away from interface/surface to a critical penetration depth as a function of material dimension. This work experimentally demonstrates the length scale effect in a permalloy and degenerately doped p-Si heterostructure system due to the flexoelectricity mediated contact electrification. The contact electrification induced interlayer charge transfer is observed through the whole thickness in case of 400 nm thick p-Si samples. Whereas, the charge carrier diffuses to a depth of 51 nm from the interface in case of 2 um thick Si. The length scale effect also leads to metal-insulator transition in p-Si layers in both cases. These results present a new opportunity to tailor the physical properties in conducting materials using contact electrification.

</details>


### [12] [Graphene Electric Double-Layer Transistors for Enhanced-Sensitivity Label-Free Detection of Human Serum Albumin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03491)
*Arslan Liaquat,Ghassem Baridi,Federico Rapuzzi,Daniele Goldoni,Vito Clerico,El Hadj Abidi,Yahya Moubarak Meziani,Mario Amado,Enrique Diez,Naveen Kumar,Camilia Coletti,Beatrice Cipriani,Hender Lopez,Giorgia Brancolini,Leonardo Martini,Luigi Rovati,Francesco Rossella*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper presents a graphene-based electrolyte-gated field-effect transistor (EGFET) for label-free, real-time detection of human serum albumin (HSA) under non-Faradaic operation, achieving a limit of detection of 0.0087 mg mL⁻¹ with reversible behavior, supported by Brownian Dynamics simulations and disorder-mediated transport analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate detection of human serum albumin (HSA) is essential for early diagnosis and monitoring of renal and hepatic disorders, motivating the need for sensitive, label-free, real-time quantification methods.

Method: A graphene-based electrolyte-gated field-effect transistor (EGFET) is used under non-Faradaic operation, exploiting the high interfacial capacitance of the electric double layer (EDL) to transduce electrostatic perturbations from HSA adsorption into conductance modulation. Brownian Dynamics simulations provide molecular-level interpretation of adsorption orientations and energetics. An inverse-mobility analytical metric is used to highlight disorder-enhanced carrier scattering in signal amplification.

Result: The device exhibits a limit of detection of 0.0087 mg mL⁻¹ and a linear dynamic range extending to 10 mg mL⁻¹. HSA adsorption induces concentration-dependent Dirac voltage displacement consistent with p-type doping. The response is non-Faradaic under sub-volt operation with reversible and reproducible behavior.

Conclusion: Liquid-gated graphene EGFETs are established as a promising platform for quantitative protein sensing, with insights into disorder-mediated transport mechanisms in graphene bioelectronic devices.

Abstract: Accurate detection of human serum albumin (HSA) is essential for the early diagnosis and monitoring of renal and hepatic disorders. We present a graphene-based electrolyte-gated field-effect transistor (EGFET) for label-free, real-time quantification of HSA under non-Faradaic operation. Devices exploit the high interfacial capacitance of the electric double layer (EDL) to transduce electrostatic perturbations induced by albumin adsorption into measurable conductance modulation. Negatively charged HSA molecules induce systematic modulation of the graphene channel, producing a concentration-dependent displacement of the Dirac voltage consistent with p-type doping. To establish a molecular-level interpretation of the sensing response, Brownian Dynamics simulations show that HSA adsorbs onto graphene through multiple adsorption orientations associated with heterogeneous interfacial charge distributions and variable dipole alignments relative to the surface. Adsorption is energetically stabilized by van der Waals interactions. Analysis of transfer characteristics across concentrations ranging from 0.01 to 30mgmL-1 reveals a correlation between surface charge density and carrier transport modulation within the electric double layer. Optimized devices exhibit a limit of detection of 0.0087 mg mL-1 and a linear dynamic range extending to 10 mg mL-1. The response remains non-Faradaic under sub-volt operation with reversible and reproducible behavior. The use of an inverse-mobility analytical metric highlights the role of disorder-enhanced carrier scattering in signal amplification, enabling sensitive electrostatic detection while preserving reversible device operation. These results establish liquid-gated graphene EGFETs as a promising platform for quantitative protein sensing and provide insight into disorder-mediated transport mechanisms in graphene bioelectronic devices.

</details>


### [13] [Robustness of quantized Hall resistivity under cavity coupling at zero temperature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03511)
*Juan Román-Roche,Jie Wang,Michael Ruggenthaler,Angel Rubio,Vasil Rokaj*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper reveals that while Hall conductivity in quantum Hall systems can be modified under strong light-matter coupling at finite temperature/broadening, the Hall resistivity remains immune to such modifications at zero temperature, explaining why the von Klitzing constant is unrenormalized in experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the apparent contradiction between theoretical predictions of modified Hall conductivity under strong light-matter coupling and experimental observations of unrenormalized Hall resistivity (von Klitzing constant), by investigating the asymmetry between conductivity and resistivity tensors in such hybrid systems.

Method: A phenomenological model analyzing the relationship between conductivity and resistivity tensors in quantum Hall systems under strong light-matter interaction, considering finite temperature, polariton lifetime/broadening, and light-matter coupling strength.

Result: At zero temperature, the Hall resistivity remains completely immune to cavity-induced modifications from polariton broadening, independent of coupling strength. This creates an asymmetry between conductivity (which can be modified) and resistivity (which is protected).

Conclusion: The robustness of Hall resistivity explains the absence of von Klitzing constant renormalization in low-temperature experiments probing even QH plateaus, and clarifies the distinct roles of dissipation vs. strong coupling in hybrid light-matter systems.

Abstract: Recent experiments have shown that strong light-matter coupling in electromagnetic cavities can modify transport properties of quantum Hall systems through the formation of Landau polaritons, prompting questions about the robustness of topological protection. While earlier theory demonstrated that the Hall conductivity can be modified at finite temperature and finite polariton lifetime (or finite broadening), experiments primarily probe the resistivity tensor. Our phenomenological model reveals an asymmetry between conductivity and resistivity in quantum Hall systems under strong light-matter interaction, showing that at zero temperature the Hall resistivity remains completely immune to cavity-induced modifications arising from polariton broadening, independent of the light-matter coupling strength. These results provide a deeper explanation for the absence of renormalization in the von Klitzing constant in experiments probing the even QH plateaus through the Hall resistivity at low temperature, and clarify the distinct roles of dissipation and strong light-matter coupling in hybrid light-matter systems.

</details>


### [14] [Spin-orbit torque-driven synthetic antiferromagnetic oscillator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03708)
*P. K. Rout,J. Godinho,F. Vilsmeier,R. Salikhov,J. A. Vélez,Z. Šobáň,D. Laroze,O. Gomonay,R. M. Otxoa,C. H. Back,O. Hellwig,J. Wunderlich*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates a spin-orbit torque-driven antiferromagnetic oscillator using a synthetic antiferromagnet nanoconstriction, showing both linear eigenmodes (acoustic/optical) and nonlinear self-oscillatory dynamics near the spin-flop transition, with signatures of injection locking and chaotic dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Antiferromagnets are promising for robust spintronic devices due to their compensated magnetic order and exchange-enhanced spin dynamics, but current-driven antiferromagnetic oscillators remain underexplored experimentally.

Method: Spin-rectification spectroscopy and micromagnetic/macrospin simulations on a nanoconstriction-patterned synthetic antiferromagnet driven by spin-orbit torque.

Result: Electrical excitation of acoustic and optical SAF eigenmodes; observation of low-field spin-rectification peaks above threshold DC current near spin-flop transition with current-polarity-dependent sign and RF injection locking; micromagnetic simulations confirm threshold excitation and injection locking; macrospin simulations predict stable and chaotic dynamics.

Conclusion: SAF nanoconstrictions provide an experimentally accessible platform for studying current-driven antiferromagnetic-like oscillator dynamics, enabling future nonlinear spintronic devices for signal processing and reservoir computing.

Abstract: Antiferromagnets offer a promising route toward robust spintronic devices because of their compensated magnetic order and exchange-enhanced spin dynamics. Here, we demonstrate a spin-orbit torque (SOT)-driven antiferromagnetic oscillator based on a nanoconstriction patterned from a synthetic antiferromagnet (SAF). Spin-rectification spectroscopy reveals electrical excitation of both acoustic and optical SAF eigenmodes, whose field and frequency dependences are quantitatively described by an antiferromagnetic resonance model. In addition to these linear eigenmodes, we observe low-field spin-rectification peaks that emerge only above a threshold DC current near the spin-flop transition. Their current-polarity-dependent sign and locking to an injected RF frequency provide electrical spin-rectification signatures consistent with current-selected chiral self-oscillatory dynamics. Micromagnetic simulations reproduce the threshold excitation of SOT-driven self-oscillations and injection locking, while macrospin simulations predict stable and chaotic nonlinear dynamics within the same spin-flop region. We interpret the multi-peak, weakly RF-frequency-dependent responses as a qualitative signature of complex nonlinear dynamics. These results establish SAF nanoconstrictions as an experimentally accessible platform for studying current-driven antiferromagnetic-like oscillator dynamics and motivate future work on nonlinear spintronic devices for signal processing and reservoir-computing concepts.

</details>


### [15] [Nonlocal Orbital-Angular-Momentum Dichroism of Vortex Light in Strained Crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03544)
*Habib Rostami*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Vortex light carrying OAM cannot produce OAM dichroism in translation-invariant systems, but nonuniform strain in 2D Dirac materials bypasses this restriction via a pseudo-gauge field, enabling measurable OAM-odd conductivity (~10⁻³ e²/ℏ) for micron-scale Gaussian bubbles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether optical orbital angular momentum (OAM) dichroism can exist in continuous translation-invariant systems, and to find a physical mechanism that enables OAM-dependent light-matter interaction.

Method: Theoretical analysis proving a no-go theorem for OAM dichroism in translation-invariant systems, then demonstrating that nonuniform strain in 2D Dirac materials creates a pseudo-gauge field that provides transverse momentum transfer, enabling OAM dichroism through a nonlocal elasto-optic tensor.

Result: The resonant OAM-odd conductivity reaches approximately 10⁻³ e²/ℏ for a micron-scale Gaussian bubble, which is within detectable range for differential optical measurements.

Conclusion: Nonuniform strain in 2D Dirac materials provides a viable route to achieve measurable OAM dichroism by breaking translation invariance via a pseudo-gauge field, circumventing the fundamental no-go theorem.

Abstract: Vortex light carries orbital angular momentum (OAM) in its transverse phase, unlike spin, which is encoded in polarization. We show that this phase cannot produce OAM dichroism in any continuously translation-invariant system, even when optical nonlocality and multipole light--matter coupling are included. Nonuniform strain in a two-dimensional Dirac material bypasses this no-go condition by supplying transverse momentum transfer through a long-wavelength pseudo-gauge field, so that the OAM dichroism factorizes into a nonlocal elasto-optic tensor and the overlap of the vortex transverse phase current with the strain field. For a micron-scale Gaussian bubble, the resonant OAM-odd conductivity reaches \(\sim10^{-3}e^2/\hbar\), within reach of differential optical measurements.

</details>


### [16] [Hybrid-order nonlinear topological phases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03678)
*Yu-Peng Ma,Ming-Jian Gao,Jun-Hong An*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper extends bulk-boundary correspondence to nonlinear eigenvalue systems using an auxiliary formalism, demonstrating that 2D nonlinear systems can host both first-order gapless and second-order corner modes. Stacking these 2D layers into 3D creates hybrid-order topological phases: uniform stacking yields second-order (hinge states) and first-order (side-surface states) phases, while dimerized stacking produces third-order (confined to ends) and second-order (hinge-localized) phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the open question of bulk-boundary correspondence in nonlinear systems where multigap topologies obscure traditional topological descriptions.

Method: Development of an auxiliary-system formalism for topological classification of nonlinear-eigenvalue systems, combined with stacking engineering (uniform and dimerized) to construct 3D higher-order topological phases.

Result: 2D nonlinear systems exhibit first-order gapless boundary modes and second-order corner modes. Uniform stacking produces 3D first-order (side-surface) and second-order (hinge) phases; dimerized stacking yields 3D second-order (hinge-localized) and third-order (end-confined) phases.

Conclusion: Establishes a multiband bulk-boundary correspondence for nonlinear systems and identifies stacking engineering as a platform for exploring hybrid-order topological phases.

Abstract: The bulk-boundary correspondence (BBC), which relates topological invariants to boundary modes, is well understood for linear systems but remains an open question in the presence of nonlinearity, where multigap topologies make the BBC obscure and the topological description troublesome. We address this by developing an auxiliary-system formalism that enables topological classification of nonlinear-eigenvalue systems. In the two-dimension (2D) case, we show that two fixed eigenvalues can harbor first-order gapless boundary modes and second-order corner modes. Stacking this 2D system along the third dimension (3D) reveals distinct hybrid-order realizations. Under uniform stacking, the corner states in the $xy$ plane transform into hinge states along the $z$ axis, yielding a 3D second-order phase, while the gapless boundary states become side-surface states, yielding a 3D first-order phase. For dimerized stacking, these states are further confined to the two ends of the $z$ axis, yielding a 3D third-order phase, and localized at the hinges, yielding a 3D second-order phase. Our results establish a multiband bulk-boundary correspondence and identify stacking engineering as a versatile platform for exploring hybrid-order topological phases in nonlinear systems.

</details>


### [17] [Tunable Nonlinear Landscapes in Graphene Nanoelectromechanical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04724)
*Ateesh K. Rathi,Rajan Singh,Javed A. Mondal,Arnab Sarkar,Ryan J. T. Nicholl,Kirill I. Bolotin,Saikat Ghosh*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper reports experimental observation of integer high-harmonic generation and phononic frequency combs in a suspended monolayer graphene drum, achieved by electrostatic gating to tune the fundamental mode into 1:2 internal resonance. The nonlinear coefficients are extracted from measured spectra, and distinct dynamical regimes (frequency multiplication, frequency combs, chaotic generation) are demonstrated in a single device.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore classical analogs of extreme nonlinear optics and phononic signal processing using nonlinear nanomechanical resonators, specifically leveraging the tunable nonlinear landscape of graphene membranes.

Method: A suspended monolayer graphene drum is gated to break out-of-plane symmetry and tune its fundamental flexural mode into a 1:2 internal resonance with a higher mode. A single drive tone is applied, generating phase-locked integer harmonics and subsequently frequency combs at larger drives. A reverse period-doubling transition is observed at even higher drive levels.

Result: Integer high-harmonic generation, dense frequency combs, and a reverse period-doubling transition (comb spacing doubling, line density halving, energy flow back into even-order comb lines) are observed. Quadratic (ζ) and cubic (β) nonlinear coefficients are extracted from the measured spectra.

Conclusion: The tunable nonlinear landscape of graphene allows a single gated device to act as a frequency multiplier, broadband comb source, and chaotic generator on demand, demonstrating distinct dynamical regimes via a single platform.

Abstract: Nonlinear nanomechanical resonators give convenient solid-state access to classical analogs of extreme nonlinear optics and to phononic signal processing. Here we report integer high-harmonic generation and phononic frequency combs in a suspended monolayer graphene drum. A gate voltage breaks the out-of-plane symmetry of the membrane and tunes its fundamental flexural mode onto a 1:2 internal resonance with a higher mode at twice the frequency, where the quadratic coupling between the two modes becomes large. A single drive tone then generates phase-locked integer harmonics in sequence, and at larger drive these fill in to a dense frequency comb. Raising the drive further, we find a reverse period-doubling transition: the comb spacing doubles, the line density halves, and energy flows back into the even-order comb lines. The measured spectra yield the quadratic ($ζ$) and cubic ($β$) nonlinear coefficients of the membrane. These results show how the tunable nonlinear landscape of graphene supports distinct dynamical regimes on demand, allowing a single gated device to act in turn as a frequency multiplier, a broadband comb source, and a chaotic generator.

</details>


### [18] [On the $\mathrm{In_{x}Ga_{1-x}As}$ channel noise in InP HEMTs from 4 K to 300 K](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04757)
*Junjie Li,Justin H. Chen,Austin J. Minnich,Jan Grahn*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper investigates channel noise in InP HEMTs with varying indium content (53%, 60%, 70%) from 4 K to 300 K, finding that 60% indium content yields the lowest noise, and that channel noise comprises thermal noise (dominant at 300 K) and excess noise (dominant at 4 K), with excess noise arising from shot noise, impact ionization, and real-space transfer noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: InP HEMTs are critical for low-noise amplifiers in radio astronomy and quantum computing, and while channel indium content is known to affect noise performance, the underlying physical noise mechanisms are not fully characterized or understood.

Method: 100-nm gate-length InP HEMTs with 53%, 60%, and 70% indium content were characterized via on-wafer measurements (40-300 K) and LNA-based measurements (4-40 K). Channel noise was quantified by extracting equivalent drain noise temperature Td, and analyzed by decomposing into thermal and excess noise components.

Result: The 60% indium channel exhibited the lowest noise across all temperatures. Td showed parabolic temperature dependence; thermal noise increased linearly with temperature and dominated at 300 K, while excess noise dominated at 4 K and exhibited non-monotonic dependence on indium content.

Conclusion: Excess noise in InP HEMTs originates not only from temperature-independent shot noise but also from impact ionization and real-space transfer noise.

Abstract: The InP high-electron-mobility transistor (HEMT) is indispensable for low-noise amplifiers (LNAs) in radio astronomy and quantum computing. The composition of the $\mathrm{In_{x}Ga_{1-x}As}$ channel in InP HEMT is known to influence the LNA noise performance. However, the various physical mechanisms responsible for noise generation are not fully characterized and understood. Here, we investigate the $\mathrm{In_{x}Ga_{1-x}As}$ channel noise from 4 K to 300 K for 100-nm gate-length InP HEMTs with channel indium content of 53\%, 60\% and 70\%. Channel noise was quantified by extracting the equivalent drain noise temperature $\mathit{T}_{d}$ using both on-wafer and LNA-based measurements, covering 40-300 K and 4-40 K, respectively. The 60\% indium channel InP HEMT exhibited the lowest channel noise across the full temperature range. The $\mathit{T}_{d}$ extracted from on-wafer characterization was found to obey a parabolic temperature dependence which predicted the $\mathit{T}_{d}$ at 4 K for all InP HEMTs in good agreement with LNA-based measurements. By expressing the channel noise as the sum of one thermal and one excess noise term, it was found that the former increased linearly with ambient temperature and dominated at 300 K. The channel noise at 4 K was determined by the excess noise term and exhibited a non-monotonic dependence on the channel indium content in the InP HEMT. The results suggest that the excess noise in the InP HEMT originates not only from temperature-independent shot noise but also from impact ionization and real-space transfer noise.

</details>


### [19] [Nonlinear Hall effect in Floquet-driven monolayer 1T$'$-MoS$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03717)
*Muhammad Faisal,Muzamil Shah,Imtiaz Khan,Reza Asgari*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper theoretically demonstrates that off-resonant circularly polarized light can control the sign and magnitude of the nonlinear Hall effect in monolayer 1T'-MoS₂ via Floquet topological phase transitions, with sign reversal serving as a transport fingerprint of topological transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study the nonlinear Hall effect in a low-symmetry quantum spin Hall material (1T'-MoS₂) that intrinsically exhibits Berry-curvature dipole without external strain or trigonal warping, and to explore optical control of topological phase transitions via Floquet engineering.

Method: Floquet effective Hamiltonian combined with nonlinear semiclassical transport theory to analyze Berry-curvature dipole and nonlinear Hall conductivity under off-resonant circularly polarized light.

Result: (1) Anisotropic symmetry enforces D_x ≡ 0 while finite D_y arises from intrinsic band tilt. (2) Floquet drive inverts spin-valley sectors, causing sign reversal of both Berry-curvature dipole and nonlinear Hall conductivity at each bulk gap closing. (3) Band tilt only affects magnitude, not sign. (4) Response is tunable via driving strength, electric field, Fermi energy, and temperature.

Conclusion: The sign of the nonlinear Hall response is a universal transport fingerprint of Floquet-engineered topological phase transitions, and monolayer 1T'-MoS₂ is a viable platform for all-electrical detection of nonequilibrium topological phases.

Abstract: We study the nonlinear Hall effect in Floquet-driven monolayer \(1T'\)-MoS\(_2\), a low-symmetry quantum spin Hall material whose tilted Dirac bands sustain an intrinsic Berry-curvature dipole without the need for strain or trigonal warping. We show that off-resonant circularly polarized light offers a way to control both the sign and the magnitude of the nonlinear Hall response through optically induced topological phase transitions using a Floquet effective Hamiltonian and nonlinear semiclassical transport theory. We show that the anisotropic crystal symmetry enforces a selection rule in which the Berry-curvature dipole elements satisfy $D_x\equiv0$, while a finite $D_y$ originates from the intrinsic band tilt. The Berry curvature is recreated in momentum space as the Floquet drive successively inverts individual spin-valley sectors, resulting in an identical sign reversal of the nonlinear Hall conductivity and the Berry-curvature dipole at each bulk gap closing. In contrast, tuning the band tilt modifies only the magnitude of the response without changing its sign, establishing the observed sign reversal as an unambiguous transport signature of genuine Floquet topological phase transitions. We further show that the nonlinear Hall response can be controlled by the driving strength, perpendicular electric field, Fermi energy, and temperature, providing multiple experimental knobs for observation. Our findings establish the sign of the nonlinear Hall response as a universal transport fingerprint of Floquet-engineered topology and point to monolayer \(1T'\)-MoS\(_2\) as a viable platform for all-electrical detection of nonequilibrium topological phases.

</details>


### [20] [Spin-momentum locking of polariton edge states in honeycomb lattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03961)
*Andrea Herrero Otermin,Nicola Carlon Zambon,Dheerendra Singh,Neha Bhoria,Rimi Banerjee,Christian Mayer,Simon Betzold,Siddhartha Dam,Monika Emmerling,Sven Höfling,Luis Viña,Subhaskar Mandal,Carlos Antón-Solanas*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates spin-momentum locking in photonic edge states via TE/TM splitting in microcavities, enabling chiral edge state lasing without external magnetic fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve spin-controlled unidirectional propagation in polariton systems without external magnetic fields, leveraging spin-orbit coupling from TE/TM splitting.

Method: Used Stokes polarimetry to analyze zigzag edge states in a honeycomb exciton-polariton lattice; employed a stretched honeycomb structure to create a photonic bandgap for selective lasing of chiral edge states via spin-polarized carrier injection.

Result: Edge states exhibit elliptical polarization with handedness locked to propagation direction (spin-momentum locking). Spin-polarized injection enables selective lasing of either chiral edge state in the stretched honeycomb lattice.

Conclusion: Provides a viable route toward ultrafast spin-controlled unidirectional light propagation in polariton systems without external magnetic fields.

Abstract: Transverse-electric/Transverse-magnetic splitting in dielectric-mirror microcavities introduces an effective spin-orbit coupling for photons. While the bulk states remain linearly polarized, for exponentially localized edge states in a photonic lattice, this coupling induces elliptical polarization whose handedness is locked to the propagation direction, analogous to the transverse spin of evanescent electromagnetic waves. We reveal spin-momentum locking through Stokes polarimetry of zigzag edge states in a honeycomb exciton-polariton lattice. The effect persists in a stretched honeycomb supporting a photonic bandgap, where spin-polarized carrier injection enables selective lasing of either chiral edge states. Our results provide a route toward ultrafast spin-controlled unidirectional propagation in polariton systems without external magnetic fields.

</details>


### [21] [Phase-Factor-Controlled Interaction and Bonding between a Chiral Bobber and a Skyrmion String in the Conical Phase](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04266)
*Haijun Zhao,Tingting Yan,Shuai Dong*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper reveals that the interaction between chiral bobbers and skyrmion strings in the conical phase is intrinsically phase-dependent, producing repulsive, attractive, and bonding-like regimes that depend on the surface phase factor φ₀, not just separation distance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While skyrmion interactions (lattices, clusters, growth patterns) are well studied, the interaction between chiral bobbers and skyrmion strings remains unexplored, even though bobbers serve as intermediate states in skyrmion-string formation and annihilation.

Method: Three-dimensional micromagnetic simulations computing the locally relaxed constrained energy landscape as a function of bobber–skyrmion separation R and surface phase factor φ₀.

Result: Changing φ₀ qualitatively reshapes the interaction, producing repulsive, attractive, and bonding-like regimes beyond a conventional distance-dependent potential. This originates from phase-dependent reconstruction of nonaxisymmetric outer distortion shells. The effect persists over a finite field range and follows the top–bottom phase relation of surface-sensitive conical textures.

Conclusion: The conical phase direction is identified as a previously underappreciated degree of freedom in interactions between skyrmion strings and finite-length chiral textures, often hidden in projected or thickness-averaged descriptions.

Abstract: Skyrmion interactions govern the formation of skyrmion lattices, clusters, and particle-like growth patterns. In contrast, the interaction between a chiral bobber and a skyrmion string remains largely unexplored, despite the role of bobbers as intermediate states in skyrmion-string formation and annihilation. Here we show that this interaction is intrinsically phase dependent in the conical phase. Using three-dimensional micromagnetic simulations, we compute the locally relaxed constrained energy landscape as a function of the bobber--skyrmion separation $R$ and the surface phase factor $φ_0$. We find that changing $φ_0$ qualitatively reshapes the interaction, producing repulsive, attractive, and bonding-like regimes that cannot be reduced to a conventional distance-dependent potential. Real-space analysis shows that this behavior originates from phase-dependent reconstruction of nonaxisymmetric outer distortion shells. The phase-controlled interaction persists over a finite field range and follows the expected top--bottom phase relation of surface-sensitive conical textures. These results identify the conical phase direction, often hidden in projected or thickness-averaged descriptions, as a previously underappreciated degree of freedom in the interaction between skyrmion strings and finite-length chiral textures, as demonstrated here for chiral bobbers.

</details>


### [22] [Quasi-two-dimensional Majorana zero modes from finite-size-coupled chiral hinge states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04358)
*Wenhao Liang,Xun-Jiang Luo*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes using a quasi-two-dimensional chiral higher-order topological insulator (HOTI) proximitized by an s-wave superconductor to realize spatially separated Majorana zero modes (MZMs) at corners, forming a second-order topological superconductor. The platform also yields extended Majorana hinge modes in 3D, and the corner modes are robust against weak-to-moderate disorder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To realize spatially separated Majorana zero modes (MZMs) in a controlled platform for topological quantum computation, using chiral higher-order topological insulators proximitized by conventional s-wave superconductors.

Method: Theoretical model study: confining a 3D chiral HOTI (which hosts gapless z-directed hinge states) along x, keeping y extended and z finite, so that opposite chiral hinge states hybridize into 1D helical channels. Superconducting proximity effect from an s-wave superconductor induces effective p-wave pairing in these helical channels, opening a topological gap.

Result: A fully open-boundary sample hosts four localized MZMs, one at each endpoint of the helical channels, realizing a second-order topological superconductor with Majorana corner modes. In 3D, superconducting pairing also produces extended Majorana hinge modes. The corner modes are robust against weak-to-moderate disorder as long as the excitation gap remains open.

Conclusion: Finite-size-coupled chiral hinge states provide a promising platform for engineering multiple MZMs via conventional superconducting proximity effect, establishing a route toward topological quantum computation.

Abstract: Majorana zero modes (MZMs) in topological superconductors have attracted broad research interest for their potential applications in topological quantum computation. In this work, we propose a quasi-two-dimensional route to realize spatially separated MZMs in a chiral higher-order topological insulator (HOTI) proximitized by a conventional $s$-wave superconductor through a theoretical model study. In three dimensions, the chiral HOTI hosts gapless hinge states along the $z$ direction, arising from a mass term that anisotropically gaps the surface Dirac cones of a topological insulator. By confining the sample along the $x$ direction while keeping it extended along $y$ and finite along $z$, opposite $z$-directed chiral hinge states hybridize and effectively form one-dimensional helical channels. Incorporating the superconducting proximity effect into this quasi-two-dimensional system induces effective $p$-wave pairing in these helical channels, thereby opening a topological gap. A fully open-boundary sample then hosts four localized MZMs, one at each endpoint of the helical channels, realizing a second-order topological superconductor characterized by Majorana corner modes. In addition to MZMs, we also find that superconducting pairing in this model produces extended Majorana hinge modes in three dimensions. Furthermore, representative disorder calculations indicate that these Majorana corner modes are robust against weak-to-moderate disorder, provided the excitation gap remains open. These results establish finite-size-coupled chiral hinge states as a promising platform for engineering multiple MZMs via conventional superconducting proximity effect.

</details>


### [23] [Quantum Geometric Friedel Oscillations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04654)
*Xing-Lei MA,Jinchao Zhao,Bo-Qing Wu,K. T. Law*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper shows that in metals with isolated flat bands, quantum geometry induces a new type of Friedel oscillations (QGFOs) with period set by quantum metric hot spots and decay length set by the quantum metric length, which are temperature-resistant and distinct from conventional Friedel oscillations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The conventional theory of Friedel oscillations is incomplete when Bloch wavefunctions carry nontrivial quantum geometry, specifically in metals with isolated flat bands at the Fermi energy.

Method: The authors theoretically analyze charge density oscillations induced by impurities in flat-band metals, incorporating quantum geometric effects through the quantum metric of Bloch wavefunctions.

Result: Quantum geometric Friedel oscillations (QGFOs) have periods set by momentum-space separation of quantum metric hot spots, and decay lengths set by the quantum metric length. QGFOs persist at temperatures much larger than the flat-band bandwidth, with decay length independent of temperature over a wide range.

Conclusion: The quantum metric induces novel Friedel oscillations, and measuring QGFOs provides a powerful way to detect the quantum metric length and quantum metric hot spot separations in momentum space.

Abstract: In conventional Friedel oscillations, the real-space charge density oscillations induced by an impurity are characterized by an oscillation period set by the Fermi momentum. In this work, we show that the conventional theory is incomplete when the Bloch wavefunctions carry nontrivial quantum geometry. We demonstrate that in metals with an isolated (nearly) flat band at the Fermi energy, quantum geometry induces a distinct type of oscillations, which we call the \emph{quantum geometric Friedel oscillations} (QGFOs). The period of the QGFOs is set by the momentum space separation of the quantum metric hot spots of the flat band. The conventional and quantum metric-induced oscillations coexist at low temperatures. At higher temperatures, the conventional Friedel oscillations away from the impurity site are set by the thermal length such that the oscillations can be easily washed out by temperature effects. Remarkably, the QGFOs decay length is set by the quantum metric length which is defined by the integration of the quantum metric of the flat band. As a result, the QGFOs can persist even at temperatures much larger than the bandwidth of the flat band. Moreover, the decay length is independent of temperature for a wide range of temperatures which is a manifestation of the quantum metric protection. In conclusion, we show that the quantum metric induces novel Friedel oscillations. Our work suggests that the measurement of the QGFOs is a powerful way to detect the quantum metric length (which is associated with the integral of the quantum metric) and the quantum metric hot spot separations (which are associated with the distribution of the quantum metric in the momentum space).

</details>


### [24] [Observation of Non-linear hall effect in Polycrystalline magnetic multilayes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04754)
*Preet Kamal,Chirag kalouni,Amiya Mondal,Awadesh Narayan,Debangsu Roy*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper reports the first observation of a robust Berry-curvature-dipole-driven Nonlinear Hall Effect (NLHE) in polycrystalline heavy-metal/ferromagnet multilayers, extending this effect from van der Waals crystals into scalable, industry-compatible spintronic platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the Nonlinear Hall Effect, previously established only in non-centrosymmetric van der Waals crystals, topological semimetals, and moiré superlattices, into technologically mature heavy-metal/ferromagnet magnetic multilayer platforms that are compatible with industrial spintronic applications.

Method: The authors report experimental observation of NLHE in sputter-deposited polycrystalline magnetic multilayers, characterized by measuring second-harmonic transverse voltage signals. They performed scaling analysis of second-order Hall conductivity against longitudinal conductivity to identify the intrinsic Berry curvature dipole contribution. Vanishingly small third-harmonic response ruled out quantum-metric contributions. First-principles DFT calculations supported the experimental findings.

Result: A robust NLHE persisting from 2 K to room temperature was observed. The second-harmonic transverse voltage was independent of excitation frequency and out-of-plane magnetic field. The third-harmonic response was negligible, confirming a genuine second-order electronic response. The scaling analysis revealed a dominant, conductivity-independent term establishing the intrinsic Berry curvature dipole as the origin.

Conclusion: This work establishes sputter-deposited polycrystalline thin films as the first engineered magnetic multilayer platform for BCD-driven NLHE, extending the material landscape beyond van der Waals systems into scalable, industry-compatible thin-film spintronic architectures suitable for high-frequency rectification and nonlinear sensors.

Abstract: The Nonlinear Hall effect(NLHE) driven principally by the Berry curvature dipole has been established in non-centrosymmetric vander Waals crystals, topological semimetals, and moiré superlattices, but its extension to technologically mature heavy-metal/ferromagnet multilayer platforms remains largely unexplored. Here, we report the observation of a robust NLHE in polycrystalline magnetic multilayers, persisting from 2 K to room temperature. The second-harmonic transverse voltage is independent of both excitation frequency and applied out-of-plane magnetic field, while the vanishingly small third-harmonic response confirms that the observed signal is not dominated by a quantum-metric contribution and instead reflects a genuine second-order electronic response. A scaling analysis of the second-order Hall conductivity against the longitudinal conductivity identifies a dominant, conductivity-independent term establishing the intrinsic berry curvature dipole. Our theoretical analysis, supported by first-principles DFT calculations, further satisfies and corroborates the experimental results. These results establish sputter-deposited polycrystalline thin film as the first engineered magnetic multilayer platform for BCD-driven nonlinear Hall transport, extending the NLHE material landscape beyond van der Waals systems into scalable, industry-compatible thin-film spintronic architectures suitable for high frequency rectifications and nonlinear sensors.

</details>


### [25] [Strain- and potential-controlled tunneling in monolayer MoS$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04766)
*Hasna Chnafa,Rachid El Aitouni,Clarence Cortes,David Laroze,Ahmed Jellal*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper theoretically investigates how mechanical strain and an external scalar potential jointly control spin- and valley-resolved quantum transport in monolayer MoS₂, identifying a dual-knob control scheme where barrier width tunes conductance oscillation frequency while strain independently controls phase and amplitude, and predicting electrostatic spin inversion via gate tuning at finite strain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the unexplored simultaneous effect of mechanical strain and external scalar potential on spin- and valley-resolved transport in monolayer MoS₂, aiming to establish orthogonal control mechanisms for spintronic and valleytronic applications.

Method: Effective massive Dirac Hamiltonian incorporating intrinsic spin–orbit coupling; theoretical analysis of spin- and valley-resolved quantum transport under combined strain and scalar potential.

Result: Strain induces valley-dependent momentum shifts lifting K/K' degeneracy; scalar potential modifies tunneling spectrum (resonant transmission, Fabry–Pérot interference, conductance). Dual-knob control: barrier width governs conductance oscillation frequency, strain controls phase and amplitude. Predicted electrostatic spin inversion (sign reversal of spin polarization by gate tuning at finite strain). Strain orientation selectively suppresses or enhances transmission and conductance.

Conclusion: Strain and potential engineering provide orthogonal, independently operable mechanisms for controlling conductance, spin, and valley degrees of freedom in monolayer MoS₂, with promising prospects for spintronic and valleytronic devices.

Abstract: We present a theoretical study of spin- and valley-resolved quantum transport in monolayer MoS$_2$ under the combined influence of mechanical strain and an external scalar potential, a combination whose simultaneous unexplored. Within an effective massive Dirac Hamiltonian that incorporates intrinsic spin--orbit coupling, strain induces valley-dependent momentum shifts that lift the degeneracy between the $K$ and $K'$ valleys and strongly modify the transport characteristics. The scalar potential modifies the tunneling spectrum, leading to pronounced changes in resonant transmission, Fabry--Pérot interference, and conductance. We show that the interplay between strain and electrostatic potential enables efficient control of both valley and spin polarization of the transmitted current. In particular, we identify a dual-knob control scheme in which the barrier width governs the frequency of conductance oscillations while strain independently controls their phase and amplitude. Furthermore, we predict electrostatic spin inversion -- a sign reversal of spin polarization achievable purely by gate tuning at finite strain, requiring no geometric reconfiguration. Depending on the strain orientation, the transmission probability and conductance can be selectively suppressed or enhanced, resulting in highly tunable valley- and spin-polarized transport. These findings demonstrate that strain and potential engineering provide orthogonal and independently operable mechanisms for controlling conductance as well as spin and valley degrees of freedom in monolayer MoS$_2$, offering promising prospects for spintronic and valleytronic device applications.

</details>


### [26] [Local Defects and the Topology of the Haldane Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04771)
*Vishesh Makwana,Eric Akkermans*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates how vacancies (local defects) in the Haldane model (a Chern insulator with C=±1) create topologically protected states classified by a Z₂ invariant ν = C·m mod 2, where m is the sublattice imbalance. Odd imbalance yields protected zero-energy modes; even imbalance does not. Three signatures distinguish these from trivial defects: wavefunction phase dislocations, fractional e/2 charge accumulation, and reversed probability current circulation relative to chiral edge states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the interplay between local defects and topology in the Haldane model within the tenfold classification framework, bridging the gap between bulk topological invariants and defect-induced phenomena.

Method: Analytical and numerical analysis of the Haldane model with vacancies, using a Z₂ classification (ν = C·m mod 2) to predict protected zero modes based on sublattice imbalance, and identifying three experimental signatures (wavefunction dislocations, fractional charge, current reversal) to distinguish topological from trivial defects.

Result: Vacancies with odd sublattice imbalance host protected zero-energy modes, while even imbalance does not. Three signatures distinguish these: (1) wavefunction dislocations tracking phase winding, (2) fractional e/2 charge at vacancies (absent at adatoms), (3) reversed probability current circulation relative to chiral edge states, analogous to vortex current reversal in p-wave superconductors. All agree with the Z₂ prediction.
