# physics.chem-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [35] [A self-adaptive tube restraint for free-energy calculations along path collective variables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02735)
*Radu A. Talmazan,Cheng Giuseppe Chen,Chenyu Tang,Christophe Chipot*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents an adaptive tube restraint method for Path Collective Variables that dynamically adjusts the half-width to track a fixed contour of orthogonal free energy, automatically widening in flat basins and narrowing at saddles, eliminating the need for system- and s-dependent a priori width choices while avoiding bias in free-energy calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional fixed-width harmonic restraints for Path Collective Variables require an a priori tube width that is both system- and s-dependent; too tight biases the free energy, too loose causes numerical instability and inter-channel leakage.

Method: The method adaptively adjusts the tube half-width on the fly to track a fixed contour ΔF* of the orthogonal free energy F(z|s), widening in flat basins and narrowing at pinched saddles. The algorithm is implemented in the open-source Colvars library.

Result: Proven that for a locally harmonic perpendicular well, a contour-following tube captures an s-independent fraction of the orthogonal partition function, canceling out bias in the hard-wall limit, unlike fixed-width tubes. Validated on model potentials, alanine dipeptide isomerization, chignolin folding, and ribose-binding protein folding-upon-binding.

Conclusion: The adaptive tube restraint eliminates the need for manual tuning of tube width, removes systematic bias from free-energy landscapes, and is readily usable in major MD engines via the Colvars library.

Abstract: Path collective variables (PCVs) reduce a high-dimensional transition to a progress coordinate, s, and an orthogonal distance, z. Computing the free energy along s often requires restraining z, so that sampling embraces a tube centered on the reference path. However, a conventional fixed half-harmonic wall demands an a priori tube width that is both system dependent and s-dependent. Too tight a tube biases the path-projected free energy. Conversely, too loose a tube induces numerical instability and inter-channel leakage. We present an adaptive tube restraint the half-width of which evolves on the fly to track a fixed contour ΔF* of the orthogonal free energy F(z|s), widening automatically in flat basins and narrowing at pinched saddles, with minimalist user intervention. We prove that, for a locally harmonic perpendicular well, a contour-following tube captures an s-independent fraction of the orthogonal partition function, so that in the hard-wall limit, its bias cancels out. In stark contrast, the bias of a fixed-width tube varies with s and distorts the free-energy landscape. We probe the algorithm on several model potentials, on N-Acetyl-N'-methylalanylamide isomerization, on the folding of the mini-protein chignolin, and on the folding-upon-binding of the ribose-binding protein. The method is implemented in the open-source Colvars library, and is, therefore, usable within popular MD engines, such as NAMD, LAMMPS, GROMACS, and Tinker-HP.

</details>


### [36] [Auditing Haldane Consistency in Reversible Enzyme Kinetics: A Curated Two-Sided Backbone and a Labeled Fold-Error Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02784)
*Megan Simons,Jonathan Washburn*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a calibrated Haldane discrepancy score C_Haldane for auditing reversible enzyme kinetics. Using this metric on a curated backbone of 21 records, 18 fall within twofold error; a semi-synthetic benchmark achieves AUC 0.784. All code and data are archived for reproducibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reversible enzyme kinetic constants can be inconsistent with thermodynamic equilibrium constants, but no standardized, direction-symmetric, and calibratable metric existed to audit such discrepancies. The authors aim to provide a reproducible curation workflow and a calibrated reporting scale.

Method: Defines C_Haldane = J(K'_eq,kin / K'_eq,thermo) with J(x)=½(x+x⁻¹)−1 (cosh(ln x)−1). Applies to a curated set of 21 single-study records with prespecified inclusion criteria. A semi-synthetic benchmark (29 seeds, 1,885 injected errors) quantifies detectability via AUC.

Result: Eight genuinely independent tests all fall within twofold (max C_Haldane=0.069). Across the full backbone, 18/21 records fall within twofold; three are flagged. Benchmark AUC = 0.784 (95% CI 0.725–0.838), invariant under monotone rescaling of |ln x|.

Conclusion: The C_Haldane score provides a calibrated, direction-symmetric audit metric for Haldane consistency. The backbone is concentrated in carbohydrate isomerases/epimerases, so results are within-family. The archived pipeline enables exact reproduction and extension.

Abstract: Reversible enzyme kinetic constants can be audited through the Haldane relation: the apparent equilibrium constant implied by the rate law should match biochemical thermodynamics under matched conditions. We use the reciprocal cost $C_{\mathrm{Haldane}}=J(K'_{\mathrm{eq,kin}}/K'_{\mathrm{eq,thermo}})$, with $J(x)=\tfrac12(x+x^{-1})-1=\cosh(\ln x)-1$, as a calibrated, direction-symmetric reporting scale. The score is zero at agreement, penalizes reciprocal over- and underestimates equally, encodes the free-energy discrepancy in $RT$ units, and ranks records identically to $|ΔΔG|$; the contribution is therefore biochemical curation, a reproducible workflow, and fold-error calibration rather than a new ordering. We apply the score to a curated demonstration set and, under prespecified inclusion criteria, assemble a two-sided backbone of twenty-one audited single-study records. Eight genuinely independent tests pair kinetics fit without a thermodynamic prior against separately measured equilibria; all eight fall within twofold (maximum $C_{\mathrm{Haldane}}=0.069$), although this remains a feasibility demonstration. Across the full backbone, eighteen records fall within twofold and three are flagged. The backbone is concentrated in carbohydrate isomerases and epimerases, so these results are within-family observations. Because real records carry no ground-truth labels, a semi-synthetic benchmark (twenty-nine within-twofold seeds, $1{,}885$ injected known-error cases) quantifies detectability: AUC $0.784$ ($95\%$ bootstrap CI $0.725$--$0.838$), conditional on the injected error taxonomy and invariant under monotone rescaling of $|\ln x|$. All data, code, protocol, and benchmark generator are archived for exact reproduction.

</details>


### [37] [Resolving the inverse problem in pulse response analysis of TAP reactors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04776)
*Anjali Aleria,Evgeniy Redekop,A. K. Suresh,Jason R. Picardo*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper improves the Y-procedure for analyzing transient kinetics in TAP reactors by reformulating it as a regularized inverse problem using truncated singular value decomposition (TSVD) with localized square-pulse basis functions, replacing the previous ad hoc Fourier-based filtering. This yields better reconstructions of reaction rate vs. concentration relationships, especially for nonlinear reactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Y-procedure is a powerful model-free framework for inferring reaction-rate vs. concentration relationships from TAP reactor pulse experiments, but its practical application is hindered by noise amplification caused by the need to backtrack diffusive transport; previous Fourier-based filtering is ad hoc and suboptimal.

Method: The authors reformulate the Y-procedure as a discrete inverse problem using localized square-pulse basis functions, solve it via truncated singular value decomposition (TSVD), use Picard plots to guide regularization parameter selection, and develop an objective selection strategy for single-valued R(C) functions.

Result: The proposed inverse-problem approach produces better reconstructions of R vs. C relationships compared to the Fourier-filtration method, especially for nonlinear reactions.

Conclusion: This work facilitates automation of pulse response analyses and enables application of other discrete inverse-problem techniques (e.g., Tikhonov regularization or machine-learning methods) to TAP reactor data.

Abstract: Pulse experiments in the temporal analysis of products (TAP) reactor are one of the most important methods for studying transient kinetics of gas-solid catalytic reactions. The Y-procedure (Yablonsky et al., Chem. Eng. Sci. 62, 6754, 2007) is a model-free analysis framework for inferring the relationship between the reaction-rate $R$ and the reactant concentration $C$ from measurements of the outlet flux of gas. While elegant in conception, its application is hindered by the amplification of measurement noise that results from having to backtrack diffusive transport from the outlet to the reaction zone. Here, we explicitly recognize the inverse problem inherent in the Y-procedure and treat it using well-developed tools from the field of inverse problems. While previous implementations of the Y-procedure used Fourier-based filtering, we do not pre-process the measurements with an ad hoc noise-filter. Instead, we use a basis of localized square pulses to formulate a discrete inverse problem, whose regularized solution is obtained via the truncated singular value decomposition (TSVD) method. This method requires one to select a cutoff mode number; while we show how the choice of this regularization parameter can be guided by a Picard plot, we also develop an objective selection strategy for state defining experiments, for which $R(C)$ is a single-valued function. We apply our proposed inverse-problem approach to synthetic data corresponding to linear and nonlinear reactions and compare the results with the Fourier-filtration method. The former produces better reconstructions of the $R$ vs $C$ relationship, especially for nonlinear reactions. Our work facilitates the automation of pulse response analyses and enables the application of other discrete inverse-problem techniques, such as Tikhonov regularization or machine-learning methods.

</details>


### [38] [Chemical applicability of $π$-Sombor index](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02946)
*Monika S,H. M. Nagesh*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This study explores the chemical applicability of the π-Sombor index (SO · ᵐSO) through curvilinear regression analysis on 22 benzenoid hydrocarbons, examining its relationship with physicochemical properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The π-Sombor index, introduced in 2023 as the product of the Sombor index (SO) and modified Sombor index (ᵐSO), had established mathematical properties but its chemical applicability remained unexplored. This work addresses that gap.

Method: Curvilinear regression models were employed to analyze the π-Sombor index against the physicochemical properties of 22 benzenoid hydrocarbons.

Result: The study demonstrates the chemical significance of the π-Sombor index through its correlation with physicochemical properties of benzenoid hydrocarbons.

Conclusion: The π-Sombor index exhibits chemical applicability, extending its utility beyond the purely mathematical domain into physicochemical characterization.

Abstract: The Sombor index ($SO$) and the modified Sombor index (${}^m SO$) are two closely related vertex-degree-based graph invariants. Both were introduced in the early 2020s and have found diverse applications in chemical, physicochemical, and network-theoretical studies. In 2023, the $π$-Sombor index was introduced as the product $SO \cdot {}^m SO$, with an initial focus on its fundamental mathematical properties. However, its potential chemical applicability has remained unexplored. In the present work, we address this gap by employing regression models to analyze the $π$-Sombor index in 22 benzenoid hydrocarbons. This study investigates the chemical significance of the $π$-Sombor index through curvilinear regression analysis against the physicochemical properties of these hydrocarbons.

</details>


### [39] [Improving Jet A-1 Thermal-Oxidative Stability through Selective Removal of Unwanted Trace Species via 3.7 Å Chabazite Filtration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03375)
*Morteza Roostaeinia,Ehsan Alborzi,Xue Yong,Kyungwha Park,Vsevolod Ivanov*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Using DFT and AIMD, the authors evaluate how chabazite zeolite composition (Si/Al ratio and bimetallic Co/Mg/Zn substitution) affects selective adsorption of Jet A-1 fuel promoters vs. antioxidant BHT, showing that bimetallic doping offers chemically selective behavior while acidity-only tuning incurs an intrinsic selectivity penalty, and that adsorption energy alone is insufficient due to molecular mobility effects at 400 K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Jet A-1 fuel thermal stability is compromised by trace heteroatomic species that form thermal oxidative deposits, while antioxidant additives like BHT help preserve stability. Chabazite adsorbents can remove deposit promoters but may also remove beneficial antioxidants. The trade-off between promoter removal and antioxidant preservation needs systematic optimization.

Method: Density functional theory (DFT) and ab initio molecular dynamics (AIMD) at 400 K were used to evaluate two compositional descriptors: framework acidity (Si/Al = 35–8) and bimetallic substitution (Co, Mg, Zn) at fixed Al content. Adsorption energies, molecular mobility, density of states, and wavefunction analysis were performed.

Result: Lowering Si/Al strengthens adsorption of all species (including BHT), confirming an intrinsic selectivity penalty for acidity-only tuning. Bimetallic substitution introduces chemically selective behavior: specific promoter uptake is strengthened while antioxidant adsorption is either suppressed or enhanced depending on dopant identity. AIMD shows adsorption energy alone is insufficient because molecular mobility inside chabazite influences residence time and effective capture at 400 K. DOS and wavefunction signatures confirm dopant-dependent adsorption shifts.

Conclusion: A unified adsorption–transport–electronic screening framework is established for selecting chabazite compositions that remove deposit promoters while preserving antioxidant functionality in Jet A-1 fuel treatment.

Abstract: The thermal stability of Jet A-1 fuel is strongly affected by trace heteroatomic species that promote thermal oxidative deposit formation, as well as antioxidant additives such as butylated hydroxytoluene, which preserve fuel stability. 3.7 Å chabazite is a tunable microporous adsorbent, but optimizing its composition requires balancing promoter removal against antioxidant loss. Here, we use density functional theory and \textit{ab initio} molecular dynamics (AIMD) to evaluate this trade-off using two compositional descriptors: framework acidity (Si/Al $= 35$--$8$) and bimetallic substitution (Co, Mg, Zn) at fixed Al content. Lowering Si/Al strengthens adsorption of all species, including BHT, confirming an intrinsic selectivity penalty for acidity-only tuning. In contrast, bimetallic substitution introduces chemically selective behavior, strengthening uptake of specific promoters while either suppressing or enhancing antioxidant adsorption depending on dopant identity. AIMD simulations at 400 K further show that adsorption energy alone is insufficient to describe calculated trends, because molecular mobility inside chabazite can influence residence time and effective capture during fuel treatment. Analysis of the density of states and the participating wavefunctions reveals signatures that are consistent with dopant-dependent adsorption shifts. These results establish a unified adsorption--transport--electronic screening framework for selecting chabazite compositions that remove deposit promoters while preserving antioxidant functionality in Jet A-1 treatment.

</details>


### [40] [Spectroscopic photorelaxation signatures in pyrazine from nonadiabatic dynamics simulations with coupled cluster theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03395)
*Sara Angelico,Eirik F. Kjønstad,Yi-Ping Chang,O. Jonathan Fajen,Todd J. Martínez,Henrik Koch*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents high-level simulations of pyrazine's ultrafast excited-state dynamics using coupled cluster theory and ab initio multiple spawning, achieving quantitative agreement with experimental photoelectron spectra and qualitative agreement with X-ray absorption spectra, while providing new insights into the three-state relaxation mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The ultrafast relaxation mechanisms of photoexcited pyrazine remain poorly understood despite extensive study, and there is a lack of clear agreement between experimental results and theoretical predictions.

Method: The authors used coupled cluster theory with single and double excitations (CCSD) combined with ab initio multiple spawning (AIMS) dynamics, enabled by a newly developed multistate implementation of similarity constrained coupled cluster theory. They simulated time-resolved photoelectron spectra and X-ray absorption spectra at nitrogen and carbon edges.

Result: Quantitative agreement with experimental photoelectron spectra for the ¹B₂ᵤ state decay was achieved, along with qualitative agreement with available experimental X-ray absorption spectra. Detailed spectroscopic signatures were identified that should be experimentally verifiable with sufficient time/frequency resolution.

Conclusion: The three-state mechanism (¹B₂ᵤ → ¹B₃ᵤ and ¹Aᵤ) is confirmed, with the simulations providing deeper insight into the state interplay during photorelaxation than previous theoretical studies.

Abstract: Despite extensive theoretical and experimental efforts, the mechanisms underlying the ultrafast relaxation of pyrazine after photoexcitation remain challenging to disentangle. Recently, theoretical investigations have been converging towards a three-state mechanism, with an ultrafast decay of the bright ${}^{1}B_{2u}$ state followed by beats in the populations of the low-lying ${}^{1}B_{3u}$ and ${}^{1}A_u$ states. However, a clear agreement between the experimental results and the corresponding theoretical predictions remains elusive. Here, we present a high-level simulation of the ultrafast excited states dynamics of pyrazine using coupled cluster theory with single and double excitations and ab initio multiple spawning, together with predictions of the time-resolved photoelectron spectrum and X-ray absorption spectra at the nitrogen and carbon edges. This is made possible by using a newly developed multistate implementation of similarity constrained coupled cluster theory. We find quantitative agreement with the experimental signature of the ${}^{1}B_{2u}$ decay in the photoelectron spectrum, and qualitative agreement with the available experimental X-ray absorption spectra. Moreover, we detail spectroscopic signatures that should be verifiable in experiments with sufficient resolution in the time and frequency domains. Compared to previous theoretical studies, we provide further detailed insight into the interplay of the states involved in the photorelaxation.

</details>


### [41] [Photo-thermal 2D spectroscopy: a different type of action](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03469)
*Pavel Malý,Xiaoji G. Xu,Tomáš Mančal*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a unified theoretical framework for population- and heat-based 2D spectroscopy, showing that photothermal detection (AFM-2DIR, PT-2DES) produces spectra resembling conventional 2D spectroscopy while offering advantages of action detection, unlike population-based methods (TL-2DIR, F-2DES) which reflect linear response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To unify the theoretical understanding of emerging action-detection-based 2D spectroscopic techniques (population-based vs. heat-based) and highlight their complementary features, particularly for new photothermal detection methods like AFM-2DIR and proposed PT-2DES.

Method: Development of a unified theoretical framework for population- and heat-based 2D spectroscopy; comparison of TL-2DIR, AFM-2DIR, F-2DES, and proposed PT-2DES using this framework; reproduction of experimental AFM-2DIR features via modeling.

Result: TL-2DIR reflects the system linear response, while AFM-2DIR produces spectra resembling conventional 2DIR with sensitivity to anharmonicity and mode coupling. The model reproduces key experimental AFM-2DIR features, confirming a highly nonlinear measurement regime. PT-2DES closely resembles conventional 2DES while retaining action detection advantages.

Conclusion: Photothermal detection (AFM-2DIR, PT-2DES) is a promising approach that yields spectra analogous to conventional 2D spectroscopy with the practical benefits of action detection, distinct from population-based action detection methods that only capture linear response.

Abstract: Advances in multidimensional spectroscopy have seen the rise of action detection, where coherent response is encoded into incoherent signals. These are typically proportional to the excited-state population, an example in 2D electronic spectroscopy (2DES) is fluorescence-detected 2DES (F-2DES), and in 2D infrared (2DIR) tag-loss 2DIR (TL-2DIR). Very recently, a new type of photo-thermal action signal has been introduced in 2DIR, detecting the generated heat by atomic force microscopy-based 2DIR (AFM-2DIR). We present a unified theoretical framework for population- and heat-based 2D spectroscopy, highlighting their complementary features. In the infrared, TL-2DIR reflects the system linear response, whereas AFM-2DIR produces spectra resembling conventional 2DIR, with sensitivity to anharmonicity and mode coupling. Our model reproduces key experimental AFM-2DIR features, confirming measurement in a highly nonlinear regime. Extending photothermal detection to electronic spectroscopy, we compare F-2DES with proposed photo-thermal 2DES (PT-2DES). PT-2DES closely resembles conventional 2DES, while enjoying the advantages of action detection.

</details>


### [42] [AquaGen: Scaling generative models to molecular dynamics precision on thousands of atoms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03513)
*Emmanuel Bengio,Sanjeev Raja,Yui Tik Pang,Kerstin Klaeser,Cristian Gabellini,Nikhil Shenoy,Francesco Di Giovanni,Prudencio Tossou*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: AquaGen is the first all-atom, explicit solvent, periodic-boundary-condition-aware generative model that produces Boltzmann-distributed molecular configurations faster than MD, demonstrated on absolute hydration free energy (AHFE) estimation with 4-10x speedup and comparable accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative models for molecular configurations operate on coarse-grained, vacuum, or implicit solvent systems, removing degrees of freedom. The authors aim to build a high-resolution (all-atom, explicit solvent, PBC-aware) generative model that preserves full atomic detail for post-processing and property prediction.

Method: AquaGen generates uncorrelated samples from alchemical Boltzmann distributions using an all-atom, explicit solvent, periodic-boundary-condition-aware generative model. The generated samples are post-processed via force field energy evaluations to compute ensemble averages for property estimation (gray-box paradigm).

Result: AHFE estimates are produced 4-10x faster than standard GPU-based MD with comparable accuracy. The approach yields calibrated uncertainty estimates, predictable scaling benefits from increasing model size and sample count, and more interpretable ensemble predictions than black-box regression methods.

Conclusion: High-resolution ensemble generation is useful for free energy estimation and has future potential to replace MD in tasks like predicting lipophilicity, membrane permeability, or absolute binding free energy (ABFE), which are critical for drug and materials development.

Abstract: We present AquaGen, the first all-atom, explicit solvent, periodic-boundary-condition-aware generative model that produces molecular configurations from the Boltzmann distribution at a fraction of the cost of molecular dynamics (MD). This is in contrast with existing generative models that remove degrees of freedom by operating on coarse-grained, vacuum, or implicit solvent systems. Operating at this resolution allows for post-processing through force field energy evaluations and MD simulations, and enables the prediction of relevant properties in a gray-box manner (as ensemble averages of potential energy evaluations over generated samples). We demonstrate the utility of this paradigm on absolute hydration free energy (AHFE), producing estimates 4-10x faster and with comparable accuracy to standard GPU-based MD. By generating uncorrelated samples from alchemical Boltzmann distributions, we create more accurate, interpretable, and refinable ensemble predictions with calibrated uncertainty estimates, unlike regression methods which are entirely black-box predictors. Our approach also yields predictable benefits from increasing train- and test-time compute, realized by scaling model size and generating more samples, respectively. We believe that this approach demonstrates the utility of high-resolution ensemble generation for free energy estimation, with future potential to replace MD in tasks such as the prediction of lipophilicity, membrane permeability, or absolute binding free energy (ABFE) -- whose grounding and interpretability may be critical for the development of new drugs and materials.

</details>


### [43] [Intrinsic Matching Frustration in Fluctuating Finite Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03549)
*Leonid Rubinovich,Micha Polak*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper identifies Intrinsic Matching Frustration (IMF) as a universal statistical constraint in finite systems where equilibrium fluctuations reduce the number of realizable matching events between interacting constituents, since matching is determined by the smaller of fluctuating populations rather than their averages. IMF generates spectator particles at equilibrium, suppresses matching-dependent processes, and exhibits universal scaling behavior governed by the variance of population imbalance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formalize the previously unrecognized direct impact of equilibrium fluctuations on processes requiring simultaneous matching of discrete populations, showing that fluctuations systematically reduce matching events beyond what mean-field descriptions predict.

Method: The paper formalizes IMF as a universal statistical constraint, derives an exact solution for independent Poisson populations using the Skellam distribution, and extends the analysis to interacting/correlated systems where mismatch is determined by the variance of population imbalance with universal scaling.

Result: IMF generates a finite population of spectator particles even at equilibrium, suppresses matching-dependent processes relative to mean-population expectations, and for sufficiently large populations exhibits universal scaling behavior independent of microscopic rates and interaction mechanisms.

Conclusion: IMF is a generic equilibrium property of finite systems providing a universal fluctuation-induced limitation on pairwise association, with implications for chemical reactions, molecular binding, nanoscale quantum systems, and biological environments, complementing existing descriptions of finite-size and fluctuation effects.

Abstract: We show that matching between interacting constituents is intrinsically incomplete in finite systems due to equilibrium fluctuations. While fluctuations in finite populations are a well-established feature of statistical mechanics, their direct impact on processes requiring simultaneous matching has not been explicitly formulated. Because matching is determined by the smaller of fluctuating populations rather than by their average values, equilibrium fluctuations systematically reduce the number of realizable matching events. We formalize this effect as intrinsic matching frustration (IMF), a universal statistical constraint that arises whenever discrete fluctuating populations must be paired. IMF generates a finite population of intact ("spectator") particles even at equilibrium and suppresses matching-dependent processes relative to expectations based on mean populations. For independent Poisson populations, IMF admits an exact solution based on the Skellam distribution, valid for all particle numbers. More generally, interactions and correlations may modify the detailed fluctuation statistics, but for sufficiently large populations the mismatch is determined solely by the variance of the population imbalance and exhibits universal scaling behavior. IMF therefore represents a generic equilibrium property of finite systems, independent of microscopic rates and interaction mechanisms. The effect provides a universal fluctuation-induced limitation on pairwise association, binding, and matching processes, with implications for chemical reactions, molecular binding, nanoscale quantum systems, and biological environments. By identifying a fundamental fluctuation-induced constraint on matching, IMF complements established descriptions of finite-size and equilibrium fluctuation effects in statistical mechanics.

</details>


### [44] [Single-Scan Characterization of $^{14}$N Nuclei via $^1$H-Detected Rotating-Frame Relaxometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03796)
*Florin Teleanu,Huijing Zou,David E. Korenchan,Alexej Jerschow*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a method to indirectly probe ¹⁴N NMR signals via scalar relaxation of the second kind in double-resonance spin-locking experiments, enabling characterization of ¹H-¹⁴N scalar couplings, ¹⁴N quadrupolar interactions, and chemical shifts through enhanced rotating-frame ¹H relaxation rates, demonstrated on biomolecular systems with over an order of magnitude relaxation enhancement and sensitivity to hydrogen bonding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ¹⁴N NMR is extremely challenging in liquids due to fast spin relaxation and large quadrupolar couplings, rendering most signals undetectable even after extensive signal averaging. The authors aim to develop an indirect method to access nitrogen NMR parameters via proton detection.

Method: The method reintroduces scalar relaxation of the second kind contribution to ¹H polarization lifetimes in double-resonance spin-locking experiments. Enhanced ¹H rotating-frame relaxation rates are used to extract ¹⁴N chemical shifts, polarization lifetimes, one- and two-bond ¹H-¹⁴N scalar couplings, and ¹⁴N quadrupolar interactions. The approach is integrated into ¹H-detected relaxation-editing sequences with water suppression.

Result: Demonstrated on several biologically important molecules showing proton relaxation enhancements exceeding one order of magnitude. A pronounced effect from intermolecular hydrogen bonding was observed. The method provides access to nitrogen's picosecond-modulated quadrupolar interaction via single-scan proton detection in systems yielding almost no detectable direct ¹⁴N signal even after thousands of transients.

Conclusion: This indirect approach can be readily integrated into existing biomolecular NMR methodologies, offering a practical solution for studying ¹⁴N nuclei in biologically relevant systems where direct detection is infeasible.

Abstract: $^{14}$N NMR is notoriously difficult to perform in liquids due to the very fast spin relaxation and the large quadrupolar couplings, which render many signals invisible. We show here how $^{14}$N nuclei of biomolecular constituents can be probed indirectly by reintroducing the scalar relaxation of the second kind contribution to the polarization lifetimes of J-coupled protons in double resonance spin-locking experiments. The enhanced $^1$H relaxation rates in the rotating-frame allow for direct evaluation of nitrogen chemical shift and polarization lifetimes, from which one- and even two-bond $^1$H-$^{14}$N scalar couplings as well as $^{14}$N quadrupolar interactions can be determined. We demonstrate the versatility of this method by characterizing $^1$H-$^{14}$N spin pairs in several molecules of biological importance, showing proton relaxation enhancements beyond one order of magnitude. We further observe a pronounced effect from intermolecular hydrogen bonding. Our approach can be readily integrated into existing biomolecular NMR methodologies, as demonstrated here for $^1$H-detected relaxation-editing experiments with water suppression. This method provides access to nitrogen's picosecond-modulated quadrupolar interaction via single-scan proton detection in systems that would otherwise yield almost no detectable direct $^{14}$N signal even after averaging over thousands of transients.

</details>


### [45] [Application of SAP-X2C to Spectroscopy and Comparison to Screened Nuclear Spin--Orbit Approximations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03814)
*Yannick J. Franzke,Christof Holzer*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper generalizes the SAP-X2C method (superposition of atomic potentials to correct two-electron picture-change error in one-electron exact two-component theory) to analytical derivative theory and applies it to molecular spectroscopy (NMR, EPR, Mössbauer, UV/vis, X-ray absorption). SAP-X2C performs excellently, comparable to SNSO-X2C, but with a well-defined thermochemical limit and less empirical nature, making it the preferred default for DFT-based spectroscopy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the SAP-X2C ansatz to analytical derivative theory and assess its accuracy for various molecular spectroscopic properties, aiming to provide a simple, accurate, and efficient correction for two-electron picture-change error in X2C methods.

Method: Generalization of SAP-X2C to analytical derivative theory; comparison with four-component reference results and SNSO-X2C across NMR, EPR, Mössbauer, UV/vis, and X-ray absorption spectroscopy.

Result: Both SNSO-X2C and SAP-X2C perform excellently for spectroscopic properties. SAP-X2C's key advantage is its well-defined thermochemical limit and less empirical nature.

Conclusion: SAP-X2C is expected to become the default choice for mitigating two-electron picture-change error in DFT spectroscopy due to its accuracy, simplicity, and efficiency. More complicated atomic mean-field approaches remain relevant for high-level correlated methods and highly accurate thermochemistry.

Abstract: Recently, Surjuse and Valeev [\textit{J. Chem. Theory Comput.} \textbf{22}, 3443--3452 (2026).] suggested to use a simple superposition of atomic potentials to account for two-electron picture-change error in one-electron exact two-component (SAP-X2C) theory. Herein, we generalize this ansatz to analytical derivative theory and apply SAP-X2C to molecular spectroscopy. The accuracy is assessed for NMR, EPR, Mössbauer, UV/vis, as well as X-ray absorption spectroscopy. A thorough comparison with four-component results and the even simpler screened nuclear spin--orbit (SNSO) approximation reveals that both SNSO-X2C and SAP-X2C perform excellently for spectroscopic properties. The major advantage of SAP-X2C over SNSO-X2C is consequently its well defined thermochemical limit and its less empirical nature. Therefore, SAP-X2C may be expected to become the default choice to mitigate the two-electron picture-change error in density functional theory approaches for spectroscopy thanks to its accuracy, simplicity, and efficiency. More complicated approaches to account for this error based on atomic mean-field ansätze may still be relevant for high-level correlated methods and highly accurate thermochemistry.

</details>


### [46] [ML and AI for density functional theory: different priorities for Kohn-Sham and orbital-free DFT, for electronic and nuclear DFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04095)
*Xin-Hui Wu,Sergei Manzhos*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper compares ML/AI approaches for electronic and nuclear DFT, analyzing computational bottlenecks and accuracy requirements for learning exchange-correlation functionals, kinetic energy functionals, densities, and basis functions. It contrasts deep neural networks (suitable for KS DFT) with symbolic regression (advantageous for KEFs), and identifies promising cross-domain transfer opportunities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify similarities and differences in computational bottlenecks and accuracy requirements between electronic and nuclear DFT, enabling informed choices of ML/AI techniques for learning different components of DFT.

Method: Comparative analysis of ML/AI techniques (deep neural networks vs. symbolic regression) applied to learning four DFT components: exchange-correlation functionals, kinetic energy functionals, densities, and basis functions, across both electronic and nuclear DFT domains.

Result: Deep neural networks are potent for KS DFT but have disadvantages for building kinetic energy functionals; symbolic regression shows conceptual advantages for KEFs in both electronic and nuclear DFT, though not yet fully realized. Several promising approaches from ML-enhanced electronic DFT can be transferred to nuclear DFT.

Conclusion: Methodological and algorithmic choices for ML in DFT should be tailored to the specific component being learned, with symbolic regression being a particularly promising but underexplored direction for kinetic energy functionals in both domains.

Abstract: We overview similarities and, importantly, differences in computational bottlenecks and accuracy requirements that can be addressed with machine learning (ML) and artificial intelligence (AI) techniques in electronic and nuclear DFT. From these follow different promising methodological and algorithmic choices depending on whether one machine learns the exchange correlation (XC) functional, the kinetic energy functional (KEF), the density or the basis functions. In particular, while the popular deep neural networks remain a potent choice in the context of KS DFT, we highlight their disadvantages when building KEFs and highlight conceptual advantages - yet to be fully realized - of symbolic regression for both electronic and nuclear DFT. We point out promising approaches that can be carried from the more extensively investigated ML-enhanced electronic DFT to nuclear DFT.

</details>


### [47] [Differentiable OPLS Force Field Parameterization for Ionic Electrolytes and High-Throughput Application to Lithium-ion Batteries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04633)
*Haichao Huang,Zilin Chen,Qi Liu,Tianqi Zhao,Yunpei Liu,Guotao Qiu,Jianhui Chen,Zhen Li,Wenshuo Liang,Minsung Cho,Manxue Zhang,Feiyu Kang,Xiaolong Zou,Yidan Cao,Xushan Zhao,Ziqi Cheng,Ye Mei*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops an automated differentiable OPLS-AA force field parameterization workflow for ionic electrolytes, enabling high-throughput MD simulations of over 10,000 formulations (67 solvents, 15 lithium salts) to generate a comprehensive dataset of five core properties (density, dielectric constant, viscosity, diffusion coefficient, ionic conductivity) for rational design of LIB electrolytes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rational design of ionic electrolytes for LIBs is constrained by vast solvent-salt combinatorial space and inefficient empirical trial-and-error; classical force fields lack sufficient accuracy for multicomponent systems.

Method: Developed an automated differentiable OPLS-AA force field parameterization workflow with topology-guided atom typification to reduce redundancy, optimized Lennard-Jones parameters via DMFF using experimental density as fitting target, validated by ionic conductivity; conducted high-throughput MD simulations (~100,000-atom systems, 35-40 ns NVT runs) on the Tianqiong platform across 10,000+ formulations.

Result: Generated a comprehensive dataset covering five core properties (density, dielectric constant, viscosity, diffusion coefficient, ionic conductivity); t-SNE visualization revealed partial clustering by salt chemistry, continuous property gradients with concentration/temperature, internal physical self-consistency, and solvent composition as a key performance regulator.

Conclusion: The accurate transferable force field and large-scale dataset provide a solid foundation for data-driven rational design of ionic electrolytes.

Abstract: The rational design of ionic electrolytes for lithium-ion batteries (LIBs) is severely constrained by the vast solvent-salt combinatorial space and low efficiency of empirical trial-and-error. While molecular dynamics (MD) bridges microscopic solvation structures and macroscopic physicochemical properties, classical force fields often lack sufficient accuracy for multicomponent systems. To address these challenges, we develop an automated differentiable OPLS-AA force field parameterization workflow tailored for general ionic electrolytes. It employs topology-guided atom typification to reduce parameter redundancy and optimizes Lennard-Jones parameters via the DMFF framework, with experimental density as the fitting target and ionic conductivity as an independent validation metric. Rigorous convergence tests yield a standardized simulation protocol with $\sim$100,000-atom systems and 35-40 ns NVT runs to ensure reliable transport property quantification. High-throughput MD simulations of over 10,000 formulations spanning 67 solvents and 15 lithium salts are conducted on the Tianqiong platform, generating a comprehensive dataset covering five core properties: density, dielectric constant, viscosity, diffusion coefficient, and ionic conductivity. t-SNE visualization reveals partial clustering of distinct salt chemistries, continuous property gradients with concentration and temperature, and internal physical self-consistency, with solvent composition identified as another key performance regulator. Together, the accurate transferable force field and large-scale dataset provide a solid foundation for data-driven rational design of ionic electrolytes.

</details>


### [48] [Speed limits on biomolecular processes from fundamental physical constants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04549)
*Dmitrii E Makarov*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper examines quantum-mechanical speed limits on biological timescales, including chemical reaction rates and biomolecular folding, while considering environmental constraints bounded by anthropic principles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the fundamental quantum-mechanical constraints that set speed limits on biological processes, and how these limits interact with anthropically-bounded environmental factors like temperature.

Method: The paper reviews and analyzes quantum-mechanical constraints on biological timescales, specifically examining elementary chemical reaction rates and biomolecular folding speeds, while also evaluating the limitations of simplistic back-of-envelope calculations.

Result: Identifies quantum-mechanical speed limits on biological processes and shows that simple estimates have inherent limitations when applied to complex biological systems.

Conclusion: Biological timescales are fundamentally bounded by quantum-mechanical constraints, though environmental factors like temperature (constrained by anthropic considerations) also play a role; simple approximations may not capture the full complexity.

Abstract: Many of the timescales of life have speed limits set by quantum-mechanical constraints along with non-fundamental quantities, such as the temperature of the environment, which are however bounded by anthropic considerations. Here, some of such speed limits are examined, including those for the rates of elementary chemical reactions and biomolecular folding. Limitations of simple back-of-envelope estimates are also discussed.

</details>


### [49] [Hidden Gauge Freedom in Complex-Pole Hierarchical Equations of Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04834)
*Tianchu Li,Andrés Montoya-Castillo*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper identifies a gauge freedom in complex-pole HEOM that causes numerical instabilities, and introduces GO-HEOM to eliminate spectral divergences and non-normal error amplification, enabling stable simulations of strongly coupled and non-Markovian open quantum systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex-pole HEOM suffer from numerical instabilities due to the non-Hermitian structure of their Liouvillian, but the origin of this structure was previously unknown, limiting simulations in strong coupling and non-Markovian regimes.

Method: The authors discovered a continuous gauge freedom in complex-pole HEOM that yields analytically equivalent but numerically distinct Liouvillians. By optimizing this gauge, they developed GO-HEOM, which decouples spectral divergence and non-normal error amplification as independent instability mechanisms.

Result: GO-HEOM eliminates divergences in strongly coupled Brownian oscillator environments and extends numerically exact simulations of sub-Ohmic dynamics through the quantum phase transition to previously inaccessible coupling strengths.

Conclusion: Because the gauge transformation is independent of the bath-correlation decomposition scheme, GO-HEOM is a general, broadly compatible strategy for accessing numerically exact quantum dynamics over arbitrary coupling and highly non-Markovian regimes.

Abstract: While complex-pole hierarchical equations of motion (HEOM) have dramatically expanded the reach of numerically exact quantum dynamics simulations of open quantum systems, they suffer from numerical instabilities rooted in the non-Hermitian structure of their Liouvillian. Yet, the origin of this structure remains obscure. Here, we report a previously unknown gauge freedom in complex-pole HEOM: a continuous family of analytically equivalent Liouvillians, all encoding the same bath correlation function, whose numerical properties vary dramatically. This gauge controls both the eigenspectrum and non-normality of the hierarchy generator, revealing spectral divergence and non-normal error amplification as two distinct instability mechanisms. By optimizing this gauge, we introduce GO--HEOM, which eliminates divergences in strongly coupled Brownian oscillator environments and extends numerically exact simulations of sub-Ohmic dynamics -- including through the delocalized-to-localized quantum phase transition -- to previously inaccessible coupling strengths. Because this gauge transformation is independent of the bath-correlation decomposition scheme, our GO--HEOM becomes a general, broadly compatible strategy for accessing numerically exact quantum dynamics of open quantum systems over arbitrary coupling and highly non-Markovian regimes.

</details>


### [50] [Combinatorial Compression in Nucleobase Self-Assembly Governed by Seeding Kinetics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04841)
*Yael Kapon,Rotem Edri,Moran Frenkel-Pinter*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Supersaturated mixtures of adenine, thymine, and uracil crystallize more slowly than binary mixtures due to kinetic competition among structurally similar hydrogen-bonding partners. Preformed A+T seeds rescue ordered growth by reducing lag time, increasing nucleation rate, and redirecting composition toward the A+T pathway. This "kinetic compression" mechanism shows how seeded crystallization can select specific assembly trajectories from complex molecular mixtures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how kinetic competition among structurally similar molecules in chemically complex mixtures delays crystallization, and to investigate whether preformed crystalline seeds can overcome this frustration by selecting specific assembly pathways.

Method: Time-resolved turbidity measurements of supersaturated aqueous mixtures of A, T, and U in binary and ternary combinations; HPLC analysis of solid products; seeding experiments with preformed A+T crystals.

Result: Ternary A+T+U mixtures crystallize more slowly and less efficiently than binary systems. Seeding with A+T crystals sharply reduces lag time, increases nucleation/onset rate, and redirects solid product toward the A+T crystalline pathway.

Conclusion: Seeded crystallization acts as a mechanism of "kinetic compression"—reducing accessible supramolecular assembly pathways by amplifying selected productive growth routes—providing a physical route for ordered molecular subsets to emerge from chemically complex environments.

Abstract: Crystallization from chemically complex mixtures is governed not only by thermodynamic stability but also by kinetic competition among structurally related components during nucleation and growth. When multiple compatible molecules compete for incorporation into early nuclei, the number of possible local recognition motifs can expand, delaying access to productive crystalline pathways. Here, we investigate this effect using supersaturated aqueous mixtures of the canonical nucleobases adenine (A), thymine (T), and uracil (U) as a minimal model for competitive supramolecular crystallization. Time-resolved turbidity measurements showed that ternary A+T+U mixtures crystallize more slowly and less efficiently than selected binary systems, despite containing individually crystallizable components. This inhibition is consistent with competition-induced kinetic frustration, where structurally similar hydrogen-bonding partners compete during early nucleation, delaying formation of a productive crystalline nucleus. We then show that preformed A+T crystalline seeds rescue ordered growth from the ternary mixture. Seeding sharply reduces the lag time, increases the apparent nucleation/onset rate, and redirects the solid product toward the A+T crystalline pathway, as confirmed by HPLC. Thus, the seed acts not only as a nucleation accelerator but as a selector of molecular composition and assembly trajectory within a multicomponent solution. We describe this process as kinetic compression: the reduction of accessible supramolecular assembly pathways through amplifications of selected productive growth pathways. These findings establish seeded crystallization as a mechanism for selecting compositional pathways in competitive molecular mixtures and suggest a physical route by which ordered molecular subsets can emerge from chemically complex environments.

</details>


### [51] [Anomalous Peak Formation in the Second Stability Zone of Quadrupole Mass Filters: Role of Non-linear Resonances Induced by Single Rod Defects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04867)
*Archisman Sinha,Pintu Mandal,Nabanita Deb*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes how a localized, asymmetric defect on a single rod in a quadrupole mass filter (QMF) operating in the second stability zone breaks symmetry, introduces odd-parity hexapole fields, and drives destructive nonlinear secular resonances that degrade performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the previously unaddressed electrodynamic impact of a completely localized, asymmetric defect on a single rod in a QMF operating in its second stability zone, where extreme sensitivity to structural imperfections exists.

Method: Combined analytical modeling with SIMION trajectory simulations; spatial multipole expansion; explicit zone-II Floquet projection mappings to track secular indices.

Result: Single-rod defects break remaining symmetry, producing pronounced transmission ridges within the second stability zone. Odd-parity harmonics (dominated by hexapole A3 field) couple orthogonal transverse equations of motion, driving destructive higher-order nonlinear secular resonances that cause rapid ion amplitude growth and electrode collisions.

Conclusion: Asymmetric single-rod defects establish critical geometric tolerance frameworks and operating conditions for high-performance mass spectrometry in the second stability zone.

Abstract: Operating a quadrupole mass filter (QMF) within its second stability zone offers superior mass resolving power but introduces extreme sensitivity to structural imperfections. While symmetric misalignments are well-documented, this work combines analytical modeling with SIMION trajectory simulations to investigate the unaddressed electrodynamic impact of a completely localized, asymmetric defect on a single rod. Unlike diagonally symmetric perturbations, which reduce the four-fold rotational symmetry to two-fold symmetry while maintaining a smooth stability landscape, a single-rod defect breaks the remaining symmetry, giving rise to pronounced transmission ridges within the second stability zone. Spatial multipole expansion reveals that this structural breakdown injects odd-parity harmonics -- dominated by the hexapole A3 field -- which couple the orthogonal transverse equations of motion. By tracking secular indices through explicit zone-II Floquet projection mappings, we demonstrate that these asymmetric fields drive destructive, higher-order nonlinear secular resonances. Ions traversing these precise parametric coordinates undergo rapid amplitude growth and collide with adjacent electrodes, establishing critical geometric tolerance frameworks and operating conditions for high-performance mass spectrometry.

</details>


### [52] [Physically-Relevant Information Learning in High-Dimensional Time-Derivatives Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05127)
*Domiziano Doria,Matteo Becchi,Giovanni M. Pavan*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Time-Derivatives (TiDe) space, a high-dimensional framework built from time-series data where dimensions represent successive time-derivatives. This space enables joint learning of structure and dynamics in complex dynamical systems without prior dimensionality reduction, demonstrated on molecular dynamics and experimental tracking data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional high-dimensional analysis of complex dynamical systems often relies on order parameters or descriptors capturing structural information (e.g., relative positions, symmetries), but this alone may be insufficient. A more complete physical understanding requires correlating both structure and dynamics simultaneously.

Method: The method constructs a TiDe space from time-series data, where each dimension corresponds to a growing-order time-derivative of the extracted data. This space can be generated for virtually any system and analyzed directly via unsupervised approaches without prior dimensionality reduction.

Result: Analysis of two prototypical datasets from molecular dynamics simulations and experimental tracking demonstrates that TiDe spaces enable efficient navigation and learning of complex dynamical systems, providing intuitively interpretable results.

Conclusion: TiDe spaces offer a robust, general framework for data analysis and studying complex dynamical systems, allowing one to efficiently extract physical information by jointly leveraging structural and dynamical information from observed time-series data.

Abstract: Understanding the physics of many-body complex dynamical systems is typically non-trivial. High-dimensional analysis approaches are often deemed necessary to prevent losing important information. Typically, these use order parameters or descriptors capturing information related to, e.g., relative positions, symmetries, etc., of the units in the studied system. However, in many cases, gaining information related to the relative positions (or velocities) of the constitutive units alone may be insufficient, and to reach a more complete physical knowledge, one should ideally learn and correlate with each other both structure and dynamics. Here we demonstrate how to efficiently achieve such a goal by building and navigating high-dimensional Time-Derivatives (TiDe) space. A TiDe space can be easily generated for virtually any type of system/phenomenon under study from the time-series data collected along its observation over time. Each TiDe's dimension corresponds to a growing-order time-derivative of the extracted data, thus containing information related to different types of physical phenomena/events that can be easily extracted via unsupervised approaches. We demonstrate how, by definition, TiDes can be directly analyzed without a need for prior dimensionality reduction, providing results that are intrinsically intuitive to interpret. We show the potential of the method by analyzing two prototypical example datasets extracted from molecular dynamics simulations or experimental tracking of different complex dynamical systems. Our results demonstrate how efficiently one can navigate and learn in such information-rich TiDe spaces, which provide robust general frameworks for data analysis and for studying complex dynamical systems from the data collected along their observation over time.

</details>


### [53] [cQED-iCIPT2: A Near-Exact Method for Polaritonic Chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05192)
*Ning Zhang,Wenjian Liu*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops cQED-iCIPT2, a near-exact ab initio method for cavity quantum electrodynamics that treats electrons and photons equally. It introduces two variants (photon-number and coherent-state representations) with a graded configuration space for efficiency, and demonstrates accurate polaritonic chemistry results across multiple molecular systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strong light-matter coupling in optical cavities offers a versatile platform for modulating chemical structure, reactivity, and spectroscopy, motivating the development of ab initio cQED methods that treat electronic and photonic degrees of freedom on an equal footing.

Method: Two cQED-iCIPT2 variants are proposed: cQED-PN-iCIPT2 (photon-number representation) and cQED-CS-iCIPT2 (coherent-state transformation for origin invariance). A graded configuration space organized by photon numbers is introduced, decomposing selection, diagonalization, and perturbation correction into intra- and inter-subspace steps to reuse existing MetaWave infrastructure. The selection step automatically determines the optimal photon number without a priori truncation.

Result: Tested on N₂ dissociation, ethylene torsion, proton transfer in malonaldehyde and aminopropenal, and polyacene excited states. The method provides numerically accurate reference data and reveals how cavities fine-tune reaction barriers, alter potential energy surfaces, and induce state crossings.

Conclusion: This work establishes a robust near-exact framework for polaritonic chemistry under both strong electron correlation and strong light-matter coupling.

Abstract: Strong light-matter coupling in optical cavities provides a versatile platform for modulating chemical structure, reactivity, and spectroscopy, and hence motivates the development of ab initio cavity quantum electrodynamics (cQED) methods that can treat the electronic and photonic degrees of freedom on an equal footing. We present such a method, cQED-iCIPT2, by combining the near-exact iCIPT2 (iterative configuration interaction with selection and second-order perturbation theory) with the cQED Hamiltonian in two ways, cQED-PN-iCIPT2 and cQED-CS-iCIPT2. The former works directly in the photon-number representation, whereas the latter employs a coherent-state transformation that restores origin invariance of the cQED Hamiltonian and avoids artificially strong coupling in charged systems. To efficiently handle the tensor-product structure of the electron-photon wavefunction, we introduce a graded configuration space organized by photon numbers and decompose the key computational steps (selection, diagonalization, and perturbation correction) into intra- and inter-subspace steps, so as to maximize the reuse of the existing infrastructure in the MetaWave platform (J. Phys. Chem. A 2025, 129, 5170). The selection step enables automatic determination of the optimal number of photons without a priori truncation. The efficacy of the methods is showcased dissociation of N2, torsion of ethylene, proton transfer reactions in malonaldehyde and aminopropenal, and low-lying excited states of polyacenes. The results provide numerically accurate reference data and meanwhile reveal how the cavity can fine-tune reaction barriers, alter potential energy surfaces, and induce state crossings. As such, this work establishes a robust near-exact framework for polaritonic chemistry in the presence of both strong electron correlation and strong light-matter coupling.

</details>
