### [78] [Measurement-induced spatially nonuniform fluctuations of the local particle number and their crossover in a quasiperiodic free-fermion chain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03799)
*Toranosuke Matsubara,Kazuki Yamamoto*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper studies continuously monitored dynamics of a quasiperiodic free-fermion chain on a Fibonacci lattice, showing that continuous measurement induces a nonuniform spatial pattern in local particle number fluctuations and a measurement-induced crossover from long-range-structured to locally dominated distributions, with implications for other observables like correlation functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how continuous measurement affects fluctuations and spatial patterns in quasiperiodic systems, bridging nonequilibrium dynamics and quasiperiodic long-range order.

Method: The authors study a free-fermion chain on a Fibonacci lattice under continuous monitoring, analyzing local particle number fluctuations using both physical-space and perpendicular-space analyses.

Result: Fluctuations become nonuniform under continuous measurement due to quasiperiodic order; a measurement-induced crossover occurs: weak measurement reflects long-range spatial structure, while strong measurement leads to a distribution dominated by local site environments. This crossover also appears in connected correlation functions.

Conclusion: Continuous measurement reveals new nonequilibrium phenomena in quasiperiodic systems, with measurement strength tuning between global structure-dominated and local environment-dominated fluctuation patterns.

Abstract: We study continuously monitored dynamics of a quasiperiodic free-fermion chain defined on a Fibonacci lattice. We focus on fluctuations of the local particle number, which exhibit a spatially uniform distribution in the unitary limit. Remarkably, we demonstrate that they exhibit a nonuniform spatial pattern originating from the quasiperiodic long-range order under continuous measurement. Furthermore, employing both physicaland perpendicular-space analyses, we elucidate that measurement-induced crossover emerges in fluctuations due to the interplay between the incommensurate modulation and the continuous measurement. While weak measurement yields a distribution reflecting the long-range spatial structure of the quasiperiodic system, an increase in measurement strength alters the distribution into one dominated by the local environment of each site. We also elucidate that the measurement-induced crossover emerges in other physical quantities such as connected correlation functions. These findings offer insights into nonequilibrium quasiperiodic phenomena emerging in continuously monitored dynamics.

</details>


### [79] [Geometric Characteristics of Subproblems in Ising-Machine-Assisted Large Neighborhood Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05014)
*Masashi Yamashita,Shu Tanaka*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This study compares two large neighborhood search (LNS) strategies for solving QUBO formulations of vehicle routing problems on Ising machines: LNS-K (route-based subproblem construction) and LNS-Q (variable/constraint-based construction). Controlling for subproblem size, LNS-K achieves shorter total distances, indicating that subproblem design should consider semantic and geometric structures beyond size alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale QUBO formulations of constrained combinatorial problems exceed Ising machine input limits or degrade solution quality as binary variables increase. LNS mitigates this by optimizing restricted subproblems, but structural factors distinguishing subproblems beyond size are insufficiently characterized.

Method: The study examines vehicle routing problems and compares two LNS subproblem constructions controlling for the number of binary variables: LNS-K (route-based, using current solution vehicle routes) and LNS-Q (QUBO variable and constraint relation-based). The position variance metric measured spatial spread of selected customers.

Result: Under matched-size comparisons, LNS-K obtained shorter total distances than LNS-Q. Position variance decreased during iterations in LNS-K, indicating that spatial structure evolves over time.

Conclusion: Subproblem design for sequential optimization with Ising machines should consider semantic and geometric structures inherited from the current solution, not just subproblem size.

Abstract: Large-scale quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) formulations of constrained combinatorial optimization problems often exceed the input-size limit of present Ising machines or suffer from degraded solution quality as the number of binary variables increases. Large neighborhood search (LNS) mitigates this difficulty by sequentially optimizing restricted subproblems, but the structural factors that distinguish subproblems beyond the number of binary variables remain insufficiently characterized. In this study, we examine vehicle routing problems and compare a construction based on the vehicle routes of the current solution, denoted by LNS-K, with a construction based on QUBO variables and constraint relations, denoted by LNS-Q, while controlling the number of binary variables in the subproblems. Under the tested conditions, LNS-K obtained shorter total distances than LNS-Q in the matched-size comparisons, and the position variance, a measure of the spatial spread of the selected customers, decreased during the iterations in LNS-K. These observations suggest that subproblem design for sequential optimization with Ising machines should consider not only subproblem size but also semantic and geometric structures inherited from the current solution.

</details>


### [80] [Non-equilibrium coupling to a diffusing density breaks Ising universality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02661)
*Mattia Scandolo,Johannes Pausch,Michael E. Cates,Luca Di Carlo*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper reveals that nonreciprocal coupling between an order parameter and a conserved diffusive density can destabilize the otherwise robust Ising universality class, leading to a new non-equilibrium universality class characterized by a fast-diffusion fixed point, violation of fluctuation-dissipation relations, and splitting of correlation and response exponents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether the Ising universality class remains robust under nonreciprocal coupling to a conserved diffusive density, challenging the conventional wisdom that non-equilibrium perturbations generically flow to zero under renormalization.

Method: Renormalization group analysis below the upper critical dimension d_c=4, with two-loop calculations to establish fixed-point stability and compute the correction-to-scaling exponent ω≈0.020 in d=3. An all-orders modified Harris criterion ν>2/(d+z-2) is employed to confirm the nature of the fixed point.

Result: Below d_c=4, RG flows to a fast-diffusion fixed point where the density acts as long-range multiplicative noise, producing a novel universality class. The fixed point shows large-scale violation of fluctuation-dissipation relations, splitting the scaling exponents of correlation and response functions. In d=3, the correction-to-scaling exponent is small (ω≈0.020), indicating strong finite-size corrections. Ising universality is recovered only at d=2.

Conclusion: The nonreciprocal coupling breaks the robustness of the Ising universality class, yielding a novel non-equilibrium criticality with measurable hallmarks. The BIM (bistable Ising model) fixed point governs criticality in d=3, while Ising behavior is restored only in d=2.

Abstract: The Ising universality class is remarkably robust to non-equilibrium perturbations, which generically flow to zero under renormalization. We show that this robustness fails when an order parameter is coupled nonreciprocally to a conserved diffusive density. Below $d_c=4$, the renormalization group flows to a fast-diffusion fixed point at which the density acts as a long-range multiplicative noise, producing a novel universality class. The non-equilibrium nature of the fixed point is manifest in the large-scale violation of the fluctuation-dissipation relations, reflected in a splitting of the scaling exponents of the two-point correlation and response functions--a measurable hallmark of non-equilibrium critical fluctuations. A two-loop calculation establishes the stability of this fixed point but yields a small correction-to-scaling exponent $ω\approx0.020$ in $d=3$, implying strong finite-size corrections. An all-orders modified Harris criterion $ν>2/(d+z-2)$ confirms that the BIM fixed point governs criticality in $d=3$, with Ising universality recovered only at $d=2$.

</details>


### [81] [Non-equilibrium phase transition in the Brownian Ising Model: field theory, renormalization group, and exact results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02667)
*Mattia Scandolo,Luca Di Carlo*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper presents a complete renormalization-group analysis of the Brownian Ising Model (BIM), a non-equilibrium system coupling a Z₂ order parameter to a passive conserved density. Using ε=4-d expansion, the authors show this coupling is RG-relevant below 4D, driving the system to a new non-equilibrium fixed point distinct from Ising. They compute critical exponents (including a negative anomalous dimension η), find that correlation and response functions acquire different anomalous dimensions (violating fluctuation-dissipation theorem), and derive exact scaling relations that hold to all orders in perturbation theory. The Ising and quenched diluted-Ising classes are shown to be unstable in d=3, making the BIM fixed point the unique infrared attractor for nonzero diffusion constant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how non-equilibrium dynamics (specifically coupling between an order parameter and a conserved density with broken detailed balance) alters critical behavior, and to determine whether such systems belong to a new universality class distinct from equilibrium Ising.

Method: Martin-Siggia-Rose formalism combined with ε=4-d expansion, two-loop perturbative renormalization-group analysis, and derivation of all-order exact scaling relations from symmetry principles (linearity of density dynamics and emergent shift symmetry).

Result: The BIM exhibits a new non-equilibrium fixed point with negative anomalous dimension η, distinct correlation and response anomalous dimensions (violating fluctuation-dissipation theorem), a small correction-to-scaling exponent (implying large preasymptotic corrections), and exact scaling relation ν=2/(d+z-2). The Ising and quenched diluted-Ising classes are unstable in d=3.

Conclusion: The BIM fixed point is the unique infrared attractor for any nonzero diffusion constant in d=3, establishing a new non-equilibrium universality class. Numerical and experimental tests must account for large preasymptotic corrections due to the small correction-to-scaling exponent.

Abstract: We present a complete field-theoretical renormalization-group (RG) analysis of the Brownian Ising Model (BIM), in which a $\mathbb{Z}_2$ order parameter is coupled to a passive conserved density, breaking detailed balance. Using the Martin-Siggia-Rose formalism and an $ε=4-d$ expansion, we show that this density-order parameter coupling is RG-relevant below four dimensions and drives the system to a new non-equilibrium fixed point, distinct from the Ising universality class. Critical exponents are computed at lowest nontrivial order, some of which require a dedicated two-loop analysis. At large scales, the density acts as an effective noise that is white in time but long-range in space, enhancing order-parameter fluctuations and producing a negative anomalous dimension $η$. A defining feature of the new class is that the correlation and response functions acquire different anomalous dimensions, $η\neq 2 - γ/ ν$ - a direct, observable signature of fluctuation-dissipation-theorem violation at large scales that cannot occur in equilibrium. We also find a small correction-to-scaling exponent, implying large preasymptotic corrections that must be accounted for in numerical and experimental tests. We further derive a set of relations among renormalization factors that hold to all orders in perturbation theory, following from the linearity of the density dynamics and an emergent shift symmetry. These yield an exact scaling relation $ν= 2/(d+z-2)$ at the BIM fixed point and establish that the Ising universality class, as well as that of quenched diluted-Ising, is unstable in $d=3$. This establishes the BIM fixed point as the unique infrared attractor for any nonzero diffusion constant.

</details>


### [82] [Finite-Time Thermodynamics of Battery Discharging: Power-Efficiency Trade-Off and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03157)
*Rui-Han Liu,Yun-Qian Lin,Yu-Han Ma*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates a universal parabolic trade-off between battery power and efficiency (P ∝ η(1-η)), with efficiency at maximum power being exactly 1/2. It then derives an optimal multistage constant-current discharging policy (Iᵢ* = max(Iᵢ⁻, I₀)) via KKT conditions, where demand-limited stages run at minimum required currents and remaining stages use a uniform baseline current set by the deadline constraint, establishing a thermodynamic baseline for battery management scheduling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a rigorous thermodynamic baseline for battery management systems by uncovering the fundamental trade-off between output power and energy conversion efficiency due to internal dissipation, and to translate this theoretical bound into practical operational scheduling rules under real-time load demands and a global discharging deadline.

Method: The paper first derives a universal parabolic envelope P ∝ η(1-η) from the power-efficiency trade-off. It then formulates a multistage constant-current discharging (MSCD) schedule optimization problem with load demands and deadline constraints, solved analytically via Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) conditions. The dissipation-time Pareto front is traced to quantify how internal resistance shifts operational boundaries.

Result: The optimal policy is a remarkably compact rule: Iᵢ* = max(Iᵢ⁻, I₀). Demand-limited stages run exactly at their minimum required currents, while remaining stages are elevated to a uniform baseline I₀ determined by the deadline constraint. The efficiency at maximum power under the fundamental trade-off is exactly 1/2, mirroring the half-Carnot limit.

Conclusion: This work establishes a rigorous thermodynamic baseline for the scheduling layer of battery management systems and offers natural extensions to nonlinear models incorporating temperature and state-of-charge dependencies.

Abstract: Battery discharging is governed by a fundamental trade-off between output power and energy conversion efficiency due to internal dissipation. In this paper, we demonstrate that such a trade-off universally yields a parabolic envelope $P\proptoη(1-η)$. The efficiency at maximum power is exactly one half, mirroring the well-known half-Carnot limit in finite-time thermodynamics. To extend this bound into practical operational rules, we formulate a multistage constant-current discharging (MSCD) schedule subject to simultaneous real-time load demands and a global discharging deadline. Analytical resolution via the Karush--Kuhn--Tucker conditions reveals a remarkably compact optimal policy: $I_{i}^{\star}=\max(I_{i}^{-},I_{0})$. Under this rule, stages limited by external demand run exactly at their minimum required currents, while all remaining stages are elevated to a uniform baseline $I_{0}$ fixed by the deadline constraint. By tracing the dissipation--time Pareto front, we quantify how internal resistance shifts the operational boundaries and sharpens the trade-off corner. This analysis establishes a rigorous thermodynamic baseline for the scheduling layer of battery management systems, offering natural extensions to nonlinear models incorporating temperature and state-of-charge dependencies.

</details>


### [83] [Variance of the $SIS$ Epidemic on Networks: A Diffusion Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03300)
*Lucija Nora Farkaš,Sebastian Morel Balbi,Hrvoje Štefančić,István Zoltán Kiss,Vinko Zlatić*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper develops a variance approximation for the SIS epidemic process on configuration-model networks by combining Gleeson's approximate master equation with a van Kampen system-size expansion, yielding a closed drift-diffusion system for susceptible/edge counts validated against simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing FLLNs describe mean trajectories but ignore fluctuations; a rigorous FCLT exists for SI but not for SIS because recovery reintroduces vertices with partially unknown neighborhoods, breaking the clean distributional assumptions used in SI derivations.

Method: Combines Gleeson's approximate master equation (AME) framework with a van Kampen system-size expansion, deriving a closed drift and diffusion matrix for a reduced susceptible/SI-edge/SS-edge count vector, then obtaining time-dependent covariance via the associated Langevin/Lyapunov equation.

Result: Validation against Gillespie simulations across Poisson, regular, and power-law networks shows close agreement, with some deviations near the epidemic threshold and in strongly heterogeneous networks.

Conclusion: The proposed approximation captures SIS fluctuations tractably, bridging a gap where no FCLT analogue exists, though accuracy degrades near thresholds and in highly heterogeneous degree distributions.

Abstract: Functional laws of large numbers (FLLNs) describe the mean-field trajectory of epidemics on networks, but say nothing about the fluctuations around it. These fluctuations are governed by moments of the degree distribution not relevant at the level of the mean. A rigorous functional central limit theorem (FCLT) exists for the susceptible--infected ($SI$) process on configuration-model graphs, but no analogue exists for $SIS$, where recovery reintroduces vertices into the susceptible pool with partially known neighborhoods, breaking the clean neighborhood distribution the $SI$ derivation relies on. We develop a tractable variance approximation for Markovian $SIS$ on configuration-model graphs, combining Gleeson's approximate master equation (AME) framework with a van Kampen system-size expansion in the spirit of the $SI$ FCLT. We derive a closed drift and diffusion matrix for a reduced susceptible/$SI$-edge/$SS$-edge count vector and obtain the time-dependent covariance via the associated Langevin/Lyapunov equation. Validation against Gillespie simulation across Poisson, regular, and power-law networks shows close agreement, with deviations near the epidemic threshold and in strongly heterogeneous networks.

</details>


### [84] [Free energies and optimal reaction coordinates via entropy production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03722)
*Jérémy Diharce,Line Mouaffac,Axel Dian,Fabio Pietrucci*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper shows that calculating entropy production from short molecular dynamics trajectories can estimate free-energy barriers and identify optimal reaction coordinates for activated processes, demonstrated on benchmarks and a complex carbon nanoparticle system in water.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a method for estimating free-energy barriers and identifying optimal reaction coordinates using computationally inexpensive short trajectories, avoiding traditional costly enhanced sampling methods.

Method: Stochastic energetics analysis on ensembles of short MD trajectories relaxing toward equilibrium from the same initial configuration, projected onto different putative reaction coordinates. Entropy production is computed and used to infer free-energy barriers and rank order parameter quality.

Result: The method accurately estimates free-energy barriers on simple benchmarks and for a complex high-dimensional system (carbon nanoparticles in water). The ranking of order parameters via entropy production agrees with committor probability analysis.

Conclusion: Entropy production from short trajectories is directly linked to free-energy landscapes, reaction coordinates, and kinetic rates, revealing the connection between the second law and activated processes.

Abstract: We show through theory and numerical experiments that a straightforward calculation of entropy production on short molecular dynamics trajectories allows estimating free-energy barriers and identifying optimal reaction coordinates of activated processes. To this aim, we perform an analysis based on stochastic energetics on a set of trajectories relaxing towards equilibrium from a same initial configuration, projected on different putative coordinates. After demonstrating the approach on simple benchmarks, we show that it is possible to estimate the free-energy barrier of a complex high-dimensional system (carbon nanoparticles in water) and to rank the quality of order parameters, in agreement with the committor probability. The results shed light on the entanglement between the second law, free-energy landscapes, reaction coordinates and kinetic rates.

</details>


### [85] [Quantum tunneling Mpemba effect](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03845)
*Hisao Hayakawa,Satoshi Takada*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper investigates the quantum tunneling Mpemba effect in a symmetric double-well potential with boundary sinks, proving that the non-monotonic temperature-dependent spectral coefficient is a universal topological property governed by the Sturm-Liouville oscillation theorem and invariant to system size, while its observability requires strict timescale separation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the quantum tunneling Mpemba effect (where a hotter system cools faster than a colder one) within a continuous open quantum system framework, moving beyond abstract state-space quenches to real-space boundary-driven dissipation.

Method: Non-Hermitian spectral decomposition of the effective Hamiltonian for a symmetric double-well potential with external sinks at boundaries; mathematical analysis of the first non-trivial even-parity spectral coefficient using Sturm-Liouville theory.

Result: The intermediate thermal peak in a₂(Tᵢ) is a universal topological property invariant to system size L, arising from geometric/nodal alignment between localized states and non-Hermitian decay channels. Observability requires strict timescale separation: over-barrier escape rate ≫ deep-well tunneling doublet decay rates.

Conclusion: The continuous formulation bridges classical boundary-driven dissipation with open quantum dynamics, offering insights for engineering non-equilibrium states via tailored boundary loss.

Abstract: The quantum tunneling Mpemba effect is investigated within a continuous one-dimensional symmetric double-well potential open to external environmental sinks at the boundaries ($x=\pm L$). Using a non-Hermitian spectral decomposition of the effective Hamiltonian, we characterize the open-system relaxation dynamics without relying on abstract state-space quenches. We mathematically prove that the non-monotonic behavior of the first non-trivial even-parity spectral coefficient, $a_{2}(T_{i})$, with respect to the initial preparation temperature $T_{i}$ is a universal topological property born from quantum statistical mechanics. Crucially, we demonstrate that this intermediate thermal peak is governed by the Sturm-Liouville oscillation theorem and remains completely invariant with respect to the global system size $L$, contrasting sharply with the boundary-driven classical Mpemba effect. This universal peak arises from the geometric and nodal alignment between highly localized unperturbed states and extended non-Hermitian decay channels. Furthermore, we clarify that while this mechanism is robust, the actual observation of anomalous crossings in the total survival probability trace $S(t,T_{i})$ and the trace distance $\mathcal{D}(t,T_i)$ demand a strict separation of timescales, requiring the over-barrier escape rate to vastly exceed the decay rate of the deep-well tunneling doublet ($Γ_{2}\gg Γ_{0}$ and $Γ_2\gg Γ_1$). Our continuous formulation successfully bridges real-space classical boundary-driven dissipation with open quantum dynamics, providing novel insights for engineering non-equilibrium states via tailored boundary loss.

</details>


### [86] [The Reweighting Principle in Statistical Mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03867)
*Salvatore Romano*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper unifies conditioning and exponential tilting as minimum-relative-entropy updates, linking them to canonical (Gibbs) and microcanonical (Boltzmann) ensembles, and shows ensemble equivalence arises from entropy-bias competition in the thermodynamic limit, with extensions to path space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a unified information-theoretic framework for reweighting probability measures, reconciling concepts from conditioning, exponential tilting, and ensemble transformations in statistical mechanics.

Method: The paper employs relative entropy minimization, an enlarged probability space where observables are explicit variables, and large deviation theory to connect soft/hard constraints to canonical/microcanonical ensembles.

Result: Exponential tilting and conditioning correspond to soft- and hard-constraint minimum-relative-entropy updates respectively; they yield complementary thermodynamic structures (Legendre vs. Boltzmann/surprisal); microcanonical and canonical ensembles emerge as conditional and marginal distributions of a common structural prior; ensemble equivalence is derived from entropy-bias competition.

Conclusion: The framework extends naturally to path space, offering a unified probabilistic description of equilibrium thermodynamics and conditioned stochastic dynamics.

Abstract: Reweighting of probability measures provides a unifying perspective on conditioning, exponential tilting, and, more generally, ensemble transformations in statistical mechanics. We show that exponential tilting and conditioning arise as the minimum-relative-entropy updates associated with soft and hard constraints, respectively. Their relative entropies naturally inherit complementary thermodynamic structures: exponential tilting gives rise to the Legendre structure of the canonical ensemble and reduces to the Gibbs entropy for a uniform reference measure, while conditioning reduces to the Boltzmann entropy through the surprisal of the constrained macrostate. By introducing an enlarged probability space in which observables are treated as explicit variables, we further show that microcanonical and canonical ensembles arise as conditional and marginal distributions of a common structural prior after exponential reweighting. In the thermodynamic limit, described through large deviation theory, conditioning emerges from exponential tilting by concentration of measure, revealing ensemble equivalence as a consequence of entropy--bias competition. Finally, we outline how the same information-theoretic framework naturally extends to path space, suggesting a unified probabilistic description of equilibrium thermodynamics and conditioned stochastic dynamics.

</details>


### [87] [Universal fluctuations of first discoveries in competitive exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04252)
*Arthur Plaud,P. L. Krapivsky,S. Redner,Olivier Bénichou*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper introduces the discovery share X_n (fraction of first n discoveries by a tagged searcher) and shows that competitive exploration is governed by the spectral dimension d_s, with distinct regimes: persistent randomness for d_s<2, anomalously slow concentration for 2≤d_s<3, and Gaussian concentration for d_s≥3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In competitive exploration (e.g., foragers competing for food, agents capturing targets), knowing which sites are discovered is insufficient—we must determine which searcher arrived first. The central question is whether early competitive advantages persist or are erased in each exploration history.

Method: The authors introduce the discovery share X_n for a tagged searcher. They analyze two identical competitors with exchange symmetry fixing ⟨X_n⟩=1/2, and show that the behavior is controlled by the spectral dimension d_s, defined by p_0(t)∼t^{-d_s/2} for the probability of a single searcher returning to its starting point.

Result: The spectral dimension d_s separates three regimes: (1) persistent randomness in recurrent exploration (d_s<2), (2) anomalously slow non-Gaussian concentration (2≤d_s<3), and (3) Gaussian concentration with logarithmic correction at d_s=3 for d_s≥3. Exact asymptotic variances and discovery scales are derived for d_s≥2.

Conclusion: This phase structure persists across changes in geometry, competitor heterogeneity, number of competitors, and memory, revealing a general fluctuation theory of first-arrival inequalities. Two-point correlations of first-discovery labels identify the memory mechanism behind these regimes.

Abstract: Random exploration is usually quantified by how fast new space is found, from
  the range of a single walker to the territory collectively covered by many
  walkers. In competitive exploration, first arrival secures an exclusive resource, as when foragers compete for food items or agents capture distributed targets. It is then no longer enough to know which sites have been discovered: one must determine, for each discovered site, which searcher reached it first. We introduce the discovery
  share $X_n$, the fraction of the first $n$ collective discoveries secured by
  a tagged searcher. For two identical competitors, exchange symmetry fixes
  $\langle X_n\rangle=1/2$, but the central question is whether this equal
  split emerges in each long exploration history or only on average, \emph{i.e.} whether early
  competitive advantages are erased or persist. Here we show that the answer is
  controlled by the spectral dimension $d_s$, defined by the large-time decay of the probability that a single searcher is at
  its starting point after $t$ steps, $p_0(t)\sim t^{-d_s/2}$.
  Across ordinary diffusion, long-range superdiffusion and subdiffusion induced
  by crowding or memory, $d_s$ separates persistent randomness in
  recurrent exploration $(d_s<2)$, anomalously slow non-Gaussian concentration
  for $2\le d_s<3$, and Gaussian concentration, logarithmically corrected at
  $d_s=3$, for $d_s\ge3$. For $d_s\ge2$, we derive exact asymptotic
  variances, including prefactors, and the discovery scale on which competitive
  imbalances are erased. Two-point correlations of first-discovery labels identify the memory mechanism behind these regimes.
  The same phase structure persists under changes in geometry, competitor
  heterogeneity, number of competitors and memory, revealing a general fluctuation
  theory of first-arrival inequalities.

</details>


### [88] [Critical behavior of the driven Curie-Weiss model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05130)
*Ruohan Xu,Faezeh Khodabandehlou,Christian Maes*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper extends the phase diagram of the Curie-Weiss magnet under a time-periodic external field, revealing a coexistence regime of paramagnetic and ferromagnetic phases at large driving amplitudes and frequencies with low temperatures. It reports a diverging specific heat at the critical inverse temperature βc with asymmetric critical exponents (α=1 for β↓βc, α≈0.86 for β↑βc), and uses Floquet analysis to reveal dynamical criticality absent in thermal equilibrium.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To complete the phase diagram of the macroscopic Curie-Weiss magnet in a time-periodic external field and to uncover nonequilibrium critical phenomena—particularly the specific heat divergence—that are inaccessible under equilibrium conditions.

Method: Analytical and numerical analysis of the Curie-Weiss model under a time-periodic driving field, including computation of the phase diagram, specific heat divergence, critical exponents, and Floquet analysis to characterize dynamical criticality.

Result: Stable paramagnetic and ferromagnetic phases coexist at large driving amplitude/frequency and low temperatures. The specific heat diverges at βc with asymmetric critical exponents: α=1 approaching from above βc and α≈0.86 approaching from below, even for small driving. The Curie temperature decreases with driving.

Conclusion: Driving fundamentally alters the critical behavior of the Curie-Weiss magnet, producing a dynamical criticality with asymmetric exponents and a shifted Curie temperature, highlighting phenomena that are mostly impossible in thermal equilibrium.

Abstract: We complete the phase diagram of the macroscopic Curie-Weiss magnet in a time-periodic external field, as a function of temperature and driving parameters. There is a regime (large enough driving amplitude and frequency, at low temperatures) where stable paramagnetic and ferromagnetic phases coexist. In particular, we present a new detailed analysis of the (nonequilibrium) specific heat, diverging at the same critical inverse temperature $β_c$ as the magnetic susceptibility. The new Curie temperature decreases with the driving, and we find critical exponent $α=1$ for $β\downarrow β_c$, and $α\simeq 0.86$ for $β\uparrow β_c$, even for small driving. A Floquet analysis shows the nature of the criticality, which is dynamical, with implications that remain unseen and are mostly impossible when the system is in thermal equilibrium.

</details>


### [89] [On data-driven parameterizations of multidimensional generalized Langevin dynamics in the presence of a quadratic potential](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05151)
*Maximilian Braun,Martin Hanke,Niklas Wolf*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A numerical algorithm constructs Markov models for coarse-grained physical systems with memory effects by using velocity autocorrelation data to build a Prony series approximation, avoiding direct inversion for the memory kernel. The method is validated on MD data with 5 auxiliary variables (1D) and 12 auxiliary variables (2D).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a method for constructing Markov models for coarse-grained systems where memory effects are significant, avoiding the difficult inverse problem of directly computing memory kernels from autocorrelation data.

Method: Uses stationary velocity autocorrelation data to construct a Prony series approximation of the autocorrelation function; the Prony series parameters directly define the corresponding Markov model with auxiliary variables.

Result: Good agreement between the parameterized models and molecular dynamics data: 5 auxiliary variables suffice for a 1D system, 12 for a 2D system.

Conclusion: The proposed Prony-based approach effectively bypasses the inverse problem of memory kernel extraction and yields accurate Markovian embeddings for multidimensional coarse-grained systems with memory.

Abstract: We propose a numerical algorithm to construct a Markov model with an extended list of variables to parameterize the equation of motion of a multidimensional coarse-grained physical system in an external potential, when memory effects are relevant. Our method uses autocorrelation data of the stationary velocities, but it avoids the inverse problem of finding the corresponding memory kernel from these data in a first step. Rather, the data are used to construct a Prony series approximation of the autocorrelation function, and the parameters of this Prony series provide the corresponding Markov model. Numerical results for molecular dynamics data show a good match for parameterized models with five auxiliary variables for a one-dimensional, and twelve auxiliary variables for a two-dimensional system.

</details>


### [90] [Ising-Machine-Assisted Large Neighborhood Search with Flexibly Tunable Subproblem Size](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05169)
*Koshiro Fujimoto,Masashi Yamashita,Shu Tanaka*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: LNS-VT improves Ising-machine-assisted large neighborhood search by introducing a parameter (steps per vehicle) to finely control subproblem size, achieving ~10% better objective value on a 300-site VRP and demonstrating similar importance for the quadratic multiple knapsack problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing feasibility-preserving Ising-machine-assisted LNS for VRP re-optimizes entire routes of selected vehicles, which cannot adjust subproblem size finely, potentially degrading solution quality.

Method: Proposes LNS-VT, which introduces the number of consecutive steps re-optimized per vehicle as a tunable parameter to control subproblem granularity while preserving feasibility; tested on VRP and quadratic multiple knapsack problem.

Result: On a 300-site, 5-vehicle VRP, the best setting of LNS-VT reduced objective value by ~10% vs. the existing method after 100 iterations; appropriate subproblem size varied with current solution quality; similar effect observed on quadratic multiple knapsack problem.

Conclusion: Subproblem-size control is crucial in Ising-machine-assisted LNS, and the proposed fine-grained parameterization improves performance across different constrained optimization problems.

Abstract: Ising machines are heuristic solvers for combinatorial optimization, but their solution quality can degrade when large-scale constrained problems are solved directly. Ising-machine-assisted large neighborhood search (LNS) instead repeatedly updates a feasible current solution by solving smaller subproblems. An existing feasibility-preserving method for the vehicle routing problem (VRP) re-optimizes the entire routes of selected vehicles and thus cannot adjust the subproblem size finely. We propose LNS-VT, which introduces the number of consecutive steps re-optimized per vehicle as a parameter to control the subproblem size finely while preserving feasibility. For a 300-site, 5-vehicle VRP, its best setting reduced the objective value by approximately 10\% relative to the existing method after 100 iterations, and the appropriate setting changed with the current solution quality. Applying the same principle to the quadratic multiple knapsack problem, we confirmed that an appropriate subproblem size also exists, indicating that subproblem-size control is important in Ising-machine-assisted LNS.

</details>


### [91] [Semi-Markovian switching in a fluctuating harmonic trap: An age-structured formulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05173)
*Derek Frydel*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper analyzes a Brownian particle in a harmonic trap with switching stiffness (semi-Markovian dynamics). By reformulating the problem in an age-structured state space, exact steady-state solutions are derived. The stochastic-resetting limit is also studied, yielding a simpler variance-space description.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the behavior of a Brownian particle subjected to a harmonic trap whose stiffness switches between two values with arbitrary waiting-time statistics, introducing temporal memory that requires a novel theoretical treatment.

Method: The problem is reformulated in an enlarged age-structured state space to restore Markovianity, leading to a local Fokker-Planck description. Exact steady-state integral equations for spatial and birth distributions are derived. For the stochastic-resetting limit, the stationary spatial distribution is represented as a superposition of Gaussian states, reformulating the problem as a switching process in variance space.

Result: Exact expressions for stationary moments, injected power, and potential energy are obtained. The variance-space reformulation yields exact integral equations for variance distributions, enabling simplified analytical treatment.

Conclusion: The age-structured approach effectively handles semi-Markovian memory in switching traps, and the variance-space representation provides a tractable framework for analyzing stochastic-resetting limits.

Abstract: We study a Brownian particle in a harmonic trap whose stiffness switches between two values with arbitrary waiting-time statistics, generating semi-Markovian dynamics. To treat the resulting temporal memory, we formulate the problem in an enlarged age-structured state space, restoring Markovianity and yielding a local Fokker--Planck description. Within this framework, we derive exact steady-state integral equations for the spatial and birth distributions and obtain exact expressions for stationary moments, injected power, and potential energy. In the second part of the paper, we analyze the stochastic-resetting limit, corresponding to a particle alternately released and trapped. By representing the stationary spatial distribution as a superposition of Gaussian states with fluctuating variance, the problem can be reformulated as a switching process in variance space. This yields exact integral equations for the variance distributions and leads to a simplified description amenable to direct analytical treatment.

</details>


### [92] [Nucleation and time-reversal symmetry breaking in nonconserved scalar field theories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05194)
*Noah Ziethen,Michalis Chatzittofi,Michael E. Cates,Cesare Nardini*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper develops Nonequilibrium Nucleation Theory (NNT), a generalization of Classical Nucleation Theory (CNT) to non-equilibrium field theories with non-conserved order parameter. It provides two equivalent derivations of droplet radius dynamics, shows the quasipotential barrier differs from the instanton assumption, and validates the theory against numerical simulations in Active Model A and population dynamics models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical Nucleation Theory (CNT) is limited to equilibrium systems described by a single reaction coordinate (droplet radius). This work aims to generalize nucleation theory to non-equilibrium systems with non-conserved order parameters, where time-reversal symmetry is broken and standard assumptions (e.g., instanton as time-reversal of relaxation) fail.

Method: Two equivalent derivations are presented: (1) a stochastic route via direct projection of the stochastic field equation onto the radial reaction coordinate, and (2) a route based on minimization of the Freidlin-Wentzell action. The theory leverages the perturbative structure emerging in the limit of large critical radius and uses careful definition of the reaction coordinate to handle interfacial profiles differing from the relaxation path. Capillary wave dynamics are also derived to justify the near-spherical droplet assumption.

Result: NNT predicts a quasipotential barrier different from the naive instanton assumption. Analytical results are validated against numerical action minimization for two examples: Active Model A (a non-equilibrium generalization of Model A) and a population dynamics model with two noise choices breaking time-reversal symmetry. Excellent agreement is found in all cases.

Conclusion: NNT provides a systematic, general framework for constructing nucleation theories in broad classes of non-equilibrium systems, including active matter, reaction-diffusion systems, and population dynamics.

Abstract: Classical nucleation theory (CNT) describes the formation of a stable phase from a metastable one in terms of a single reaction coordinate that corresponds to the radius of a nucleating droplet. In this work, we provide a full account of nonequilibrium nucleation theory (NNT), which generalizes CNT to non-equilibrium field theories with non-conserved order parameter. We present two equivalent derivations of the dynamics of the droplet radius: a stochastic route, based on a direct projection of the stochastic field equation onto the radial reaction coordinate, and a route based on the minimization of the Freidlin-Wentzell action. Crucially, the quasipotential barrier predicted by NNT differs from the one found when assuming the instanton to be the time-reversal of the relaxation dynamics. Whereas the interfacial density profile differs from that on the relaxation path, an analytical derivation of NNT remains possible using a careful definition of the reaction coordinate. This leverages the perturbative structure that (in common with CNT) emerges in the limit of large critical radius. We further derive with similar techniques the dynamics of capillary waves, whose stability is required for the CNT/NNT precept of a near-spherical droplet to prevail. After deriving our theory for generic non-conserved field-theories, we address two explicit examples: a non-equilibrium generalization of Model A (Active Model A), and a population dynamics model (with two choices of noise that each break time-reversal symmetry). In both cases, we validate our analytical NNT against numerical results obtained by action minimization, with excellent agreement. NNT provide a systematic framework for constructing nucleation theories in a broad class of non-equilibrium systems from active matter, reaction-diffusion systems and population dynamics.

</details>


### [93] [A universal emulator for planar Ising lattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05308)
*Riccardo Ben Alì Zinati,Alessandro Codello*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper introduces Ising emulators for two-dimensional Ising models, where flat lattices are represented as supercells of a single host lattice with a fixed Feynman-Vdovichenko/Kac-Ward solution. The framework is applied to Archimedean lattices, 2-uniform lattices (first-time thermodynamics), pentagonal lattices, fractal, and disordered Ising models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a unified framework (Ising emulator) that allows computing thermodynamic quantities for a wide variety of two-dimensional Ising models (ferro- and antiferromagnetic) using a single transition matrix with lattice-specific binary masks, avoiding case-by-case solutions.

Method: Construct explicit square and triangular emulators where flat lattices are represented as site- or bond-diluted supercells of a host lattice. A single transition matrix, supplemented by lattice-specific binary masks, computes all thermodynamic quantities via the Feynman-Vdovichenko/Kac-Ward solution.

Result: Applied to all eleven Archimedean lattices, all twenty 2-uniform lattices (thermodynamics obtained for the first time), several pentagonal lattices, and extended to fractal and disordered Ising models with no modification to the underlying machinery.

Conclusion: The Ising emulator framework provides a universal, efficient approach to solving 2D Ising models across diverse lattice geometries, including regular, fractal, and disordered systems, without requiring tailored solutions for each lattice type.

Abstract: We introduce the notion of an Ising emulator for two-dimensional Ising models: flat, unit-edge-length lattices can be represented as site- or bond-diluted supercells of a single host lattice, for which the Feynman--Vdovichenko/Kac--Ward solution is fixed once and for all. We construct explicit square and triangular emulators and show that a single transition matrix, supplemented by lattice-specific binary masks, gives all the thermodynamic quantities of interest for both ferro- and antiferromagnetic couplings. We apply the framework to all eleven Archimedean lattices, to all twenty $2$-uniform lattices -- whose thermodynamics is obtained here for the first time -- and to several pentagonal lattices, and show that the same construction extends directly to fractal and disordered Ising models, with no modification to the underlying machinery.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.dis-nn'></div>

# cond-mat.dis-nn [[Back]](#toc)

### [94] [Broken Ergodicity and the Violation of the Fluctuation-Dissipation Theorem Lead to Generalization Beyond Overfitting in Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04135)
*Chan Li,Nigel Goldenfeld*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper uses dynamical mean field theory to demonstrate that the "double descent" phenomenon in neural network generalization arises from a phase transition in the stochastic field theory of training, characterized by broken ergodicity and a fluctuation-dissipation theorem breakdown, with a response function analogous to the London model of superconductivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explain the counterintuitive "double descent" behavior where neural network generalization improves with increasing model capacity even beyond the interpolation threshold, despite generalization error diverging near a critical parameter count.

Method: Dynamical mean field theory applied to the stochastic field theory describing the training process; calculation of critical exponents, scaling functions, and analysis of fluctuation-dissipation theorem breakdown.

Result: The double descent phenomenon is identified as a phase transition marked by broken ergodicity and fluctuation-dissipation theorem violation; the response function mirrors the London model of superconductivity.

Conclusion: The neural network's generalization ability is analogous to the rigidity of a superconducting wave function, establishing a formal connection between statistical learning theory and condensed matter physics.

Abstract: The remarkable ability of modern neural networks to generalize improves with increasing network capacity, even when the number of model parameters or effective degrees of freedom exceeds the number of training data points. This phenomenon is all the more surprising given that generalization error diverges when the number of model parameters approaches a critical value from below. Here we use dynamical mean field theory to show that this so-called "double descent" behavior is the outcome of a phase transition in the stochastic field theory describing the training process. We calculate the critical exponents and scaling function of the double descent phase transition, and show that it is marked by a breakdown of the fluctuation-dissipation theorem associated with broken ergodicity. The corresponding response function has the same functional form as the simple London model of the superconducting transition, with the rigidity of the wave function corresponding to the neural network's ability to generalize accurately.

</details>

### [95] [Helium Ground State Treated in Classical Physics with Classical Zero-Point Radiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02727)
*Timothy H. Boyer*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper considers the helium atom's ground state using classical electrodynamics with zero-point radiation, finding approximate energy balance for electrons in a circular orbit, yielding a classical result close to but different from quantum calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether classical electrodynamics, when augmented with classical electromagnetic zero-point radiation, can approximate the ground state of the helium atom, potentially offering an alternative to quantum mechanical descriptions.

Method: The authors treat the two electrons as point charges positioned on opposite ends of a diameter of a common circular orbit around the nucleus. They balance energy loss from emitted radiation against energy gain from classical zero-point radiation within classical electrodynamics.

Result: An approximate energy balance is achieved, yielding a ground state energy value that is close to, but numerically different from, the quantum mechanical calculation.

Conclusion: Classical electrodynamics with zero-point radiation can approximate the helium ground state, but the result does not exactly match the quantum value, indicating limitations or the need for further refinement.

Abstract: The ground state of the helium atom is considered within classical electrodynamics which includes classical electromagnetic zero-point radiation. Approximate energy balance between energy loss through emitted radiation and energy gain from classical zero-point radiation is found when the two electrons are treated as charges located on opposite ends of a diameter of a common circular orbit around the nucleus. The classical result gives approximately the same value as that given by the quantum calculation, but is a different value.

</details>


### [96] [Reconfigurable Bitter-Type Electromagnet for Zeeman Slowing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02813)
*Emma G. Hataway,Emma K. Falk,Kaia E. O'Neill,Morgan P. Berghof,Ben A. Olsen*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a Bitter-type electromagnet coil design for Zeeman slowers that is easily disassembled and reconfigured without breaking vacuum, suitable for slowing lithium atoms with a near-ideal field profile, low resistance and inductance, fast switching, and forced-air cooling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many Zeeman slower magnets are trapped inside vacuum chambers, preventing modification or repair without breaking vacuum, which is costly and time-consuming.

Method: A Bitter-type electromagnet coil design is described that can be disassembled, reconfigured, repaired, and replaced without disturbing the vacuum system. The coil uses a single DC power supply and forced-air cooling.

Result: The coil produces a near-ideal field profile, with resistance 5.7(9) mΩ, inductance 8.8(2) μH, switching times ~100 μs, and moderate heating under forced-air cooling. It can be repeatedly disassembled and reassembled without performance loss.

Conclusion: This coil design offers greater flexibility than traditional electromagnets and can be retrofitted onto existing UHV chambers, enabling easy modification and repair without breaking vacuum.

Abstract: Many Zeeman slower magnets are geometrically captured by a vacuum chamber, preventing modification or repair without breaking vacuum. We describe a Bitter-type electromagnet coil design that can be easily disassembled, reconfigured, repaired, and replaced without disturbing the vacuum system. This coil, designed to slow lithium atoms, produces a near-ideal field profile with a single DC power supply. With a resistance of 5.7(9) m$Ω$, an inductance of 8.8(2) $μ$H, and switching times as low as $\sim100$ $μ$s, the coil compares favorably to other designs, and can be disassembled, modified, and reassembled repeatedly without loss of performance. With forced-air cooling, the coil experiences moderate heating. This coil design offers greater flexibility than traditional electromagnet designs, and it can retrofitted onto existing UHV chambers.

</details>


### [97] [Calculation of the energy levels and hyperfine structure for Xe~II, Rn~II, and Og~II ions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03175)
*T. H. Dinh,V. A. Dzuba,V. V. Flambaum*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper calculates energy levels, Landé g-factors, and hyperfine-structure constants for singly ionized noble-gas atoms Xe II, Rn II, and Og II using configuration-interaction and perturbative methods, achieving good agreement with experimental data for Xe II and providing predictions for the superheavy elements Rn II and Og II.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide accurate theoretical predictions for the spectroscopic properties of Rn II and Og II, where experimental data are limited or absent, and to extract nuclear magnetic dipole and electric quadrupole moments from future measurements in the superheavy region.

Method: Configuration-interaction method with perturbative treatment of high-lying configurations; time-dependent Hartree-Fock method for core polarization; inclusion of Breit and quantum-electrodynamic corrections.

Result: ~1% accuracy for energies and ~10% accuracy for hyperfine constants in Xe II compared with experiment; Breit and QED corrections to hyperfine structure can be strongly enhanced by configuration mixing when interacting states with very different hyperfine matrix elements are separated by small energy intervals, explicitly demonstrated for odd-parity J=1/2 states of Og II.

Conclusion: The calculated hyperfine-structure constants for Rn II and Og II provide the electronic factors needed for extracting nuclear moments from future spectroscopic experiments, and are useful for experimental studies of radon and oganesson ions and for testing nuclear models in the superheavy region.

Abstract: Energy levels, Landé $g$-factors, and hyperfine-structure constants are calculated for the singly ionized noble-gas atoms Xe II, Rn II, and Og II. The calculations are performed using the configuration-interaction method with perturbative treatment of high-lying configurations. Core polarization effects in the hyperfine interaction are included within the time-dependent Hartree-Fock method. Calculations for Xe II are used to test the accuracy of the approach by comparison with available experimental data. The agreement is at the level of about one percent for the energies and typically about ten percent for the hyperfine constants, with better accuracy for states with large hyperfine constants. Predictions are then presented for Rn II and Og II, for which experimental spectroscopic data are limited or absent. Special attention is paid to Breit and quantum-electrodynamic corrections to the hyperfine structure in heavy many-electron ions. We show that these corrections may be strongly enhanced by configuration mixing when interacting states with very different hyperfine matrix elements are separated by small energy intervals. This effect is demonstrated explicitly for odd-parity $J=1/2$ states of Og II. The calculated hyperfine-structure constants for Rn II and Og II provide electronic factors needed for extracting nuclear magnetic dipole and electric quadrupole moments from future spectroscopic measurements. These results may be useful for experimental studies of radon and oganesson ions and for testing nuclear models in the superheavy region.

</details>


### [98] [Development of a High-Performance Permanent Magnet System for Ion Trapping Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03255)
*Jifei Wu,Jiawei Wang,Tianhang Zhang,Zichen Su,Liangyu Huang,Wei Wu,Bingsheng Tu*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: A compact permanent magnet using 15 stacked NdFeB rings in an NS-SN-NS configuration achieves 0.8T central field with 99.988% uniformity over 1mm radius, offering a cryogen-free, power-free alternative to superconducting magnets for ion-trap and FT-ICR MS applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a compact, high-performance permanent magnet that eliminates the need for cryogenics and external power, providing a cost-effective alternative to superconducting magnets for ion-trap development and FT-ICR mass spectrometry.

Method: Design and fabrication of a compact permanent magnet using an optimized stacked structure of fifteen NdFeB rings arranged in a tunable NS-SN-NS configuration.

Result: Central magnetic field of 0.8T with reconstructed uniformity of 99.988% within a 1mm radius spherical volume; remaining field inhomogeneity dominated by radial dipole components.

Conclusion: This permanent magnet design offers a high-performance, cost-effective, cryogen-free, and power-free alternative to superconducting magnets, suitable for ion-trap and FT-ICR MS applications.

Abstract: This work presents the design and fabrication of a compact permanent magnet based on an optimized stacked structure of fifteen NdFeB rings. The tunable NS-SN-NS configuration generates a central magnetic field of 0.8T with a reconstructed uniformity of 99.988% within a 1mm radius spherical volume. The remaining field inhomogeneity is dominated by radial dipole components. Requiring neither cryogenics nor external power, this design provides a high-performance and cost-effective alternative to superconducting magnets for applications in ion-trap development and Fourier-transform ion cyclotron resonance mass spectrometry.

</details>


### [99] [Atomic oven with rapid thermal response for atom experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04393)
*Weilong Huang,Congjun Zou,Feiyu Dong,Huirong Xiao,Zejian Ren,Shanchao Zhang*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: A new heater design for atomic ovens achieves 7.65x faster heating rate, reaching 1200K, and generates a ytterbium beam flux >10^14 atoms/s at 823K, reducing experimental dead time in cold atom research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce experimental dead time in quantum gas experiments by designing an atomic oven with faster thermal response and controllable atomic beam flux.

Method: A new heater design with improved thermal response is developed for high-temperature atomic ovens, achieving faster heating rates compared to conventional resistive heaters.

Result: Heating rate improved by 7.65x over conventional resistive heaters; crucible temperature reaches 1200K; collimated ytterbium beam flux exceeds 10^14 atoms/s at 823K.

Conclusion: The new heater design offers a promising solution to shorten experimental dead time and improve efficiency in cold atom experiments.

Abstract: Atomic oven generating controllable atomic beam flux plays a fundmental role in quantum gas experiments. Here, we report a new heater design that can heat up an high temperature atomic oven with fast thermal response. The new heater shows a heating rate improved by 7.65 times comparing to that of the onventional resistive heater while the crucible temperature can heated up to 1200K. With this oven, we generated a collimated ytterbium beam with flux exceeding $10^{14} \text{ atoms/s}$ at 823 K. We believe that our design offers a promising solution for shortening experimental dead time and improve the experiment efficiency in cold atom researches.

</details>


### [100] [Hydrogenic rotational levels with spin-0 or spin-1/2 constituent particles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04815)
*Vojtěch Patkóš,Mateusz Pańtak,Krzysztof Pachucki*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents state-of-the-art theoretical predictions for muonic, kaonic, and antiprotonic atoms using nonrelativistic quantum electrodynamics, showing that improved accuracy could enable precision measurements of nuclear properties and tests of new physics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop unified theoretical predictions for exotic atoms (muonic, kaonic, antiprotonic) with spin-0 or spin-1/2 constituents in rotational states L>1, and to assess the potential of precision spectroscopy for determining nuclear properties and testing hypothetical long-range interactions.

Method: Nonrelativistic quantum electrodynamics (NRQED) with a unified description of two-body systems, accommodating arbitrary masses, magnetic moments, and spin states (0 or 1/2) for rotational states with L>1.

Result: Theoretical accuracy can be further improved, enabling high-accuracy determinations of nuclear charge radii and electric dipole polarizabilities.

Conclusion: Precision spectroscopy of muonic and hadronic atoms can serve as a powerful tool for determining nuclear properties and testing the existence of hypothetical long-range hadronic interactions.

Abstract: We employ nonrelativistic quantum electrodynamics with a unified description of two-body systems with spin-0 or spin-1/2 constituents, arbitrary masses, and arbitrary magnetic moments in rotational states with $L>1$, to present state-of-the-art theoretical predictions for muonic, kaonic, and antiprotonic atoms that have recently been measured or are targeted by upcoming experiments. We show that the theoretical accuracy can further be improved, opening the possibility of using precision spectroscopy of muonic and hadronic atoms for high-accuracy determinations of nuclear charge radii and nuclear electric dipole polarizabilities, and for testing the existence of hypothetical long-range hadronic interactions.

</details>


### [101] [Super-molasses returns: All optical near-resonance laser cooling and trapping of neutral atoms from background vapor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04966)
*Matt Himsworth,Chester Camm,Max Carey,Jack Saywell,Jonathan Woods,Vilius Atkoucius,Florence Concepcion,Konstantinos Karakostas,Hannah Brady,Doruk Tan Atila,Ellie Heywood,Alex Jantzen,Andrei Dragomir,Christopher Morley,James Bateman*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a novel laser trap using a simple collimated beam geometry that creates both velocity- and position-dependent restoring forces, enabling dense cold atomic clouds (>10⁶ atoms, 10¹⁰ atoms/cm³) without any magnetic field, compatible with sub-Doppler cooling below 10 μK, demonstrated with ⁸⁷Rb, and suitable for quantum sensing, timing, and computing applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an alternative to the Magneto-Optical Trap (MOT) that captures and cools atoms without requiring magnetic fields, simplifying the experimental setup while maintaining comparable performance.

Method: A simple geometry of collimated laser beams provides both velocity-dependent (cooling) and position-dependent (trapping) restoring forces. The trap is demonstrated by capturing ⁸⁷Rb atoms from background vapor, compatible with sub-Doppler cooling techniques.

Result: Achieves >10⁶ atoms and density of 10¹⁰ atoms/cm³ (similar to MOT), with temperatures below 10 μK using sub-Doppler cooling, all without any magnetic field.

Conclusion: This magnetic-field-free trap offers unique properties suitable for quantum sensing, timing, computing, fundamental science, and metrology applications.

Abstract: Laser cooled and trapped atoms have been the workhorse of atomic physics for the past four decades. The predominant method has been the highly versatile Magneto-Optical Trap. We describe an alternative laser trap involving a simple geometry of collimated laser beams that provides both a velocity and position dependent restoring force such that a dense cloud of cold atoms is formed. This technique produces similar atom number ($>10^6$) and density ($10^{10}$\,atoms/cm$^{3}$) to the Magneto-Optical Trap, albeit with \emph{no magnetic field}. The beam geometry is compatible with conventional sub-Doppler cooling techniques, allowing the trapped cloud to be cooled to $< 10~μ$K. We demonstrate the validity and robustness of the trap by capturing $^{87}$Rb atoms directly from the background vapor and provide a theoretical discussion of the underlying principles. This trap has many unique properties that make it highly suitable for quantum sensing, timing, and computing applications as well as a new tool in fundamental science and metrology.

</details>


### [102] [Investigating Role of Electron Correlation Effects via Triple Excitations for Precise Evaluation of Energies and Hyperfine Structure Constants in $^{23}$Na](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05012)
*Vaibhav Katyal,B. K. Sahoo*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper uses relativistic coupled-cluster theory with triple excitations, plus Breit, QED, and Bohr-Weisskopf corrections, to accurately compute hyperfine structure constants for $^{23}$Na, showing that relativistic and nuclear effects are as important as electron correlation for agreement with experiment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous all-order relativistic many-body calculations of magnetic dipole hyperfine constants for low-lying states of $^{23}$Na showed noticeable discrepancies with experiment, motivating a more complete theoretical treatment.

Method: Relativistic coupled-cluster theory with explicit inclusion of triple excitations, supplemented by corrections from the Breit interaction, quantum electrodynamics (QED), and the Bohr-Weisskopf (BW) effect.

Result: Lower-order relativistic and BW effects contribute almost as significantly as electron correlation (including triple excitations); incorporating all these corrections reconciles theoretical predictions with experimental observations.

Conclusion: Accurate hyperfine constants in Na require simultaneous treatment of electron correlation, relativistic effects, and nuclear finite-size (BW) corrections; the approach guides similar studies for heavier alkali systems.

Abstract: Accurate determination of hyperfine structure constants in atomic systems provides important insight into the interplay of electron correlation and relativistic effects in the nuclear region. Although sodium (Na) is a relatively light atom, previous all-order relativistic many-body calculations of the magnetic dipole hyperfine constants for the low-lying states of $^{23}$Na show noticeable discrepancies with experiment. To address this, we calculate the ionization potentials and hyperfine structure constants of $^{23}$Na using relativistic coupled-cluster theory with explicit inclusion of triple excitations. We further incorporate corrections from the Breit interaction, quantum electrodynamics, and the Bohr-Weisskopf (BW) effect. Results from lower-order methods are also presented to assess the importance of different physical contributions across states. Our calculations demonstrate that contributions from the lower-order relativistic and BW effects play almost similar roles with the electron correlation effects, including triple excitations, and are essential for reconciling theoretical predictions with experimental observations. This study can also serve as a useful guide for understanding the role of triples in heavier alkali systems.

</details>


### [103] [Fast Pulses for High-Fidelity Circularization of Interacting Rydberg atoms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05216)
*Matthias Hüls,Aurore A. Young,Clément Sayrin,Michel Brune,Jean-Michel Raimond,Tommaso Calarco,Felix Motzoi,Robert Zeier,Eloisa Cuestas*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This theoretical work demonstrates that fast radio-frequency pulses can efficiently circularize interacting Rydberg atoms by adapting single-atom pulses to compensate for interaction-induced shifts, achieving ≥95% fidelity for two ⁸⁷Rb atoms at n=52 in 65 ns with interatomic distances down to 6.5 μm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Circular Rydberg atoms are promising for quantum technologies, but the final circularization step—requiring transfer of large angular momentum via RF pulses—is a bottleneck. Previous work focused on single atoms, ignoring interactions that are essential for quantum simulation and computation.

Method: The authors analytically derive a simple two-parameter functional form to adapt single-atom RF pulses to interaction-induced shifts. They also combine adapted pulses with Krotov's pulse-shaping algorithm for further optimization.

Result: Adapted pulses prepare two ⁸⁷Rb atoms in n=52 circular states after 65 ns with ≥95% fidelity for interatomic distances ≥6.5 μm across all angular configurations, while meeting experimental constraints.

Conclusion: Fast RF pulses can circularize interacting Rydberg atoms by accounting for interaction shifts, removing a major bottleneck for technological applications of circular Rydberg atoms in quantum computing, simulation, and sensing.

Abstract: Circular states in Rydberg atoms offer a promising platform for quantum computation, quantum simulation and quantum sensing. However, the final step of their preparation - termed as circularization, a process that involves the transfer of a large amount of angular momentum quanta to the valence electron by means of radio-frequency (RF) pulses - remains as a major bottleneck for all technological applications based on interacting circular Rydberg atoms. Even though successfully implemented to circularize an atom cloud in the dilute regime, previous efforts to speed up the circularization process have focused on the single-atom case, thereby neglecting the interactions which constitute one of the main resources for quantum simulation and computation. In this theoretical work we show how interactions between two atoms disturb the efficiency of pulses designed for single atoms and identify shifts induced by the interactions on relevant transition energies as the dominant disturbance. We demonstrate that the initial efficiency of single-atom pulses can be restored by adapting them to these shifts. Our approach is based on a simple functional form depending only on two linear parameters, which we derive analytically. The adapted pulses prepare two $^{87}$Rb atoms after $65 \,$ns in a $n=52$ circular state with a fidelity of at least $95\,\%$ for interatomic distances down to $6.5\,μ$m and for all angular configurations, while also complying experimental amplitude and frequency constraints. Finally, we show that when combining our adapted pulses with Krotov's pulse-shaping algorithm we obtain high-fidelity pulses for any pair arrangement with interatomic distances larger than $5.9\,μ$m. This work demonstrates that fast RF pulses can circularize interacting Rydberg atoms, paving the way toward their technological application.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mtrl-sci'></div>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [104] [Data-Driven Prediction of NaCl-Type Entropy-Stabilized Oxide Compositions from First-Principles and Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04502)
*Sebastien Junier,Celine Barreteau,David Berardan,Yann-Andrev Kerneur,Jean-Claude Crivello*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A high-throughput computational framework combining DFT, SQS, convex-hull thermodynamics, and machine learning (optimized multilayer perceptron) screens 4368 equimolar quinary NaCl-type entropy-stabilized oxides, achieving ~4 kJ/mol test error with DFT calculations needed for only ~10% of compositions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Entropy-stabilized oxides (ESOs) offer access to vast multicomponent compositional spaces, but the large number of possible mixtures and the need to assess stability against competing phases make identifying promising candidates challenging.

Method: Developed a high-throughput computational framework that: (1) constructs a consistent reference database of binary/ternary ordered oxides using GGA and meta-GGA; (2) describes quinary disordered phases via SQS supercells; (3) trains machine-learning models (optimized multilayer perceptron) to predict convex-hull distance and stabilization temperature for all 4368 equimolar quinary compositions from 16 cation species; requiring DFT for only ~10% of quinary systems.

Result: The optimized multilayer perceptron achieved the best predictive performance with a test error of ~4 kJ/mol. The approach captures main stability trends and dominant competing phases when compared with experimental synthesis tests and computed decomposition paths, though absolute stabilization temperatures are affected by systematic thermodynamic approximations.

Conclusion: Establishes an efficient data-driven route for exploring multicomponent oxides and provides practical guidance for experimental search of new ESOs.

Abstract: Entropy-stabilized oxides (ESOs) open access to vast multicomponent compositional spaces, but identifying promising candidates remains challenging because of the large number of possible mixtures and the need to assess their stability against competing phases. In this work, we develop a high-throughput computational framework to screen equimolar quinary ESOs in the NaCl structure type by combining density functional theory (DFT), special quasirandom structures (SQS), convex-hull thermodynamics, and supervised machine learning. A consistent reference database of binary and ternary ordered oxides, including disordered phases such as all binary cation combinations in the NaCl-type oxide, is first constructed using GGA and meta-GGA calculations. Quinary disordered phases are then described by SQS supercells and used to train machine-learning models that predict the distance to the convex hull and the corresponding stabilization temperature over the full set of 4368 possible equimolar quinary compositions generated from 16 cation species. Among the tested models, an optimized multilayer perceptron provides the best predictive performance, with a test error of about 4 kJ/mol, while requiring explicit DFT calculations for only about 10% of the quinary systems. Comparison with experimental synthesis tests and computed decomposition paths further shows that the approach captures the main stability trends and the dominant competing phases, although absolute stabilization temperatures remain affected by systematic thermodynamic approximations. These results establish an efficient route for the data-driven exploration of multicomponent oxides and provide practical guidance for the experimental search for new ESOs.

</details>


### [105] [High-Entropy Nitride Photocatalysts for Visible-Light Antibiotic Degradation: Structural Stability, In Situ Interfacial Visualization, and Molecular-Level Mechanistic Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02629)
*Zahoor Manzoor,Shamik Chowdhury,Raphael Benjamim de Oliveira,Marcelo Lopes Pereira Junior,Guilherme da Silva Lopes Fabris,Prikshat Dadhwal,Douglas S. Galvao,Chandra Sekhar Tiwary*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: (MnFeCoNiCu)N high-entropy nitride nanoparticles were synthesized as visible-light photocatalysts, achieving 96% SME and 94% TCL degradation within 2 hours, with excellent stability, low metal leaching, and effective detoxification in real water matrices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-entropy nitrides (HENs) offer tunable electronic structures, high stability, and abundant active sites. Nitrogen incorporation enhances visible-light absorption and charge separation. The study aims to develop efficient, durable visible-light-driven photocatalysts for antibiotic degradation in complex water environments.

Method: (MnFeCoNiCu)N HEN nanoparticles were synthesized as photocatalysts. Performance was evaluated under varying pH, catalyst dosage, pollutant concentration, and coexisting ions. Characterization included temperature-dependent synchrotron AD-XRD, in situ liquid-cell TEM, molecular simulations, and phytotoxicity tests with Vigna radiata.

Result: 96% degradation of sulfamethoxazole and 94% removal of tetracycline within 2 h under visible light. Negligible metal leaching, excellent thermal/structural stability, stable multi-cycle reuse. In situ TEM and simulations revealed catalyst-pollutant interaction modes. Phytotoxicity confirmed detoxification.

Conclusion: (MnFeCoNiCu)N HENs are efficient, durable visible-light photocatalysts for antibiotic removal from complex aqueous environments, with practical applicability in real water matrices.

Abstract: High-entropy nitrides (HENs) have emerged as a promising class of advanced materials with tunable electronic structures, high stability, and abundant active sites.
