It exhibits strong optical anisotropy with in-plane Drude-like response and negative real dielectric function at low energies, while out-of-plane response remains positive. Plasma frequencies are 3.21 eV (in-plane) and 1.06 eV (out-of-plane), highlighting potential for polarization-sensitive optoelectronics and plasmonics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically characterize the structural, electronic, and optical properties of monolayer α-graphdiyne using first-principles DFT calculations, with the aim of understanding its fundamental physical properties and assessing its potential for optoelectronic and plasmonic applications.

Method: Density-functional theory within the plane-wave pseudopotential formalism was employed to calculate the structural, electronic band structure, density of states, and optical properties (dielectric function, absorption, extinction, reflectivity, electron energy-loss spectra) of monolayer α-graphdiyne.

Result: α-GDY is a gapless Dirac semimetal with Dirac crossing at the K point. Electronic states near the Fermi level are dominated by carbon 2p orbitals. The optical response is highly anisotropic: in-plane dielectric function shows Drude-like response and negative real part at low energies, while out-of-plane remains positive. Plasma frequencies are 3.21 eV (in-plane) and 1.06 eV (out-of-plane).

Conclusion: Monolayer α-GDY combines Dirac-like electronic behavior with strongly anisotropic optical properties, making it a promising candidate for polarization-sensitive optoelectronic, plasmonic, and nanoelectronic devices.

Abstract: The structural, electronic, and optical properties of monolayer $α$-graphdiyne ($α$-GDY) are systematically investigated using density-functional theory within the plane-wave pseudopotential formalism. The electronic band structure reveals a gapless Dirac crossing at the K point, demonstrating the Dirac semimetallic character of the monolayer. The calculated total and orbital-projected density of states show that the electronic states near the Fermi level are dominated by the carbon $2p$ orbitals, while the contribution of the $2s$ orbitals is comparatively weak. The optical response exhibits pronounced polarization dependence. The in-plane dielectric function displays a strong Drude-like response and negative values of the real dielectric function at low photon energies, whereas the out-of-plane component remains positive throughout the investigated energy range. Consistently, the absorption coefficient, extinction coefficient, reflectivity, and electron energy-loss spectra reveal a pronounced optical anisotropy. The calculated plasma frequencies are approximately $3.21$~eV for the in-plane polarization and $1.06$~eV for the out-of-plane polarization, indicating substantially stronger collective electronic excitations within the atomic plane. These findings demonstrate that $α$-GDY combines Dirac-like electronic behavior with highly anisotropic optical properties, highlighting its potential for polarization-sensitive optoelectronic, plasmonic, and nanoelectronic applications.

</details>


### [132] [Atomistic study of finite temperature properties in ferroelectric BiAlO$_3$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03842)
*Indranil Mal,Chandan Kumar Vishwakarma,Mohd Zeeshan,I. Ponomareva,B. K. Mani*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper develops a first-principles-based atomistic model to study BiAlO₃, a lead-free perovskite ferroelectric, predicting its rhombohedral ferroelectric ground state (R3c), a spontaneous polarization of 81 μC/cm² along [111], a Curie temperature of 1160 K, strong ferroelectric-antiferrodistortive coupling, and phase transitions under various mechanical stresses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To search for a new lead-free perovskite of technological importance by accurately predicting the properties of BiAlO₃ under experimentally relevant conditions using first-principles modeling.

Method: A first-principles-based atomistic model is developed to simulate BiAlO₃ properties, predicting structural phase transitions, polarization, Curie temperature, mode coupling, and responses to hydrostatic pressure, uniaxial stress, and biaxial stress.

Result: Predicts a rhombohedral ferroelectric (R3c) ground state with spontaneous polarization of 81 μC/cm² (along [111]), Curie temperature of 1160 K, strong ferroelectric-antiferrodistortive mode coupling, suppression of polarization and Curie temperature under hydrostatic pressure, and multiple phase transitions under uniaxial and biaxial stresses.

Conclusion: The model successfully captures experimental observations and reveals key properties and stress-induced phase behavior of BiAlO₃, highlighting its potential as a lead-free ferroelectric material.

Abstract: The lead-free perovskite ferroelectrics captivate researchers with their unique functional properties leading to important technological applications. In the search for a new lead-free perovskite of technological importance, we develop a first-principles based atomistic model to accurately predict the properties of BiAlO$_3$ in experimentally relevant conditions. Consistent with the experimental observations, our simulations predict a rhombohedral ferroelectric ($R3c$) ground state for BiAlO$_3$ facilitated by a structural phase transition from paraelectric (cubic, $Pm\Bar{3}m$) phase. The room-temperature spontaneous polarization and Curie temperature are obtained to be 81 $μ$C/cm$^2$ (along [111] direction) and 1160 K, respectively. Our simulations reveal strong coupling between ferroelectric and antiferrodistortive modes for a broad spectrum of temperature and electric field. We find that hydrostatic pressure suppresses both spontaneous polarization and Curie temperature, while both uniaxial and biaxial stresses induce multiple phase transitions in BiAlO$_3$.

</details>


### [133] [Surface Functionalization Enables Two-Dimensional Altermagnetism and Giant Tunnel Magnetoresistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03908)
*Zhou Cui,Ziye Zhu,Bowen Hao,Xunkai Duan,Xuan Zhou,Yali Xie,Huali Yang,Baisheng Sa,Runwei Li,Tong Zhou*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper proposes surface functionalization as a symmetry-guided chemical strategy to convert 2D antiferromagnets into altermagnets. Using monolayer FeSe, first-principles calculations show that hydrogenation, oxidation, and fluorination induce d-wave altermagnetic spin splitting. Device simulations demonstrate giant tunnel magnetoresistance up to 1.87×10³% in functionalized FeSe magnetic tunnel junctions, establishing a symmetry-to-chemistry-to-device approach for engineering 2D altermagnets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 2D altermagnets are highly desirable for spintronics due to combining compensated magnetic order, momentum-dependent spin splitting, and 2D advantages, but practical 2D AMs remain scarce. Rather than searching for intrinsic 2D AMs, the authors aim to transform known 2D antiferromagnets into AMs through symmetry engineering via surface functionalization.

Method: The authors propose surface functionalization as a symmetry-guided, nonvolatile chemical switch. By single-sided functionalization (hydrogenation, oxidation, fluorination), they break inversion and out-of-plane mirror symmetries while preserving rotation symmetry connecting opposite-spin sublattices. They use first-principles calculations on monolayer FeSe and transport simulations for magnetic tunnel junctions.

Result: Functionalization converts spin-degenerate antiferromagnetic FeSe into a d-wave altermagnet with pronounced momentum-dependent spin splitting. Transport simulations show giant TMR up to 1.87×10³% in functionalized FeSe MTJs, originating from momentum-selective spin filtering. TMR strongly depends on functionalization geometry.

Conclusion: This work establishes a symmetry-to-chemistry-to-device strategy for engineering 2D altermagnets, demonstrating that surface chemistry provides an effective control knob for altermagnetic transport and enables high-performance altermagnetic spintronic devices.

Abstract: Two-dimensional (2D) altermagnets (AMs) are highly desirable for ultrafast, stray-field-free spintronics because they combine compensated magnetic order and momentum-dependent spin splitting with the scalability, tunability, and interface compatibility of atomically thin materials. However, practical 2D AMs remain scarce. Rather than relying solely on the search for intrinsic 2D AMs, an appealing route is to transform known 2D antiferromagnets (AFMs) into AMs through symmetry engineering. Here, we propose surface functionalization as a symmetry-guided, nonvolatile chemical switch for realizing this AFM-to-AM transformation. By breaking inversion and out-of-plane mirror symmetries while preserving the rotation symmetry connecting opposite-spin sublattices, single-sided functionalization lifts spin degeneracy and induces altermagnetic spin splitting. Using monolayer FeSe as a representative platform, first-principles calculations show that hydrogenation, oxidation, and fluorination convert spin-degenerate antiferromagnetic FeSe into a d-wave AM with pronounced momentum-dependent spin splitting. At the device level, our transport simulations reveal that the functionalized FeSe monolayer magnetic tunnel junctions exhibit giant tunnel magnetoresistance (TMR) up to $1.87\times10^3\%$, originating from momentum-selective spin filtering between parallel and antiparallel Néel-vector configurations. The strong dependence of TMR on functionalization geometry further demonstrates that surface chemistry provides an effective control knob for altermagnetic transport. Our work establishes a symmetry-to-chemistry-to-device strategy for engineering 2D AMs and developing high-performance altermagnetic spintronic devices.

</details>


### [134] [Electronic Structure, Optical Response, Thermal and Mechanical Behavior of B6X (X = S, Se) under Pressure: A Comprehensive Ab-initio Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04116)
*Sourav Kumar Sutradhar,Tanvir Khan,Tauhidur Rahman,Sraboni Saha Moly,Mst. Maskura Khatun,S. H. Naqib*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study uses DFT to investigate pressure-dependent structural, electronic, optical, mechanical, and bonding properties of orthorhombic B6S and B6Se, finding them to be mechanically robust, indirect bandgap semiconductors with potential as thermal barrier coating materials under harsh environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To comprehensively understand the pressure-dependent properties of boron-rich chalcogenides B6S and B6Se for potential applications in harsh environments.

Method: Density functional theory (DFT) calculations across a wide range of hydrostatic pressures, analyzing elastic constants, electronic band structure, density of states, optical spectra, phonon dispersion, hardness, and thermal properties.

Result: Both B6S and B6Se are mechanically robust with strong deformation resistance, exhibit indirect bandgap semiconducting behavior, show pressure-induced spectral shifts in visible/UV regions, and are dynamically stable under pressure. They possess significant mechanical strength and suitable thermal properties for thermal barrier coatings.

Conclusion: B6S and B6Se are promising candidates for applications under harsh environments, particularly as excellent thermal barrier coating materials.

Abstract: This study presents a comprehensive investigation of the pressure dependent structural, electronic, optical, mechanical, and bonding properties of orthorhombic boron rich chalcogenides B6S and B6Se. Calculations were performed using density functional theory across a wide range of hydrostatic pressures. The computed elastic constants, bulk, Young, shear moduli, and Poisson ratio, revealed mechanical robustness and strong resistance to deformation, even under significant compression. Electronic band structure and density of states analyses indicate that the materials exhibit indirect bandgap semiconducting behavior. Optical results reveal clear pressure induced spectral shifts, particularly in the visible and ultraviolet regions, suggesting modified light matter interaction under compression. Phonon dispersion curves verified the dynamical stability of both materials within the investigated pressure range. Hardness estimations, combined with elastic parameters, and melting temperatures, further indicate that B6S and B6Se possess significant mechanical strength suitable for applications under harsh environments. The thermal properties suggest that both these compounds possess features suitable to be used as excellent thermal barrier coating materials.

</details>


### [135] [Tellurium sublattice instability driven amorphization in the chalcogenide AgSbTe2 under pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04200)
*Baihong Sun,Zihan Zhang,Wei Luo,Sergei Grazhdannikov,Wenting Lu,Shiyu Feng,Haikai Zou,Chenxin Wei,Martin Kunz,Hirokazu Kadobayashi,Bihang Wang,Azkar Saeed Ahmad,Yaron Amouyal,Rajeev Ahuja,Elissaios Stavrou*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: High-pressure study of AgSbTe₂ reveals pressure-driven amorphization via near-degenerate R3m and Im3m phases, caused by Te sublattice instability, with counterintuitive kinetic control where slow decompression yields amorphous state and fast decompression yields crystalline state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the microscopic origin of pressure-induced amorphization in complex chalcogenides, specifically AgSbTe₂, where the mechanism remains elusive.

Method: Combined synchrotron X-ray diffraction, density functional theory (DFT), and molecular dynamics (MD) calculations up to 60 GPa.

Result: Pressure drives transformation from ambient R-3m → intermediate amorphous → disordered cubic Im3m phase. Amorphization arises from near-degenerate enthalpy of R3m and Im3m structures and Te sublattice displacement instability, not cation vacancies. Decompression rate controls final state: slow decompression → amorphous, fast decompression → crystalline.

Conclusion: The amorphization mechanism in AgSbTe₂ is governed by structural degeneracy and Te sublattice instability, with unexpected kinetic effects that allow tuning between amorphous and crystalline states via decompression rate.

Abstract: Pressure provides a powerful thermodynamic route to access hidden structural states in functional materials, yet the microscopic origin of pressure-induced amorphization remains elusive in many complex chalcogenides. Here we report a detailed high-pressure structural study of AgSbTe2,combining synchrotron X-ray diffraction with density functional theory and molecular dynamics calculations up to 60 GPa. We uncover a pressure-driven transformation from the ambient R-3m phase to a fully disordered cubic Im3m phase, through an extended intermediate amorphous state. Enthalpy calculations reveal a near-degeneracy between the R3m and Im3m structures over a broad pressure range, dictating amorphization. Contrary to previously speculated cation vacancies, the amorphization is governed by a pronounced displacement instability of the Te sublattice. Remarkably, the time dependent decompression pathway controls the final structural state, resulting in either amorphous (slow decompression) or fully crystalline (fast decompression) states, indicative of a strong counterintuitive kinetic effect.

</details>


### [136] [First-principles Floquet analysis from real-time propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04269)
*Ruipeng Li,Benshu Fan,Umberto De Giovannini,Hannes Hübener,Angel Rubio*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A real-time Floquet analysis method is introduced that extracts quasi-energies and Floquet states from propagated wavefunctions via the one-period evolution operator, avoiding explicit Floquet Hamiltonian construction. An unfolding procedure based on harmonic decomposition resolves reduced-zone ambiguities and recovers equilibrium band character. Demonstrated on first-principles simulations of 2D monolayers, 3D bulk semiconductors, and finite pulses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To connect real-time time-dependent simulations with Floquet observables without constructing the computationally expensive enlarged Floquet Hamiltonian, enabling practical analysis of light-driven electronic structure in materials.

Method: Reconstructs the one-period evolution operator from overlaps between time-evolved states; introduces an unfolding procedure via harmonic decomposition of Floquet states to resolve reduced-zone representation ambiguities and recover equilibrium band character.

Result: Demonstrated applicability on first-principles simulations of 2D monolayers, 3D bulk semiconductors, and finite pulses without strict time periodicity, with negligible computational overhead added to time-dependent simulations.

Conclusion: The framework directly connects real-time simulations with Floquet observables, enabling practical analysis of light-driven electronic structure, including symmetry properties of light-induced sidebands, across various material systems.

Abstract: We present a real-time Floquet analysis method for extracting quasi-energies and Floquet states directly from propagated wavefunctions. By reconstructing the one-period evolution operator from overlaps between time-evolved states, the method avoids the explicit construction of the enlarged Floquet Hamiltonian and adds negligible computational overhead to time-dependent simulations. To resolve the ambiguity inherent in the reduced-zone representation, we introduce an unfolding procedure based on the harmonic decomposition of Floquet states, which recovers their underlying equilibrium band character beyond the reduced Floquet Brillouin zone. The reconstructed wavefunctions further provide access to the symmetry properties of individual light-induced sidebands. We demonstrate the applicability and generality of the method in first-principles time-dependent simulations of real materials, ranging from two-dimensional monolayers to a three-dimensional bulk semiconductor, and including finite pulses without strict time periodicity. This framework directly connects real-time simulations with Floquet observables, enabling practical analysis of light-driven electronic structure in materials.

</details>


### [137] [VASP Plugins: Linking the Vienna ab-initio Simulation Package with Python](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04622)
*Sudarshan Vijay,Martijn Marsman,Georg Kresse,Martin Schlipf*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper presents a Python plugin infrastructure for VASP that uses a C++ intermediate layer and pybind11 to expose DFT data as NumPy arrays, enabling flexible and efficient prototyping of new features like implicit solvent models and dispersion corrections while maintaining high performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Implementing novel features and experimental algorithms in production DFT codes (e.g., VASP) is hindered by complex legacy architectures and compiled languages like Fortran, creating barriers for rapid prototyping and software development.

Method: A Python plugin infrastructure using a C++ intermediate layer and pybind11 to expose VASP data as NumPy arrays via shared memory buffers, supporting two plugin categories: post-SCF-cycle plugins (structure, force_and_stress) and intra-SCF-cycle plugins (local_potential, occupancies).

Result: Demonstrated utility through three applications: structure relaxation using scipy, implementing an implicit solvent model, and adding DFT-D4 dispersion corrections.

Conclusion: This infrastructure effectively bridges the gap between high-performance electronic structure routines and the widespread scientific Python ecosystem, enabling rapid prototyping without compromising computational efficiency.

Abstract: Implementing novel features and experimental algorithms into widely adopted density functional theory (DFT) codes is frequently hindered by complex legacy architectures and the use of compiled languages such as Fortran. These production codes, while optimised for high-performance computing clusters, present significant hurdles for software development and rapid prototyping, often requiring deep expertise in the code's internal structure to modify. To address this challenge, we present a Python plugin infrastructure for the Vienna ab-initio Simulation Package (VASP) that combines computational efficiency with the flexibility of high-level scripting. Our architecture uses a C++ intermediate layer and pybind11 to expose VASP data as NumPy arrays via shared memory buffers, ensuring high performance without data duplication. We implement two categories of plugins: those that modify quantities at the end of each converged self-consistent field (SCF) cycle, such as structure and force_and_stress, and those that operate during the SCF cycle, such as local_potential and occupancies. We demonstrate the utility of our implementation through three applications, structure relaxation using the scipy library, implementing an implicit solvent model, and adding the DFT-D4 dispersion corrections. This infrastructure effectively bridges the gap between high-performance electronic structure routines and the widespread scientific Python ecosystem.

</details>


### [138] [Chiral Phonons Coupled to Spin-Split Bands in Altermagnetic CrSb and MnTe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04802)
*Armando Consiglio,Maximilian Ünzelmann,Giancarlo Panaccione,Domenico Di Sante*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper shows that altermagnets CrSb and MnTe host chiral phonon modes with f-wave symmetry in momentum space, where chiral lattice dynamics and altermagnetic spin splitting originate from different atomic sublattices. Chemical substitution can lift inversion symmetry to generate finite valley phonon chirality, and chiral phonons couple to spin-split electronic bands via momentum-dependent electron-phonon interaction, establishing altermagnets as a platform for chiral phononics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the interplay between chiral phonon modes and altermagnetic order in prototypical altermagnets CrSb and MnTe, and to explore how chiral lattice dynamics can couple to momentum-dependent spin-split electronic states.

Method: First-principles calculations on CrSb and MnTe to analyze phonon angular momentum, chiral lattice dynamics, sublattice contributions, and electron-phonon coupling; isoelectronic chemical substitution to break inversion symmetry.

Result: CrSb and MnTe host locally chiral phonon modes with six-lobes f-wave texture of phonon angular momentum in momentum space. Chiral lattice motion originates from pnictogen/chalcogen sublattice, while altermagnetic spin splitting comes from transition-metal sublattice. Inversion symmetry suppresses net phonon angular momentum at valleys; chemical substitution lifts cancellation and generates finite valley phonon chirality. Chiral phonons couple to spin-split bands via momentum-dependent electron-phonon interaction.

Conclusion: Altermagnets are a promising platform for chiral phononics and spin-selective lattice control, with potential experimental accessibility via photoemission.

Abstract: Altermagnets exhibit momentum-dependent spin splitting without net magnetization, providing a unique platform where magnetic order, electronic structure and lattice dynamics intertwine. Here, using first-principles calculations, we demonstrate that the prototypical altermagnets CrSb and MnTe host locally chiral phonon modes carrying finite phonon angular momentum with a six-lobes $f$-wave texture in momentum space. Our results show that the chiral lattice motion originates from the pnictogen/chalcogen sublattice, while the altermagnetic spin splitting is generated by the magnetic transition-metal atoms, indicating that chiral lattice dynamics and altermagnetic electronic states originate from different atomic sublattices of the same crystal. In pristine compounds, at each valley, inversion symmetry suppresses the net phonon angular momentum despite local circular atomic motion. We further demonstrate that isoelectronic symmetry lowering induced by chemical substitution lifts this cancellation and generates finite valley phonon chirality, while keeping the altermagnetic nature of the compounds intact. Most importantly, we reveal that chiral phonons couple to momentum-dependent spin-split electronic bands through momentum-dependent electron-phonon interaction, producing characteristic modifications of the electronic structure, possibly accessible by photoemission experiments. Our results establish altermagnets as a promising platform for chiral phononics and spin-selective lattice control.

</details>


### [139] [Formation and Thermodynamic Behavior of THF-Water Hydrates in Confined Mesoporous Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04813)
*Armin Mozhdehei,Oriana Osta,Thomas Marescot,Arnaud Desmedt,Denis Morineau,Christiane Alba-Simionesco*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates how composition, pore filling, and cooling rate govern the formation of sII clathrate hydrate (THF·17H₂O) in SBA-15 mesoporous silica using DSC and WAXS. Confined hydrate forms only at near-percolating pore filling (φ ≈ 1.0–1.1 cm³/g) and sufficient THF concentration (≥ 1:16 mol:mol). Slower cooling increases hydrate fraction at the expense of ice. Gibbs-Thomson analysis quantitatively links melting-point depressions to crystalline size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve how confinement in mesoporous silica (SBA-15) affects the formation and phase behavior of structure-II clathrate hydrate, and to establish the thermodynamic and kinetic conditions under which hydrate competes with ice in nanopores.

Method: Combined differential scanning calorimetry (DSC) and wide-angle X-ray scattering (WAXS) on bulk and confined systems, varying pore filling (φ), THF:water ratio, and cooling rate (1.0 vs 0.5 °C/min), with temperature cycling and Gibbs-Thomson analysis.

Result: Confined hydrate dissociates at −13.2 ± 0.2 °C and confined ice melts at −14.7 ± 0.2 °C. Hydrate forms only at φ ≥ ~1.0 cm³/g and THF ≥ 1:16. Slower cooling enhances hydrate fraction (e.g., +60% at 1:11, +54% at 1:14) but ≤ 17% at 1:16. Peak positions are invariant under cycling. WAXS confirms the confined phase is crystallographically sII. ΔT vs 1/r follows Gibbs-Thomson for both ice and hydrate.

Conclusion: Capillarity and composition, not thermal history, set the liquidus and dissociation temperatures in confinement. Slower cooling favors hydrate over ice by promoting nucleation. The Gibbs-Thomson framework quantitatively captures the size-dependent melting/dissociation shifts, clarifying the competition between hydrate formation and ice crystallization in nanopores.

Abstract: Tetrahydrofuran (THF) is a benchmark guest for probing clathrate hydrate thermodynamics because a stoichiometric aqueous solution (THF.17H2O) forms structure-II (sII) hydrate at ambient pressure with a well-defined dissociation temperature. Here, we combine differential scanning calorimetry (DSC) and wide-angle X-ray scattering (WAXS) in bulk and confined media to resolve how composition, pore filling, and cooling rate govern hydrate formation in SBA-15 mesoporous silica. Bulk DSC establishes mass-balanced enthalpies for ice and sII hydrate and confirms reversible dissociation/melting temperatures. In confinement, the heating traces separate into a Gibbs-Thomson depressed ice melt (= -14.7 $\pm$ 0.2 {\textdegree}C), an in-pore hydrate dissociation (= -13.2 $\pm$ 0.2 {\textdegree}C). Confined hydrate appears only when two criteria are met: near-percolating filling ($φ$ = 1.0 -1.1 cm3/g) and sufficient THF ($\ge$ 1:16 mol:mol). Cooling-rate experiments (1.0 vs 0.5 {\textdegree}C/min) demonstrate that slower precooling increases the confined-hydrate fraction and reduces confined ice without shifting equilibrium temperatures: at $φ$ = 1.1, the hydrate enthalpy rises by ~60% at 1:11 and ~54% at 1:14, but by $\le$ 17% at 1:16. Temperature-cycling tests show invariant reheating peak positions, indicating that capillarity and composition, rather than kinetic history, fix the liquidus and dissociation temperatures. WAXS indicates that the phase formed in pores is crystallographically identical to bulk sII. Finally, the variation of melting points ($Δ$T___) plotted against inverse pore radius follows the Gibbs-Thomson law for both ice melting and hydrate dissociation, quantitatively linking the observed shifts to crystalline size and clarifying how confinement, cooling rate, and composition govern the competition between hydrate formation and water crystallization.

</details>


### [140] [Glassy Dynamics of LiCl.6H2O Solution in Nanoporous Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04817)
*Armin Mozhdehei,Mohammad Nadim Kamar,Ronan Lefort,Alain Moréac,Arnaud Le Pottier,Agathe Belime,Denis Morineau*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates how nanoconfinement in 8 nm mesoporous silica alters the glass-forming behavior, hydrogen-bond network, and dynamics of LiCl·6H₂O, revealing suppressed mobility, broader heterogeneity, and slowed transport across timescales from glassy to liquid states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how nanoconfinement alters the dynamics of glass-forming aqueous electrolytes, clarifying the interplay among ionic hydration, hydrogen-bond structure, and interfacial effects.

Method: Combined DSC, Raman spectroscopy, quasielastic neutron scattering, ¹H spin-lattice relaxation, and PFG-NMR to probe thermal behavior, H-bond structure, local mobility, and translational transport over complementary time and length scales.

Result: Confinement broadens and shifts the glass transition upward; weakens the H-bond network; reduces mean-squared displacements and translational diffusion; increases residence times; broadens the distribution of local proton fluctuation times; and reduces long-range water mobility.

Conclusion: Nanoconfinement markedly alters LiCl·6H₂O dynamics across timescales, from glassy to liquid states, resulting in slower, spatially constrained, and more heterogeneous motions, without resolving distinct interfacial and pore-centered populations.

Abstract: Understanding how nanoconfinement alters the dynamics of glass-forming aqueous electrolytes is essential for clarifying the interplay among ionic hydration, hydrogen-bond structure, and interfacial effects. Here, LiCl.6H2O was investigated in the bulk and under confinement in SBA-15 mesoporous silica with an average pore diameter of 8 nm. Differential scanning calorimetry, Raman spectroscopy, quasielastic neutron scattering, 1 H spin-lattice relaxation, and pulsed-fieldgradient NMR were combined to probe thermal behavior, hydrogen-bond structure, local mobility, and translational transport over complementary time and length scales. The calorimetric results show that LiCl.6H2O remains glass-forming under confinement, while its thermal signature of the glass transition becomes slightly broader and shifted upward relative to the bulk. Raman spectra in the O-H stretching region indicate that the concentrated LiCl solution possesses a weakened and less tetrahedrally connected hydrogen-bond network compared with bulk water. On the subnanosecond timescale, elastic fixed-window analysis reveals reduced mean-squared displacements under confinement, demonstrating suppressed motional amplitudes inside the pores. Inelastic fixed-window neutron scattering scans analyzed within a jump-diffusion framework yield lower effective translational diffusion coefficients and longer residence times for the confined liquid, indicating that confinement mainly hinders translational escape from transient local environments.  1 H relaxometry further shows that confinement broadens the distribution of local proton fluctuation times, while PFG-NMR confirms that the measured long-range water mobility in bulk LiCl.6H2O solution is reduced relative to bulk water. While the present data do not resolve distinct interfacial and pore-centered populations in confined LiCl.6H2O, its dynamics are markedly altered across timescales, from the glassy to the liquid state, resulting in slower, spatially constrained, and more heterogeneous motions.

</details>


### [141] [Disentangling Electronic and Lattice Contributions to Transient Absorption in Metal Halide Perovskites: A First-Principles Study of CH3NH3PbBr3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04840)
*Lu Qiao,Ronaldo Rodrigues Pela,Claudia Draxl*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper develops a first-principles approach combining nonequilibrium Bethe-Salpeter equation with ab initio molecular dynamics to disentangle electronic and thermal lattice contributions in transient absorption spectra of metal halide perovskites, using CH3NH3PbBr3 as a model system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Soft lattices and strong electron-phonon coupling in metal halide perovskites create a complex interplay between electronic and lattice degrees of freedom, complicating the interpretation of time-resolved excitation spectra like pump-probe spectra.

Method: The approach combines a nonequilibrium extension of the Bethe-Salpeter equation with ab initio molecular dynamics to resolve the origin of transient absorption across femtosecond-to-picosecond timescales.

Result: On femtosecond scale, X-ray and optical transient absorption spectra are dominated by electronic contributions: photoinduced Coulomb screening weakens electron-hole interaction and blueshifts excitonic resonances, while Pauli blocking is negligible. On picosecond scale, thermal lattice contributions dominate: lattice vibrations cause spectral redistribution in X-ray spectra, while lattice expansion blueshifts optical spectra.

Conclusion: The developed first-principles approach can quantitatively disentangle electronic and thermal lattice contributions in transient absorption spectra across different timescales.

Abstract: Soft lattices combined with strong electron-phonon coupling in metal halide perovskites result in a complex interplay between electronic and lattice degrees of freedom. This interplay complicates the interpretation of time-resolved excitation spectra like pump-probe spectra. Here, we develop a first-principles approach that combines a nonequilibrium extension of the Bethe-Salpeter equation with \textit{ab initio} molecular dynamics to resolve the origin of transient absorption. This approach can quantitatively disentangle electronic and thermal lattice contributions across femtosecond-to-picosecond timescales. Exemplified with \ce{CH3NH3PbBr3}, we find that on the femtosecond scale, both X-ray and optical transient absorption spectra are dominated by electronic contributions: Photoinduced Coulomb screening weakens the effective electron-hole interaction and blueshifts the excitonic resonances, whereas Pauli blocking is negligible. On the picosecond scale, thermal lattice contributions become essential, with distinct mechanisms dominating different spectral regions: Lattice vibrations lead to spectral redistribution in the X-ray transient absorption spectrum, whereas lattice expansion blueshifts the optical transient absorption spectrum.

</details>


### [142] [Different dielectric, magnetic, and magnetodielectric mechanisms in M-type BaFe12O19 hexaferrite regulated by doping Ga3+ and In3+ cations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04861)
*Yang Yang,Run-Yu Lei,Jian-Ping Zhou,Xiao-Ming Chen*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study investigates the magnetic, dielectric, and magnetodielectric (MD) properties of BaFe12-xMexO19 (Me = Ga, In) ceramics. Ga³⁺ substitutes Fe³⁺ in FeO₆ octahedra, while In³⁺ replaces Fe³⁺ in FeO₅ bipyramids. Pure and Ga-doped samples show ferrimagnetism; In-doped samples exhibit a non-collinear to collinear magnetic transition. Dielectric and MD behaviors arise from different mechanisms depending on doping, temperature, and field, including quantum paraelectric state, electron hopping, dipole glass, Maxwell-Wagner effect, spin-phonon coupling, and non-collinear spin ordering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically understand how different cation substitutions (Ga³⁺ vs. In³⁺) at Fe sites in M-type barium hexaferrite influence the magnetic, dielectric, and magnetodielectric coupling mechanisms across a wide temperature range.

Method: BaFe12-xMexO19 (Me = Ga, In) ceramics were prepared via solid-state reaction. Structural analysis included site occupancy determination for Ga³⁺ (octahedral FeO₆) and In³⁺ (bipyramidal FeO₅). Magnetic, dielectric, and MD properties were measured from 10 K to 300 K under various conditions.

Result: Pure and Ga-doped samples remain ferrimagnetic down to 10 K, while In-doped samples show a transition from non-collinear to collinear ferrimagnetism. Dielectric behavior varies: quantum paraelectric state in pure (10–175 K), dipole glass in Ga-doped (20–40 K), and electron hopping in In-doped at low T. MD effects show sign changes: negative MD at low T in pure and In samples (spin-phonon coupling/non-collinear spin ordering), positive MD upon warming (electron hopping), and positive MD in Ga samples (field-dependent dipoles in FeO₅ bipyramids). At high T, MD arises from magnetoresistance + Maxwell-Wagner effect.

Conclusion: The magnetic, dielectric, and MD properties of BaFe12O19 are strongly tunable by the choice of dopant site occupancy. Different mechanisms dominate depending on temperature and doping, providing a systematic understanding for designing magnetodielectric materials with tailored responses.

Abstract: We systematically investigated the magnetic, dielectric, and MD properties of BaFe12-xMexO19 ceramics prepared by a solid-state reaction method. The Ga3+ cations with a smaller radius preferentially substitute the Fe3+ ions in FeO6 octahedra while the In3+ cations with a larger radius tend to replace the Fe3+ ions in FeO5 bipyramids of R blocks, inducing different physical characteristics. The pure BaFe12O19 and Ga-doped samples show ferrimagnetism in the temperature range from 10 K to 300 K. The In-doped samples exhibit a transition from non-collinear magnetism to collinear ferrimagnetism. The dielectric decrease of pure BaFe12O19 at around 10-175 K is attributed to the quantum paraelectric state, and the shoulder peaks of loss at about 140-200 K are from electron hopping. The dipole glass state is responsible for the dielectric peak of Ga-doped samples at around 20-40 K. The dielectric increase and plateau of In-doped samples are mainly ascribed to the electron hopping at low temperatures. Their dielectric properties at high temperatures are all attributed to the interfacial polarization caused by the Maxwell-Wagner effect. The MD effect also has different origins for the various samples at low temperatures. For the pure BaFe12O19, the negative MD effect at extremely low temperatures and the positive MD effect after warming are ascribed to spin-phonon coupling and field-dependent electron hopping, respectively. The positive MD effect in Ga-doped hexaferrites results from the field-dependent electric dipoles inside FeO5 bipyramids. For the In-doped samples, the negative MD effect and subsequent transformation to the positive MD effect originate from the field-dependent non-collinear spin ordering and electron hopping, respectively. The MD effect at high temperatures is attributed to the combination of magnetoresistance and Maxwell-Wagner effects.

</details>


### [143] [A Distributional Framework for Generative Modeling of Molecular Crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05266)
*Michael Kilgour,Alex Dong,Mark E. Tuckerman,Jutta Rogal*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: MXtalGFlow uses energy-based generative flow networks to learn Boltzmann distributions over molecular crystal structures, enabling probabilistic sampling beyond isolated low-energy candidates and revealing both known polymorphs and competitive unreported packing modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Molecular crystals are highly polymorphic, making structure and property prediction challenging. Existing crystal structure prediction methods treat candidates in isolation rather than as samples from a thermodynamic distribution, and generative approaches lack a concrete formulation of the distributions being sampled.

Method: The authors develop a canonical parameterization for crystal structures and train energy-based generative flow networks (GFlowNets) to approximate the Boltzmann distribution over crystal structures for target molecules and space groups. The framework uses two energy functions (Lennard-Jones potential and Universal Model for Atoms) to define the target distribution.

Result: MXtalGFlow yields thermodynamic distributions over crystal structures for two molecules under two energy functions. The model characterizes local structural basins around known polymorphs and identifies additional unreported packing modes with competitive probabilities to experimental structures.

Conclusion: MXtalGFlow demonstrates how to define and train a model to sample a thermodynamically meaningful distribution of molecular crystals, enabling useful analysis of such distributions beyond traditional low-energy structure enumeration.

Abstract: Molecular crystals are a highly polymorphic class of materials, with a single molecule commonly crystallizing via multiple packing patterns, making structure and property prediction very challenging. Crystal structure prediction typically comprises the production of sets of promising candidate structures, each considered in isolation rather than as samples in a thermodynamic distribution. Likewise, modern generative approaches to this problem, despite naturally sampling distributions of crystals, lack a concrete formulation of the distributions being sampled. Two components are required to impart meaning to the distributions of crystals generated under such models: a canonical parameterization, and a loss function which equilibrates the generated samples to some target distribution. We develop such a parameterization, and train energy-based generative flow networks (GFlowNets) to approximate the Boltzmann distribution over crystal structures for target molecules and space groups. Combined, these components comprise our MXtalGFlow framework for molecular crystal modeling. Going beyond sampling disconnected sets of low-energy structures, MXtalGFlow yields a thermodynamic distribution over crystal structures. We sample and analyze distributions of crystals for two molecules, each under two energy functions, a Lennard-Jones potential and the Universal Model for Atoms. We characterize the local structural basins about the known polymorphs, and identify additional as-yet un-reported packing modes with competitive probabilities to the known experimental structures. With MXtalGFlow, we illustrate how to define and train a model to sample a thermodynamically meaningful distribution of molecular crystals, and analyze such a distribution to glean useful information.

</details>


### [144] [Semiconductor nanofilms as thermal phonon polarizers: competing effects of scattering selection rules and boundary mode conversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05296)
*Vasumathy Ravishankar,Navaneetha K. Ravichandran*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Phonon scattering selection rules, traditionally known to control heat flow in bulk solids, also modulate heat flow in nanoscale semiconductor films through a competition with phonon mode conversion at boundaries, leading to mode-polarized heat currents at cryogenic temperatures that enable symmetry-selective phonon engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate that phonon scattering selection rules, which govern heat flow in bulk solids, also operate in nanoscale semiconductor films via a previously unexplored mechanism involving competition with boundary mode conversion.

Method: First-principles calculations to analyze the competition between phonon scattering selection rules and phonon mode conversion at boundaries of nanoscale semiconductor films.

Result: Mode-polarized heat currents emerge at cryogenic temperatures (≤100 K); in materials like indium phosphide, large velocity differences between phonon branches amplify LA phonon contribution to thermal conductivity by restricting intrinsic scattering, while boundary mode conversion suppresses it by depopulating LA phonons.

Conclusion: Transverse-polarized non-equilibrium phonons in nanoscale films enable symmetry-selective engineering of phonon coupling to electrons, strains, and defects—capabilities difficult to achieve in bulk solids.

Abstract: Phonon scattering selection rules are known to control heat flow through bulk solids. Here we show that these selection rules also modulate heat flow through nanoscale semiconductor films, although through a previously-unexplored mechanism. Using first-principles calculations, we expose a competition between these selection rules and phonon mode conversion at boundaries of nanoscale films, that drives mode-polarized heat currents at cryogenic temperatures ($\le$ 100 K). This polarizing effect is stronger in materials like indium phosphide, where selection rules based on large velocity differences between phonon branches amplifies the longitudinal acoustic (LA) phonon contribution to thermal conductivity by restricting their intrinsic scattering events, while boundary mode conversion in nanoscale films suppresses it by depopulating the LA phonons. The resulting transverse-polarized non-equilibrium phonons will enable symmetry-selective engineering of phonon coupling to electrons, strains and defects in nanoscale films, that is difficult to achieve in bulk solids.

</details>


### [145] [High-temperature operation of III-nitride high-electron-mobility transistors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05314)
*Yi-Chen Liu,Jacklyn Zhu,John Niroula,Hridibrata Pal,Tomas Palacios,Savannah R. Eisner*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the high-temperature operation of III-nitride HEMTs, examining effects on material properties, device structure, and circuit-level behavior, while evaluating thermal stability across logic, RF, and power applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: III-nitride HEMTs are promising for high-temperature applications (power electronics, aerospace, space exploration), but understanding their thermal limits and performance evolution under extreme conditions is essential for reliable deployment.

Method: The paper analyzes the impact of high temperature on material properties, device structure, and circuit-level behavior; explores mitigation strategies including barrier/channel engineering, substrate selection, and passivation; and evaluates thermal stability in logic, RF, and power electronics contexts.

Result: Identification of how critical device layers (barrier, channel, substrate, passivation) mitigate high-temperature-induced degradation, and assessment of thermal stability across multiple application domains.

Conclusion: Key remaining challenges in designing and optimizing III-nitride HEMTs for high-temperature operation are highlighted, indicating areas requiring further research.

Abstract: High-electron-mobility transistors (HEMTs) made with III-nitride materials are of potential use in high-temperature electronic applications including power electronics, communications, aerospace and space exploration. However, the demands of such applications make it essential to understand the thermal limits and performance evolution of III-nitride HEMTs. Here, we analyze the high-temperature operation of III-nitride HEMTs, examining the impact on material properties, device structure, and circuit-level behavior. We explore the role of critical device layers - including barrier and channel engineering, substrate selection, and passivation strategies - in mitigating high-temperature-induced effects, and evaluate the thermal stability of III-nitride HEMTs in logic, radiofrequency, and power electronics applications. We also highlight key remaining challenges in the design and optimization of III-nitride devices for high-temperature applications.

</details>


### [146] [Magnetotransport and electronic band structure of EuNi$_2$As$_2$ antiferromagnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05337)
*Faheem Gul,Mane Sahakiyan,Orest Pavlosiuk,Piotr Wiśniewski*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper studies magnetotransport in single-crystal EuNi₂As₂, an antiferromagnetic van der Waals compound with helical magnetic ordering below 14.6 K, revealing metamagnetic transitions, complex magnetoresistance, hole-dominated multi-band conduction, and electronic structure changes upon magnetic ordering via experiments and ab-initio calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the magnetotransport properties and electronic structure of the tetragonal van der Waals compound EuNi₂As₂, which exhibits antiferromagnetic helical ordering, and to understand the interplay between magnetic ordering, metamagnetic transitions, and transport phenomena.

Method: Magnetization, magnetoresistance, and Hall resistivity measurements on single-crystalline EuNi₂As₂, complemented by ab-initio electronic band structure calculations with Hubbard corrections.

Result: Metamagnetic transitions observed in magnetization and magnetoresistance; negative magnetoresistance above Néel temperature modeled by de Gennes-Friedel spin-disorder-scattering reduction; hole-dominated multi-band conductivity in the antiferromagnetic state and single-band above Néel temperature; carrier concentrations ~10²² cm⁻³; electronic structure changes strongly with magnetic ordering but DOS at Fermi level changes by less than a factor of two.

Conclusion: The metallic character of EuNi₂As₂ obscures topological contributions to Hall resistivity; Hubbard corrections significantly affect DOS, magnetic moments, and screening length of Eu-4f orbitals.

Abstract: We investigated the magnetotransport properties of single-crystals of tetragonal van der Waals compound EuNi$_2$As$_2$, that orders antiferromagnetically below 14.6 K in an incommensurate helical structure. Metamagnetic transitions are revealed by the magnetization measured in the magnetic field applied transverse to the axis of the helix, and are clearly reflected in the magnetoresistance. Overall, the magnetoresistance is small, but shows complex changes with the temperature, the strength, and the angle of the applied magnetic field. In magnetically ordered state, magnetoresistance shows prominent anomalies related to the metamagnetic transitions. For temperatures above the Néel point the negative magnetoresistance can be modeled very well with de Gennes-Friedel mechanism of the spin-disorder-scattering reduction. Hall resistivity data indicate hole-dominated multi-band conductivity in antiferromagnetic state and single-band one above the Néel temperature, with carrier concentrations of the order of 10$^{22}$cm$^{-3}$. This metallic character of the compound seems to obscure the plausible topological contribution to the Hall resistivity. Our \textit{ab-initio} calculations of electronic band structure showed that the electronic structure changes very strongly upon magnetic ordering, but the density of states at the Fermi level differs by a factor smaller than two, in agreement with experimental Hall resistivity data. Meaningful changes in the density of states, magnetic moments, and screening length of Eu-4$f$ orbitals are discussed in terms of the effects of Hubbard corrections.

</details>


<div id='physics.data-an'></div>

# physics.data-an [[Back]](#toc)

### [147] [Hybrid pattern recognition for charged particle tracking: Hough transform and convolutional neural efficiency networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04723)
*Carlo Varni,Krzysztof Ciesla,Marcin Wolter,Radoslaw Karol Mienkowski,Tomasz Bold*

Main category: physics.data-an

TL;DR: This paper presents a hybrid track-finding method combining Hough transform with neural networks to suppress false candidates in high-occupancy HL-LHC environments, achieving both efficiency and purity without data transformations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In high-occupancy environments like HL-LHC, the Hough transform produces many spurious track candidates, causing computational overhead. A method is needed to reduce false candidates while maintaining speed.

Method: A hybrid approach where Hough transform is used as a first-stage data preparation step to produce parameter space images, which are then fed into a neural network trained to suppress false track candidates. No data transformations between steps.

Result: Performance studies using Open Data Detector simulated in ACTS framework under realistic HL-LHC pileup conditions demonstrate the method's effectiveness.

Conclusion: The hybrid HT + neural network approach combines the speed of geometry-driven Hough transform with the discriminative power of machine learning, achieving both efficiency and purity in track candidate formation for high-occupancy environments.

Abstract: Reconstructing charged-particle tracks in silicon detectors is a central task in high-energy physics experiments and a key component of both offline reconstruction and online event selection. Within the reconstruction chain, the efficient and high-purity formation of track candidates plays a critical role in the overall performance. Among the many approaches developed over the years, the Hough transform (HT) has been widely studied as a fast geometry-driven method for track finding. However, in high-occupancy environments such as those expected at the High-Luminosity LHC (HL-LHC), the HT tends to produce a large number of spurious candidates, leading to increased computational overhead in subsequent reconstruction stages. In this work, we present a hybrid approach in which the HT serves as a first-stage data preparation step, providing its parameters space image as an input to a neural network trained to suppress false track candidates. The method combines the speed of the HT with the discriminative power of machine learning to achieve both efficiency and purity. In addition no data transformations are involved when combining these steps resulting in a simpler and more performant algorithm. Performance studies using the Open Data Detector simulated in the ACTS framework under realistic HL-LHC pileup conditions will be presented.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.str-el'></div>

# cond-mat.str-el [[Back]](#toc)

### [148] [Correlation-Induced Topological Reconstruction in a Periodically Driven Kagome Mott Insulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02717)
*Rahul Ghosh,Subhajyoti Pal,Ganesh C Paul*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper studies a driven Hubbard model on a Kagome lattice, finding that periodic driving renormalizes hopping and generates chiral terms, leading to repeated topological transitions and a tunable flat band. Within slave-rotor mean-field theory, the Mott transition becomes non-monotonic, producing repeated metal-insulator transitions and spinon excitations with a distinct band topology, enabling correlated topological flat-band physics out of equilibrium.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the interplay between electronic correlations and periodic driving (Floquet engineering) in a Kagome lattice, aiming to discover emergent correlated topological flat-band phases inaccessible by either mechanism alone.

Method: Brillouin-Wigner perturbation theory for the effective Floquet Hamiltonian, combined with slave-rotor mean-field theory to treat electronic correlations and the Mott transition.

Result: (1) Drive renormalizes hopping and generates chiral nearest- and next-nearest-neighbor terms, yielding repeated topological transitions and a continuously tunable flat band. (2) The Mott transition is strongly non-monotonic in drive amplitude, producing repeated metal-insulator transitions. (3) Spinon excitations inside the Mott phase acquire a band topology distinct from the non-interacting Floquet bands, yielding emergent flat-band spinon insulators.

Conclusion: Periodically driven Kagome systems serve as a platform for engineering correlated topological flat-band physics out of equilibrium, with proposed realizations in ultracold atomic lattices.

Abstract: We study the interplay between electronic correlations and circularly polarized periodic driving in the Hubbard model on a Kagome lattice. Using Brillouin-Wigner perturbation theory, we derive an effective Floquet Hamiltonian in which the drive renormalizes the bare hopping and generates chiral nearest- and next-nearest-neighbor terms, producing repeated topological transitions and a flat band whose position is continuously tunable across the spectrum. Within slave-rotor mean-field theory, we show that the resulting Mott transition is strongly non-monotonic in the drive amplitude, yielding repeated metal-insulator transitions, and that the spinon excitations inside the Mott phase acquire a band topology distinct from that of the non-interacting Floquet bands. This correlation-driven topological reconstruction produces emergent flat-band spinon insulators inaccessible to either driving or interactions alone. Our results establish periodically driven Kagome systems as a platform for engineering correlated topological flat-band physics out of equilibrium, with proposed realizations in ultracold atomic lattices.

</details>


### [149] [Quantum magnetism of the spin-1 kagome-lattice antiferromagnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03086)
*Katsuhiro Morita*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper studies the spin-1 kagome-lattice Heisenberg antiferromagnet using large-scale Lanczos diagonalization and finite-temperature Lanczos methods, revealing magnetization plateaus at m=0, 1/3, 7/9, and 8/9, identifying the m=0 plateau as a trimer valence-bond-crystal state and high-field plateaus as magnon crystals, with specific heat showing a double-peak structure and thermal effects smearing out high-field plateaus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the ground-state and finite-temperature properties of the spin-1 kagome-lattice Heisenberg antiferromagnet, which is a challenging strongly-frustrated quantum spin system, and to provide benchmark data for thermodynamic and high-field magnetization measurements in candidate materials.

Method: Large-scale Lanczos diagonalization (zero-temperature) and finite-temperature Lanczos method (finite-temperature), with Gaussian-kernel smoothing for constructing the magnetization curve.

Result: Magnetization plateaus at m=0, 1/3, 7/9, and 8/9; m=0 is a trimer valence-bond-crystal; high-field plateaus are magnon crystals (m=8/9 is exact localized-magnon crystal); specific heat has double-peak structure at T/J≈0.1 and ≈1.1; m=1/3 plateau persists at low T, while high-field plateaus are thermally smeared.

Conclusion: The results serve as benchmark data for thermodynamic and high-field magnetization measurements in candidate spin-1 kagome-lattice materials.

Abstract: We investigate the spin-1 kagome-lattice Heisenberg antiferromagnet using large-scale Lanczos diagonalization and the finite-temperature Lanczos method. The zero-temperature magnetization process exhibits plateaus at $m=0$, $1/3$, $7/9$, and $8/9$, where $m$ is the normalized magnetization. The $m=0$ plateau is identified as a trimer valence-bond-crystal state, while the high-field plateaus at $m=7/9$ and $8/9$ are identified as magnon crystals. In particular, the $m=8/9$ plateau corresponds to the exact localized-magnon crystal state. A smoothed zero-temperature magnetization curve constructed using the Gaussian-kernel smoothing method indicates magnetization jumps at the lower-field edge of the $m=1/3$ plateau and at the upper-field edges of the $m=7/9$ and $8/9$ plateaus. At finite temperatures, the specific heat exhibits a double-peak structure with peaks around $T/J\simeq0.1$ and $T/J\simeq1.1$, and the low-temperature peak may be related to trimer valence-bond-crystal ordering. The finite-temperature magnetization curves show that the $m=1/3$ plateau remains visible at low temperatures, whereas the high-field plateaus are rapidly smeared out by thermal effects. These results provide benchmark data for thermodynamic and high-field magnetization measurements in candidate spin-1 kagome-lattice materials.

</details>


### [150] [Winding charge density wave: intertwining of structural chirality and phase topology of electronic order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03102)
*Shun Asano,Youichi Yanase*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper introduces a class of chiral charge density waves (winding CDWs) that exhibit macroscopic chirality from a single ordering wavevector, driven by chiral phonons in screw-symmetric crystals, and extends the concept to achiral crystals where spontaneous symmetry breaking enables an achiral-to-chiral phase transition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a fundamental link between the geometry of chiral crystal structures and the phase topology of electronic orders, specifically explaining how macroscopic chirality can emerge in charge density waves from a single wavevector.

Method: The authors propose a theoretical framework based on chiral phonon-driven Peierls instability in screw-symmetric chiral crystals, where selection rules governing electron-phonon coupling determine a definite crystal angular momentum channel, endowing the CDW with integer azimuthal phase winding.

Result: Identification of winding CDWs as a new class of chiral order; demonstration that achiral crystals with discrete rotational symmetry can stabilize winding CDWs via spontaneous symmetry breaking, leading to an achiral-to-chiral phase transition.

Conclusion: The work reveals a fundamental connection between chiral structure geometry and electronic order phase topology, with implications for understanding chirality in condensed matter systems.

Abstract: We propose a class of chiral charge density waves (CDWs), dubbed winding CDWs, that exhibit macroscopic chirality despite a single ordering wavevector. In screw-symmetric chiral crystals, chiral phonons drive a Peierls instability that selects a definite crystal angular momentum channel, thereby endowing the CDW with an integer azimuthal phase winding dictated by the selection rule governing electron-phonon coupling. We further extend this framework to achiral crystals with discrete rotational symmetry and demonstrate that spontaneous symmetry breaking stabilizes a winding CDW with either handedness, realizing an achiral-to-chiral phase transition. Our results reveal a fundamental link between the geometry of chiral structures and the phase topology of electronic orders.

</details>


### [151] [What Makes Three-Dimensional Quantum Spin Liquids Possible?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03352)
*Yasir Iqbal,Ronny Thomale*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper argues that three-dimensional quantum spin liquids can be stable through locally constrained manifolds, emergent gauge theories, and topological protection, challenging the low-dimensional intuition that they are fragile exceptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to challenge the prevailing low-dimensional intuition for quantum spin liquids and provide a theoretical framework for understanding their stability in three spatial dimensions, addressing why 3D quantum spin liquids need not be regarded as fragile exceptions.

Method: The paper organizes discussion around three key mechanisms: constraint-driven Coulomb phases, weak harmonic order-by-disorder selection, and spin-orbit/multipolar interactions that generate required microscopic dynamics. It argues that coherent tunneling processes connect constrained manifolds and gauge defect topology permits deconfinement.

Result: In compact gauge theories, three dimensions can reverse the usual dimensional intuition—regimes that are unstable or fine-tuned in lower dimensions may become stable over finite regions of parameter space in 3D.

Conclusion: 3D quantum spin liquids can arise and be stable when local constraints suppress premature order selection, and the paper closes with experimental diagnostics, candidate materials, and open theoretical questions.

Abstract: Quantum spin liquids are often introduced through a low-dimensional intuition: weak coordination and strong zero-point motion frustrate conventional magnetic order. This view has shaped much of the field, but it misses another route to quantum disorder. In many frustrated magnets, the more natural starting point is not a fluctuating version of an ordered state, but a locally constrained manifold of low-energy configurations. Within such a manifold, quantum dynamics can generate an emergent gauge theory, with fractionalized excitations and collective modes absent in ordinary magnets. Here we ask why such phases can be stable in three spatial dimensions. We argue that three-dimensional quantum spin liquids need not be regarded as fragile exceptions to the tendency of 3D magnets to order. They can arise when local constraints suppress premature order selection, coherent tunneling processes connect the constrained manifold, and the topology of gauge defects permits a deconfined regime. For compact gauge theories, three dimensions can even reverse the usual dimensional intuition: regimes that are unstable or fine-tuned in lower dimensions may become stable over finite regions of parameter space. We organize the discussion around constraint-driven Coulomb phases, weak harmonic order-by-disorder selection, and the role of spin-orbit or multipolar interactions in generating the required microscopic dynamics. We close with experimental diagnostics, candidate materials, and open theoretical questions for three-dimensional quantum spin liquids

</details>


### [152] [Pressure-Driven Structural Transitions without a Displacive Charge-Density Wave in La$_2$SmNi$_2$O$_7$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03363)
*J. Huang,Sitaram Ramakrishnan,P. Rodière,P. Toulemonde,Z. Rahmany,V. Balédent,B. Vignolle,Sourav Marik,P. Fertey,P. Foury-Leylekian*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper investigates the structural properties of bilayer nickelate La₂SmNi₂O₇ under pressure and temperature, revealing a monoclinic superstructure at ambient conditions, no CDW ordering at low temperature, and pressure-induced structural transitions (monoclinic→orthorhombic at 15 GPa→tetragonal at 21 GPa) with refinements within the superconducting dome.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the structural properties of bilayer nickelate La₂SmNi₂O₇ as a function of pressure and temperature, particularly in relation to the emergence of superconductivity.

Method: X-ray diffraction experiments were conducted at varying pressure (up to 21 GPa) and temperature conditions to determine crystal structures and detect possible charge density wave ordering.

Result: At ambient conditions, the material exhibits a monoclinic superstructure distinct from previously reported ones. No CDW satellite reflections were observed at low temperature. Pressure induces sequential structural transitions: monoclinic→orthorhombic at ~15 GPa, then orthorhombic→tetragonal at ~21 GPa. High-quality XRD data within the superconducting dome enabled structural refinements.

Conclusion: The refined structural data within the superconducting pressure regime provide a foundation for theoretical models explaining superconductivity in this bilayer nickelate system.

Abstract: We investigated the structural properties of bilayer nickelate La$_2$SmNi$_2$O$_7$ as a function of pressure and temperature. At ambient conditions, we show that the material crystallizes as a monoclinic superstructure distinct from the one previously reported and close to the pseudo-orthorhombic structure of pristine La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$. No signatures of satellite reflections associated with charge density wave (CDW) ordering are detected at low temperature. Upon compression, a sequence of pressure-induced structural transitions from monoclinic to orthorhombic 15 GPa and then tetragonal 21 GPa symmetry is observed. Within the superconducting dome, the quality of the X-ray diffraction data enables structural refinements enabling theoretical models to understand the emergence of superconductivity.

</details>


### [153] [Quantum Destabilization of Skyrmions in Centrosymmetric Frustrated Magnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03378)
*Amit Kumar,Kalpataru Pradhan*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper investigates skyrmion stability in a quantum Heisenberg model on a square lattice using neural network quantum states, finding that skyrmions destabilize in the extreme quantum limit (s=1/2) due to quantum longitudinal fluctuations, requiring at least s=3/2 for stable atomic-scale topological textures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the role of the spin quantum number s on skyrmion stability in centrosymmetric quantum Heisenberg models, particularly how quantum fluctuations affect topological textures when transitioning from semiclassical to extreme quantum regimes.

Method: Neural network quantum states (NNQS) method applied to a J1-J2-J3 centrosymmetric quantum Heisenberg model on a square lattice.

Result: Skyrmion stability Q degrades severely when transitioning to the extreme quantum limit (s=1/2), exhibiting power-law decay as a function of reciprocal spin moment 1/s, with s=1/2 deviating drastically from this scaling. A quantitative correspondence is found between skyrmion stability, entanglement (second Rényi entropy), and local spin magnitude: Q vanishes linearly as entanglement increases and local spin magnitude is suppressed.

Conclusion: Classically robust skyrmion phases in frustrated lattices are fundamentally restricted to high-spin materials, requiring at least s=3/2 for realizing stable atomic-scale topological textures.

Abstract: We investigate the role of the spin quantum number $s$ on the stability of skyrmions in a $J_1-J_2-J_3$ centrosymmetric quantum Heisenberg model on a square lattice using the neural network quantum states method. Our results reveal that the skyrmion stability $Q$ is severely degraded when transitioning from the semiclassical regime to the extreme quantum limit ($s=1/2$), where it ultimately vanishes. We demonstrate that this destabilization is driven by quantum longitudinal fluctuations, with $Q$ exhibiting a power-law decay as a function of the reciprocal spin moment $1/s$. Notably, the extreme quantum limit ($s=1/2$) deviates drastically from this scaling behavior, exhibiting distinct physics compared to larger spin moments. Furthermore, we reveal the microscopic origin of this decay by establishing a quantitative correspondence between skyrmion stability, entanglement, and local spin magnitude: as the local second Rényi entropy (an indicator of entanglement) increases and the local spin magnitude is suppressed, the skyrmion stability vanishes linearly. This regime marks a quantum state where the skyrmion number $C$ remains as remanent geometric feature of the spin orientations, yet the skyrmion stability $Q$ vanishes due to the longitudinal suppression of the local spin magnitude. Our findings suggest that classically robust skyrmion phases in frustrated lattices are fundamentally restricted to high-spin materials, indicating that a spin moment must of at least $s = 3/2$ is required for the realization of stable, atomic-scale topological textures.

</details>


### [154] [Fixed-point tensor network for compactified boson conformal field theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03534)
*Gong Cheng,Dong-Yu Bao,Zheng-Cheng Gu*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper constructs fixed-point tensor networks for the 2D compactified boson theory (an irrational CFT) using boundary data, reproducing the closed-string spectrum accurately, generating stable RG flows, and identifying a marginal deformation that moves continuously along the c=1 moduli space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend fixed-point tensor network methods to irrational CFTs (specifically the compactified boson), enabling a discrete spacetime representation for studying holographic duality, generalized symmetries, and quantum gravity.

Method: Fixed-point tensors are constructed using boundary (open-string) data with conformal boundary conditions for the 2D compactified boson at a generic radius. The tensor complex renormalization algorithm is employed to analyze RG flows. An exactly marginal deformation is identified at the single-tensor level.

Result: The constructed tensors accurately reproduce the closed-string spectrum, yield stable RG flows under tensor renormalization, and allow continuous movement along the c=1 moduli space via a controllable marginal deformation.

Conclusion: This work establishes a concrete lattice-level framework for describing a broad class of 2D irrational CFTs using fixed-point tensor networks.

Abstract: Fixed-point (FP) tensor networks provide a discrete spacetime representation of conformal field theories (CFTs), offering a new route toward understanding holographic duality, generalized symmetries, and even quantum gravity. In this work, we construct FP tensors for the 2D compactified boson theory at a generic compactification radius, an archetypal irrational CFT, using boundary (open-string) data with conformal boundary conditions. We show that the resulting tensors reproduce the closed-string spectrum with high accuracy and generate stable renormalization-group (RG) flows under the tensor complex renormalization algorithm. Moreover, we identify a controllable exactly marginal deformation at the level of a single tensor, enabling flows that move continuously along the $c=1$ moduli space. This framework establishes a concrete lattice-level route toward describing a broad class of 2D irrational CFTs.

</details>


### [155] [Beyond-adiabatic flat Chern bands from a double-helix skyrmion crystal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03707)
*Bin Xi,Ken Chen,Qiang Luo,Jie Lu,Jia-Wei Mei,Hong-Gang Luo,Jize Zhao*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: A double-helix skyrmion crystal (DHSKX) achieves flat Chern bands with preserved Berry curvature via "phase clustering," enabling fractional Chern insulator physics at ν=1/3 and a higher-Chern flat band (C=-2), programmable in topolectric, acoustic, and photonic platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the central challenge in flat-band engineering of suppressing kinetic energy without sacrificing Berry curvature, which ordinary skyrmion crystals fail to achieve despite similar symmetry.

Method: The authors introduce a double-helix skyrmion crystal (DHSKX) as the classical ground state of a frustrated honeycomb spin model under double exchange. The mechanism—"phase clustering"—uses π-locked helicities and magnetic C₃ symmetry to create three phase-locked clusters that produce distributed destructive interference, eliminating net transport while preserving Berry curvature.

Result: DHSKX yields isolated flat |C|=1 Chern bands over broad coupling windows; one band surpasses the adiabatic reference in quantum geometry at intermediate coupling. In this beyond-adiabatic window, exact diagonalization shows finite-size evidence consistent with ν=1/3 Laughlin-type fractional Chern insulator physics. A higher-Chern (C=-2) flat band also emerges.

Conclusion: The DHSKX architecture, built purely from site-resolved complex hoppings, is directly programmable in topolectric, acoustic, and photonic platforms, offering a practical route to flat-band topology without compromising Berry curvature.

Abstract: A central challenge in flat-band engineering is suppressing kinetic energy without sacrificing Berry curvature. We show that a double-helix skyrmion crystal (DHSKX)--two sublattice-resolved skyrmion textures locked at opposite helicities, obtained here as the classical ground state of a frustrated honeycomb spin model--provides such a route under double exchange. The key mechanism is a single real-space organization, phase clustering: the $π$-locked helicities expel the wave function's phase winding from the skyrmion cores, and the magnetic $C_3$ symmetry pins it into three phase-locked clusters whose distributed destructive interference cancels net transport while preserving the Berry curvature. Ordinary skyrmion crystals, even with the same symmetry, do not develop this organization. Phase clustering yields isolated flat $|C| = 1$ Chern bands over broad coupling windows, one of which surpasses the adiabatic reference in quantum geometry at intermediate coupling. In this beyond-adiabatic window, band-projected exact diagonalization gives finite-size evidence consistent with $ν= 1/3$ Laughlin-type fractional-Chern-insulator physics; the same texture also hosts a higher-Chern ($C = -2$) flat band. Built from site-resolved complex hoppings alone, the DHSKX architecture is directly programmable in topolectric, acoustic, and photonic platforms.

</details>


### [156] [Pauli Spectrum and Stabilizer Rényi Entropy in Gapless Symmetry-Protected Topological Phases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03762)
*Ying-Lin Li,Po-Yao Chang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper studies stabilizer Rényi entropy and Pauli spectrum in symmetry protected topological (SPT) phases, including gapped and gapless variants. While stabilizer Rényi entropy shows an extremum near phase transitions but cannot distinguish SPT phases, the Pauli spectrum reveals a characteristic crossing structure reflecting exchange of string order parameters. The crossing arises from local-unitary duality for gapped/non-intrinsically gapless SPTs, and from non-invertible duality for intrinsically gapless SPTs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether quantum magic (non-stabilizerness) and the Pauli spectrum can provide complementary diagnostics for characterizing topological phases beyond conventional entanglement measures, particularly for distinguishing SPT phases and detecting phase transitions.

Method: Numerical study of stabilizer Rényi entropy and Pauli spectrum across gapped SPT, non-intrinsically gapless SPT, and intrinsically gapless SPT phases under symmetry-preserving perturbations.

Result: Stabilizer Rényi entropy shows an extremum near the phase transition but cannot distinguish different SPT phases. The Pauli spectrum exhibits a crossing structure at the transition point, reflecting exchange of dominant Pauli-string correlations linked to non-local string order parameters. The crossing is explained by local-unitary duality for gapped and non-intrinsically gapless SPTs, and by non-invertible duality for intrinsically gapless SPTs.

Conclusion: Quantum magic offers a complementary perspective for characterizing both gapped and gapless SPT phases. The Pauli spectrum contains finer information about the exchange of string order sectors, while stabilizer Rényi entropy provides only a coarse diagnostic of phase transitions.
