Abstract: Quantum entanglement is widely used as a diagnostic of topological phases of matter. Beyond entanglement, non-stabilizerness captures a distinct aspect of quantum many-body states by quantifying their distance from the manifold of stabilizer states. In this work, we study the stabilizer Rényi entropy in symmetry protected topological (SPT) phases, including both gapped SPT, non-intrinsically gapless SPT, and intrinsically gapless SPT phases. Under symmetry preserving perturbations, we find numerically that the stabilizer Rényi entropy exhibits an extremum near the phase transition. However, the stabilizer Rényi entropy alone cannot distinguish different SPT phases. In contrast, the Pauli spectrum reveals a characteristic crossing structure at the transition point. This crossing reflects the exchange of dominant Pauli-string correlations associated with the non-local string order parameters of the two topological distinct phases. For gapped SPT and non-intrinsically gapless SPT phases, the crossing structure can be understood from a local-unitary duality that maps the Pauli spectrum between the two phases. For intrinsically gapless SPT phases, such a local-unitary mapping is absent. Instead, we find that the Pauli spectrum mapping is generated by a non-invertible duality transformation. These results show that although the stabilizer Rényi entropy provides only a coarse diagnostic of phase transitions, the Pauli spectrum contains finer information about the exchange of string order sectors. Our findings demonstrate that quantum magic offers a complementary perspective for characterizing both gapped and gapless SPT phases.

</details>


### [157] [Cuprate d-wave superconductivity based on Non perturbative many body theory for spin fluctuation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04168)
*Jianwei Gong,Junnian Xiong,Ruitao Xiao,Hui Li,Ziyu Li,Huaqing Huang,Dingping Li*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper applies the many-body spin GW method and covariance method to the Hubbard model for superconductors, achieving quantitative agreement with experimental data for Néel temperature and superconducting Tc, demonstrating Fermi surface evolution across doping regimes, and offering a sign-problem-free, scalable approach for studying strongly correlated systems like cuprates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a nonperturbative, sign-problem-free computational method capable of accurately simulating strongly correlated systems (e.g., cuprate superconductors) in regimes previously inaccessible due to the fermion sign problem or limited lattice sizes.

Method: The many-body spin GW method (for single-particle Green's function) combined with the covariance method (for two-body correlation functions) applied to the Hubbard model. Benchmarked at strong coupling and low temperature, free from the fermion sign problem, and scalable to 64×64 lattices.

Result: Quantitative agreement with experimental data for Néel temperature and superconducting Tc; clear Fermi surface evolution showing transitions from pseudogap to strange metal to Fermi liquid phase with doping; demonstrated feasibility for thermodynamic limit via large lattice simulations.

Conclusion: The spin GW + covariance method offers a new, scalable, and accurate route for investigating strongly correlated systems, particularly cuprate superconductors, without suffering from the fermion sign problem.

Abstract: We employ the nonperturbative many-body spin GW method to compute the single-particle Green's function, and the covariance method to evaluate the correlation functions of two body, for the Hubbard model for superconductors. The key transition temperatures, namely the Neel temperature and the superconducting critical temperature Tc are found to be in quantitative agreement with experimental data. The evolution of the Fermi surface in different doping regimes is also presented, which clearly shows transitions from pseudogap to strange metal and to Fermi liquid phase. The method has been benchmarked at strong coupling and low temperature and is free from the fermion sign problem. Simulations on lattices up to 64 times 64 are also feasible, making access to the thermodynamic limit possible. This approach may offer a new route toward investigating strongly correlated systems, especially cuprate superconductors.

</details>


### [158] [Orbital-Selective Mott and Antiferromagnetic Phases in Diagonally Compressed Kagome Lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04621)
*Jiewei Ding,Ho-Kin Tang,Wing Chi Yu*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Using determinant quantum Monte Carlo, the authors study the half-filled Hubbard model on a diagonally compressed kagome lattice with exponential long-range hopping. They find a selective Mott transition driven by sublattice differentiation: at large angles, the A sublattice drives metallicity suppression on B/C; at small angles, B-C antiferromagnetic correlations suppress A's metallicity. The resulting phase diagram includes paramagnetic-metal, paramagnetic-Mott, antiferromagnetic-metal, and antiferromagnetic-Mott states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the interplay between lattice geometry (compression), magnetic frustration, and strong electronic correlations in frustrated two-dimensional systems, specifically how geometric deformation of the kagome lattice induces sublattice-selective electronic behavior.

Method: Determinant quantum Monte Carlo simulations of the half-filled Hubbard model on a diagonally compressed kagome lattice, with exponential decay long-range hopping t(r)=t0exp(-r/r0). The lattice compression is parameterized by angle θ, and the on-site interaction U is varied. Double occupancy, charge compressibility, and spin-spin correlation functions are measured system-wide and per sublattice.

Result: A clear sublattice differentiation emerges under compression. For θ≳52°, the A sublattice acquires long-range hoppings and suppresses metallicity on B/C, driving a selective Mott transition. For θ≲52°, B-C chains develop long-range antiferromagnetic correlations that suppress A's metallic behavior, also driving a selective Mott transition. The critical U^c_A drops sharply near the onset of B-C antiferromagnetism, while U^c_{B/C} rises.

Conclusion: Competing sublattice orders yield an orbital-selective Mott phase and a rich U-θ phase diagram with paramagnetic-metal, paramagnetic-Mott, antiferromagnetic-metal, and antiferromagnetic-Mott states, highlighting the complex interplay of geometry, frustration, and strong correlations in 2D frustrated systems.

Abstract: We perform determinant quantum Monte Carlo simulations of the half-filled Hubbard model on a diagonally compressed kagome lattice, introducing exponential decay long-range hopping $t(r) = t_0 \exp\bigl(-r / r_0\bigr)$ to account for the evolving bond length. By varying the lattice angle $θ$ and the on-site interaction $U$, double occupancy, charge compressibility, and spin-spin correlation functions of the whole system and each sub-lattice are measured. We find that geometric compression induces a clear sublattice differentiation: for $θ\gtrsim52^\circ$, the A sublattice establishes long-range hoppings, which in turn suppresses the metallic behavior of the $B/C$ sublattice and drives a selective Mott transition; for $θ\lesssim52^\circ$, the $B$-$C$ chains develop long-range antiferromagnetic correlations within the finite-size simulations, which in turn suppresses the metallic behavior of the $A$ sublattice and drives a selective Mott transition. The critical interaction $U^c_A$ for the $A$ sites decreases sharply near the onset of $B$-$C$ antiferromagnetic correlations, while $U^c_{B/C}$ increases. These competing orders give rise to an orbital-selective Mott phase and a rich $U$-$θ$ phase diagram featuring paramagnetic-metal, paramagnetic-Mott, antiferromagnetic-metal, and antiferromagnetic-Mott states. Our results highlight the complex interplay between lattice geometry, magnetic frustration, and strong correlations in frustrated two-dimensional systems.

</details>


### [159] [Supercritical fluid of quantum electrons in three-dimensional superconducting fullerides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04693)
*Xinying Li,Yuki Matsuda,Chongli Yang,Huaxue Zhou,Takuma Ogasawara,Qin Wang,Liguo Zhang,Hidekazu Shimotani,Hailiang Xia,Khuong K. Huynh,Panagiotis Kotetes,Satoshi Heguri,Katsumi Tanigaki*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Using dual-probe measurements (conductivity and magnetic susceptibility) on CsxRb3-xC60, this work identifies the first thermodynamically equilibrated supercritical fluid (SCF) phase at the Mott metal-insulator transition, revealing two distinct phase transition lines converging at a single Mott endpoint, a coexisting SCF with maximum mixing entropy, and a critical exponent consistent with quantum critical predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior studies of the Mott SCF in Cr-doped V2O3 and organic Mott systems yielded discrepant critical exponents, and scaling analysis was limited to a single experimental observable, leaving the roles of phase coexistence, inhomogeneity, and percolation unaddressed.

Method: Two independent probes (electrical conductivity and magnetic susceptibility) were used; a hypothesis-free two-particle analysis of magnetic susceptibilities yielded the coexisting SCF via maximum two-phase mixing entropy; conductivity scaling was used to extract the critical exponent.

Result: Two distinct metal-insulator transition lines converge at a single Mott endpoint; the SCF phase exhibits maximum two-phase mixing entropy consistent with a thermodynamically equilibrated Widom line; the critical exponent from conductivity scaling falls in the quantum critical prediction regime.

Conclusion: The dual-probe approach provides a unified microscopic picture of the Mott SCF with a characteristic length scale below current diffraction resolution, and offers a new interpretation of the origin of the superconducting Tc-dome.

Abstract: The supercritical fluid (SCF) of quantum electrons at the Mott metal-insulator transition without symmetry breaking is one of the most elusive phenomena in strongly correlated electron physics. Prior studies of Cr-doped V2O3 and organic Mott systems reported discrepant critical exponents. A key limitation is that the scaling analysis relies on a single experimental observable, leaving the roles of phase coexistence, inhomogeneity, and percolation unaddressed. Here we report the first experimental identification of a thermodynamically equilibrated SCF phase and its associated Mott endpoint in the three-dimensional superconducting fullerides CsxRb3-xC60, using two independent probes of electrical conductivity and magnetic susceptibility, which reveal two distinct metal-insulator transition lines converging at a single Mott endpoint. A hypothesis-free two-particle analysis of magnetic susceptibilities yields a metal-insulator coexisting SCF by exhibiting the maximum two-phase mixing entropy, in agreement with a picture of a thermodynamically equilibrated Widom line. Simultaneously, conductivity scaling yields a critical exponent in the regime of quantum critical predictions. Our new dual-probe approach provides a unified microscopic picture of the Mott SCF with a characteristic length scale below current diffraction resolution, in addition to a new interpretation on the origin of superconducting Tc-dome.

</details>


### [160] [Nonlocal Kondo-exchange-driven intrinsic anomalous Hall effect in localized-$4f$ antiferromagnetic metals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04805)
*Akimitsu Kirikoshi,Junya Otsuki*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Study of anomalous Hall effect in f-electron antiferromagnetic metals, focusing on Ce₂CuGe₆, where nonlocal Kondo exchange (not Zeeman field) drives intrinsic anomalous Hall conductivity and spin splitting via spin-dependent intersite hopping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the anomalous Hall effect in f-electron antiferromagnetic metals where localized magnetic moments and conduction electrons reside in different orbitals, as most prior work focused on d-electron systems where both charge transport and magnetism involve the same itinerant electrons.

Method: Developed a theoretical framework describing how localized antiferromagnetic order impacts itinerant electrons, applied to Ce₂CuGe₆, analyzing the role of nonlocal Kondo exchange coupling versus local Zeeman-type effective fields.

Result: The intrinsic anomalous Hall conductivity and spin splitting in Ce₂CuGe₆ originate from spin-dependent intersite hopping induced by nonlocal Kondo exchange coupling, not from a Zeeman-type effective field acting locally on conduction electrons.

Conclusion: Nonlocal Kondo exchange is the key mechanism for the anomalous Hall effect in this f-electron antiferromagnet, contrasting with d-electron systems where local Zeeman fields typically dominate.

Abstract: The anomalous Hall effect in antiferromagnetic metals has attracted considerable attention. Most known realizations involve itinerant $d$ electrons that simultaneously mediate charge transport and magnetic order. Here, we focus on $f$-electron materials, where localized magnetic moments and conduction electrons are hosted in different orbitals. We develop a theoretical framework to describe the impact of localized antiferromagnetic order on itinerant electrons. Applying this approach to the recently discovered $4f$ antiferromagnetic metal $\mathrm{Ce}_{2}\mathrm{Cu}\mathrm{Ge}_{6}$, we identify the origin of both the intrinsic anomalous Hall conductivity and the spin splitting of the energy bands as spin-dependent intersite hopping induced by nonlocal Kondo exchange coupling, rather than a Zeeman-type effective field acting locally on the conduction electrons.

</details>


### [161] [Chirped Floquet linear drives activate forbidden charge-to-spin conversions in Rashba two-dimensional electron gases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04946)
*Mohsen Yarmohammadi*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: A chirped Floquet linear drive can activate forbidden out-of-plane and longitudinal Edelstein charge-to-spin conversion in Rashba 2DEGs without needing static magnetic fields or interface engineering, offering a tunable, experimentally accessible route for spintronics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional charge-to-spin conversion via the Edelstein effect in Rashba 2DEGs is limited to transverse plane pathways, and accessing longitudinal/out-of-plane channels typically requires static magnetic fields (causing stray fields) or interface engineering (lacking tunability). A simple Floquet linear drive also cannot unlock these forbidden pathways.

Method: Proposes using a chirped Floquet linear drive, where the chirp induces in-plane Floquet-Zeeman fields and an odd-parity momentum drift, simultaneously breaking rotational and time-reversal symmetries to activate forbidden Edelstein conversion channels.

Result: The chirped linear drive unlocks the forbidden Edelstein charge-to-spin conversions in Rashba 2DEGs, enabling out-of-plane and longitudinal spin generation without magnetic fields or interface engineering.

Conclusion: This tunable chirped linear drive approach is experimentally accessible via programmable spatial light modulators or optical delay lines, offering a broadly applicable route to spin-orbit torque switching and high-efficiency spintronics.

Abstract: In Rashba two-dimensional electron gases (2DEGs), charge-to-spin conversion via the Edelstein effect is conventionally limited to the transverse plane. Accessing longitudinal or out-of-plane pathways typically requires static magnetic fields or interface engineering, which cause stray fields and lack tunability. While dynamic circular or elliptical Floquet drives can also unlock these forbidden pathways, a simple Floquet linear drive cannot. Here, we propose an alternative approach: a \textit{chirped} Floquet linear drive. The chirp induces in-plane Floquet-Zeeman fields and an odd-parity momentum drift, which simultaneously break rotational and time-reversal symmetries. This mechanism activates the forbidden Edelstein charge-to-spin conversions in Rashba 2DEGs. Experimentally accessible via programmable spatial light modulators or optical delay lines, this tunable chirped linear drive offers a broadly applicable route to spin-orbit torque switching and high-efficiency spintronics.

</details>


### [162] [Optical switching of antiferromagnetic domains by nonreciprocal heat current](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04975)
*Takeshi Hayashida,Shunsuke Izumikawa,Kenta Kimura,Carl S. Davies,Andrei Kirilyuk*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that nonreciprocity—where light propagation direction matters—can deterministically switch antiferromagnetic domains in LiFePO4 using mid-infrared light, with broadband efficacy arising from optically generated heat currents rather than narrow resonance effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a general principle for deterministically controlling symmetry-broken phases (antiferromagnetic domains) using light, overcoming the spectral and material constraints of resonance-based optical switching methods.

Method: Using mid-infrared light to illuminate the magnetoelectric antiferromagnet LiFePO4 from opposite sides, exploiting the material's intrinsic nonreciprocity (broken space-inversion and time-reversal symmetries) to selectively stabilize opposite domain states via optically generated heat currents.

Result: Deterministic switching of antiferromagnetic domains was achieved, with illumination from opposite sides selectively stabilizing opposite domain states. The switching persists over a broad wavelength range, not confined to narrow transition-specific spectral regions.

Conclusion: Nonreciprocity is established as a general principle for deterministically controlling symmetry-broken phases with light, enabled by broadband heat-current-mediated switching rather than narrow resonance-based mechanisms.

Abstract: What distinguishes front from back? In physics, such directionality emerges only when an underlying symmetry is broken. Antiferromagnets that inherently break both space-inversion and time-reversal symmetries provide a striking example, exhibiting nonreciprocal optical responses that depend on the direction of light propagation. Beyond distinguishing antiferromagnetic domains, we show that this nonreciprocity can deterministically create them. Using mid-infrared light, we demonstrate deterministic switching of antiferromagnetic domains in the magnetoelectric antiferromagnet LiFePO4, where illumination from opposite sides selectively stabilizes opposite domain states. Remarkably, the switching persists over a broad wavelength range rather than being confined to a narrow transition-specific spectral region, overcoming the spectral and material constraints of resonance-based optical switching schemes. The broadband switching originates from the material's intrinsic nonreciprocity through optically generated heat currents. Our results establish nonreciprocity as a general principle for deterministically controlling symmetry-broken phases with light.

</details>


### [163] [Near itinerancy and slow singlet formation in the triangular lattice NaRuO2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05287)
*Charles C. Tam,Alon Hendler Avidor,Pritam Bhattacharyya,Yongseong Choi,Daniel Haskel,Sven Luther,Hlynur Gretarrson,Liviu Hozoi,Stephen D. Wilson*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: NaRuO₂ forms a delafossite-like triangular lattice showing no magnetic order down to 100 mK, with RIXS, XAS, and high-field magnetization revealing a nearly itinerant system with strong magnetic/charge fluctuations that destabilize long-range order, consistent with a random singlet model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize the physical regime underlying the quantum disordered magnetic ground state in NaRuO₂, specifically whether spin-orbit coupling drives a spin-orbit assisted Mott insulating state or another mechanism leads to the suppression of magnetic order.

Method: Resonant inelastic X-ray scattering (RIXS) and X-ray absorption spectroscopy (XAS) at the Ru-L₂,₃-edge to probe local electronic structure, coupled with pulsed high-field magnetization measurements analyzed via a random singlet model.

Result: No spin-orbit exciton (characteristic of a spin-orbit assisted Mott insulator) was observed; instead, damped intraorbital excitations indicative of metallic behavior were found. High-field magnetization data are consistent with a random singlet model.

Conclusion: NaRuO₂ is a nearly itinerant system where strong magnetic and charge fluctuations, rather than a spin-orbit assisted Mott insulating state, destabilize long-range magnetic order, leading to a quantum disordered ground state.

Abstract: NaRuO$_2$ forms a delafossite-like structure that contains triangular sublattices of edge-sharing RuO$_6$ octahedra. It shows no evidence of magnetic order down to 100 mK and persistent spin fluctuations, suggestive of a quantum disordered magnetic ground state. In order to characterize the physical regime from which this disordered state arises, we use resonant inelastic X-ray scattering (RIXS) and X-ray absorption spectroscopy (XAS) at the Ru-$L_{2,3}$-edge, along with pulsed high-field magnetization to characterize both the local electronic structure and the magnetic interactions. Despite significant spin-orbit coupling inferred from XAS measurements, a spin-orbit exciton, characteristic of a spin-orbit assisted Mott insulator, was not observed with RIXS due to the presence of damped intraorbital excitations, which are characteristic of a metal. Corroborated by models of the high-field magnetization to a random singlet model, we propose a picture of a nearly itinerant system with strong magnetic and charge fluctuations that destabilize long-range magnetic order.

</details>
